Monoidal categories graded by crossed modules and 3-dimensional HQFTs
Kürşat Sözer, Alexis Virelizier

Abstract
. Given a crossed module χ \chi , we introduce χ \chi -graded monoidal categories and χ \chi -fusion categories. We use spherical χ \chi -fusion categories to construct (via the state sum method) 3-dimensional Homotopy Quantum Field Theories with target the classifying space B ​ χ B\chi of the crossed module χ \chi (which is a homotopy 2-type).



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
18M05, 57K31, 57K16

1.
Introduction

Homotopy Quantum Field Theory (HQFT) is a branch of quantum topology concerned with maps from manifolds
to a fixed target space
X
. The aim is to define and to study
homotopy invariants of such maps using methods of quantum topology.
The formal notion of a
d
-dimensional HQFT with target
X
was introduced in

[
Tu
]
. Roughly, it is a TQFT for closed oriented
(d-1)
-dimensional
manifolds and compact oriented
d
-dimensional cobordisms endowed with maps
to
X
. Such an HQFT yields numerical homotopy invariants of maps from
closed oriented
d
-dimensional manifolds to
X
. Note that 1-dimensional HQFTs with target
X
correspond bijectively to finite-dimensional representations of the fundamental group
of
X
or, equivalently, to finite-dimensional flat vector bundles over
X
. This allows one to view HQFTs as high-dimensional generalizations of flat vector bundles. The case of 2-dimensional HQFTs with target
X
is quite well understood: they are classified by certain classes of Frobenius algebras which are in particular graded by the fundamental group of
X
(see
[
BT
,
Tu
,
PT
,
ST
]
, and
[
So
]
for the extended setting).

The present paper focuses on 3-dimensional HQFTs. The case where the target space
X
is a point (i.e., a connected homotopy 0-type) corresponds to 3-dimensional TQFTs and has been intensively studied. In particular, there are two fundamental constructions of 3-dimensional TQFTs: the Turaev-Viro TQFT which consists of state sums on triangulations (or more generally on skeletons) of 3-manifolds and uses spherical fusion categories, and the Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT which is based on a surgery approach and uses modular categories (see for example the monographs
[
Tu
,
TVi4
]
and the references therein). The case where the target space
X
is a connected homotopy 1-type, that is, an Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(G,1)
where
G
is a group, has been studied by Turaev and the second author: both the state-sum and the surgery approaches can be extended using spherical and modular fusion
G
-graded categories (see
[
TVi1
,
TVi2
,
TVi3
]
). Here, a
G
-graded category is a monoidal category whose objects are equipped with a degree in
G
(which is multiplicative with respect to the monoidal product) and whose morphisms preserve the grading (i.e., there are no nonzero morphisms between objects with distinct degrees). In this paper, we extend the state sum approach to the case where the target space
X
is a connected homotopy 2-type.

Following MacLane and Whitehead
[
MLW
]
, we model pointed connected homotopy 2-types by crossed modules: if
X
is a homotopy 2-type, then
X
is homotopy equivalent to the classifying space of a crossed module. Recall that a crossed module is a group homomorphism
\chi\colon E\to H
, with
H
acting on the left on
E
, such that
\chi
is
H
-equivariant (where
H
acts on itself by conjugation) and satisfies the Peiffer identity. Such a crossed module has a classifying space
B\chi
such that

\pi_{1}(B\chi)=\mathrm{Coker}(\chi),\quad\pi_{2}(B\chi)=\mathrm{Ker}(\chi),\quad\pi_{k}(B\chi)=0\quad\text{for $k\geq 3$.}

We introduce the notion of a
\chi
-graded monoidal category in which not only objects have a degree but also morphisms. More precisely, each (homogeneous) object of such a category
\mathcal{C}
is equipped with a degree
|X|\in H
(which is multiplicative with respect to the monoidal product) and the Hom-sets of
\mathcal{C}
are
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules:

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)=\bigoplus_{e\in E}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y).

For homogeneous objects
X,Y
, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)=0
whenever
|Y|\neq\chi(e)|X|
. In particular, there may be nonzero morphisms between homogeneous objects with distinct degrees. The composition of morphisms is multiplicative in degree:

(\beta,\alpha)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(Y,Z)\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)\;\,\Rightarrow\;\,\beta\circ\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{fe}(X,Z).

The monoidal product of morphisms is compatible with the gradings as follows:

(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(Z,T)\;\,\Rightarrow\;\,\alpha\otimes\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e{\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{|X|}{\!f}}(X\otimes Z,Y\otimes T).

For example, the
\Bbbk
-linearization
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
of the 2-group
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
associated with
\chi
is a
\chi
-
graded monoidal category. Other instances of
\chi
-graded monoidal categories are given by the categories of representations of Hopf
\chi
-(co)algebras (see
[
SV
]
).
Note that if
G
is a group, then the group homomorphism
1\to G
is a crossed module and
(1\to G)
-graded monoidal categories correspond to
G
-graded monoidal categories. Any cohomology class in
H^{3}(B\chi,\Bbbk^{*})
can always be represented by a normalized 3-cocycle
\omega\colon H^{3}\times E^{3}\to\Bbbk^{*}
which allows to twist the composition, the monoidal product, and the associator of any
\chi
-graded monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
to produce another
\chi
-graded monoidal category
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
. Also, the push-forward
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
of a
\chi
-graded monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
along a crossed module morphism
\phi\colon\chi\to\chi^{\prime}
is a
\chi^{\prime}
-graded monoidal category.

Next, using the state sum approach, we derive a 3-dimensional HQFT
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
with target
B\chi
from any spherical
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
whose neutral component has invertible dimension (see Theorems
7.1
and
9.5
). To this end, we represent 3-manifolds by their skeletons and the maps to
B\chi
by certain
H
-labels on the faces and
E
-labels on the edges of the skeletons. In particular, this HQFT induces a scalar invariant
|M,g|_{\mathcal{C}}
of closed
\chi
-manifolds, that is, of pairs
(M,g)
where
M
is closed oriented 3-manifold and
g
is a homotopy class of maps
M\to B\chi
. We prove by examples that this invariant is nontrivial. It may even distinguish homotopy classes of phantom maps
M\to B\chi
(i.e., of maps inducing trivial homomorphisms on homotopy groups). For the spherical
\chi
-fusion category
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}^{\omega}
obtained by twisting the category
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
with a 3-cocycle
\omega
for
\chi
, we conjecture that

|M,g|_{\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}^{\omega}}=\langle g^{*}([\omega]),[M]\rangle,

where
g^{*}([\omega])\in H^{3}(M,\Bbbk^{*})
is the pullback of the cohomology class
[\omega]\in H^{3}(B\chi,\Bbbk^{*})
induced by the cocycle
\omega
,
[M]\in H_{3}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is the fundamental class of
M
, and
\langle\,,\,\rangle\colon H^{3}(M,\Bbbk^{*})\times H_{3}(M,\mathbb{Z})\to\Bbbk
is the Kronecker pairing. Moreover, the push-forward
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
of a
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
along a crossed module morphism
\phi\colon\chi\to\chi^{\prime}
(satisfying some finiteness conditions) is a
\chi^{\prime}
-fusion category, and we relate the invariant
|M,g^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}
of a closed
\chi^{\prime}
-manifold
(M,g^{\prime})
with the invariants
\{|M,g|_{\mathcal{C}}\}_{g}
and the fundamental groups
\{\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi),g)\}_{g}
, where
g
runs over homotopy classes of maps
M\to B\chi
such that
B\phi\circ g=g^{\prime}
(see Theorem
7.2
). Here
\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi)
is the mapping space of pointed maps and
B\phi\colon B\chi\to B\chi^{\prime}
is the map induced by
\phi
.
In particular, if
\phi
is an equivalence of crossed modules (so that
B\phi
is a homotopy equivalence), then
|M,g^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=|M,(B\phi)^{-1}\circ g^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{C}}
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we review crossed modules and their classifying spaces.
Section
3
is dedicated to categories whose Hom-sets are graded by a group.
In Section
4
, we introduce the notion of a monoidal category graded by a crossed module
\chi
and
discuss various classes of such categories including pivotal, spherical, and
\chi
-fusion ones. In Section
5
, we first introduce colored
\chi
-
cyclic sets and their multiplicity modules, and then we define an isotopy invariant of colored
\chi
-graphs which is used in our state sums (as a replacement of
6j
-symbols).
In Section
6
, we discuss skeletons of 3-manifolds and
presentations of maps to
B\chi
by labelings of skeletons. We use these presentations in Section
7
to derive from any spherical
\chi
-fusion category a numerical invariant of closed
\chi
-manifolds. In Section
8
, we recall the definition (adapted to our need) of a 3-dimensional HQFT with target
B\chi
. Finally, in Section
9
, we extend the state sum invariants of Section
7
to an HQFT with target
B\chi
.

We fix throughout the paper a nonzero commutative ring
\Bbbk
. For topological spaces
X
and
Y
, we denote by
[X,Y]
the set of homotopy classes of maps
X\to Y
.

2.
Crossed modules and their classifying spaces

We recall some standard notions of the theory of crossed modules.

2.1.
Crossed modules

A
crossed module
is a group homomorphism
\chi\colon E\to H
together with a left action of
H
on
E
(by group automorphisms) denoted

(x,e)\in H\times E\mapsto{\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}^{x}{\hskip-0.80002pte}\in E

such that
\chi
is equivariant with respect to the conjugation action of
H
on itself:

(1)

\chi({\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}^{x}{\hskip-0.80002pte})=x\chi(e)x^{-1}

and satisfies the Peiffer identity:

(2)

{\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{{\chi(e)}}{\!f}=efe^{-1}

for all
x\in H
and
e,f\in E
.

These axioms imply that the image
{\mathrm{Im}}(\chi)
is normal in
H
and that the kernel
\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
is central in
E
and is acted on trivially by
{\mathrm{Im}}(\chi)
. In particular,
\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
inherits an action of
H/{\mathrm{Im}}(\chi)=\mathrm{Coker}(\chi)
.

2.2.
Examples

1. Given any normal subgroup
E
of a group
H
, the inclusion
E\hookrightarrow H
is a crossed module with the conjugation action of
H
on
E
.

2. For any group
E
, the homomorphism
E\to\mathrm{Aut}(E)
sending any element of
E
to the corresponding inner automorphism is a crossed module.

3. For any group
H
and any left
H
-module
E
, the trivial map
E\to H
is a crossed module.

4. Any group epimorphism
E\to H
with kernel contained in the center of
E
is a crossed module with trivial action of
H
on
E
. In particular, if
A
is an abelian group, then the trivial map
A\to 1
is a crossed module.

5. To any map
\omega\colon S\to H
, where
S
is a set and
H
is a group, one associates the free crossed module
\chi\colon\mathrm{CM}(\omega)\to H
as follows. The group
\mathrm{CM}(\omega)
is generated by
S\times H
with the relations

(e,x)(f,y)=(f,x\omega(e)x^{-1}y)(e,x)

for all
e,f\in S
and
x,y\in H
. The action of
H
on
CM(\omega)
and the morphism
\chi
are given for all
e\in S
and
x,y\in H
by

{\vphantom{{(e,y)}}}{}^{x}{(e,y)}=(e,xy)\quad\text{and}\quad\partial(e,x)=x\omega(e)x^{-1}.

6. A key geometric example of a crossed module is due to Whitehead. He showed that if
(X,A,x)
is a pair of pointed topological spaces, then the homotopy boundary map
\partial\colon\pi_{2}(X,A,x)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(A,x)
,
together with the standard action of
\pi_{1}(A,x)
on
\pi_{2}(X,A,x)
, is a crossed module.

2.3.
Crossed module morphisms

A
morphism
from a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
to a crossed module
\chi^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to H^{\prime}
is a pair
\phi=(\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime},\varphi\colon H\to H^{\prime})

of group homomorphisms making the square

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}}

commutative and preserving the action in the sense that for all
x\in H
and
e\in E
,

\psi({\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}^{x}{\hskip-0.80002pte})={\vphantom{{\psi(e)}}}^{\varphi(x)}{\psi(e)}.

Crossed modules and crossed module morphisms form a category denoted
\mathrm{CRMod}
. Whitehead’s construction (see Example 6 of Section
2.2
) extends to a functor

\Pi_{2}\colon(\text{pair of pointed topological spaces})\to\mathrm{CRMod}.

2.4.
Classifying spaces of crossed modules

There is a classifying space functor
B
(see
[
BH
]
) which assigns to a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H

a connected, reduced
1
1

1

A CW-complex is
reduced
if it has a single 0-cell, serving then as basepoint.
CW-complex

B\chi
with the following properties:

(i)

The homotopy groups of the classifying space
B\chi
are given by

\pi_{1}(B\chi)=\mathrm{Coker}(\chi),\quad\pi_{2}(B\chi)=\mathrm{Ker}(\chi),\quad\pi_{k}(B\chi)=0\quad\text{for $k\geq 3$.}

(ii)

The classifying space
B\chi
has a canonical (reduced) subcomplex
BH
which is a classifying space of the group
H
, contains all 1-cells of
B\chi
, and satisfies

\Pi_{2}(B\chi,BH)=\chi.

In particular,
BH
is the classifying space
B(1\to H)
of the trivial crossed module
1\to H
.

(iii)

Let
X
be a reduced CW-complex. Let
X_{1}
be the 1-skeleton of
X
and consider the Whitehead crossed module
\partial\colon\pi_{2}(X,X_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(X_{1})
. Then there is a map

X\to B\partial

inducing isomorphisms on
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.

It follows that crossed modules model all pointed connected homotopy 2-types (as originally proved by MacLane and Whitehead
[
MLW
]
).

2.5.
2-groups and crossed modules

Recall that a 2-group is a monoidal small category in which every morphism is invertible and every object has a weak inverse.
A strict 2-group is a strict monoidal small category in which every morphism is invertible and every object has a strict inverse.

Strict 2-groups are identified with crossed modules as follows. Any crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
gives rise to a strict 2-group
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
defined as follows. The objects of
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
are the elements of
H
. For any
x,y\in H
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}}(x,y)=\{e\in E\,|\,y=\chi(e)x\}.

The composition of morphisms is given by the product of
E
:

\left(y\xrightarrow{f}z\right)\circ\left(x\xrightarrow{e}y\right)=\left(x\xrightarrow{fe}z\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{id}_{x}=1\in E.

The monoidal product of objects is given by the product of
H
:

x\otimes y=xy\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{1}=1\in H.

The monoidal product of morphisms is induced by the left action of
H
on
E
:

\left(x\xrightarrow{e}y\right)\otimes\left(z\xrightarrow{f}t\right)=\left(xz\xrightarrow{e{\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{x}{\!f}}yt\right).

Conversely, any strict 2-group
\mathcal{G}
gives rise to a crossed module
E\to H
, where
H
is the
set of objects of
\mathcal{G}
,
E
is the set of morphisms emanating from the unit object, and the map
E\to H
is the target map.

3.
Categories with Hom-sets graded by a group

In this section, we study Hom-graded categories which are linear categories whose Hom-sets are graded by a group. Throughout this section,
E
denotes a group.

3.1.
Linear categories

A category
\mathcal{C}
is
\Bbbk
-linear
if for all objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
, the set
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
carries a structure of a left
\Bbbk
-module so that the composition of morphisms is
\Bbbk
-bilinear.

An object
X
of a
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
is
simple
if
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(X)
is a free
\Bbbk
-module of rank 1 (and
so has the basis
\{\mathrm{id}_{X}\}
). It is clear that an object isomorphic to a simple object is itself simple.

A
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
is
semisimple
if

(a)

each object of
\mathcal{C}
is a finite direct sum of simple objects;

(b)

for any non-isomorphic simple objects
i,j
of
\mathcal{C}
, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(i,j)=0
.

Clearly, the Hom spaces in such a
\mathcal{C}
are free
\Bbbk
-modules of finite rank.

A set
I
of simple objects of a semisimple
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
is
representative
if every simple object of
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to a unique element of
I
. Each object of
\mathcal{C}
is then a direct sum of a finite family of elements of
I
. Also,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(i,j)=0
for any distinct elements
i,j\in I
.

A monoidal category is
\Bbbk
-linear
if it is
\Bbbk
-linear as a category and the monoidal product of morphisms
is
\Bbbk
-bilinear.

3.2.
Hom-graded categories

By an
E
-
Hom-graded category (over
\Bbbk
)
we mean a category enriched over the monoidal category of

E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules and
\Bbbk
-linear grading-preserving homomorphisms. In other words, an
E
-Hom-graded category (over
\Bbbk
)
is a
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
such that:

(a)

The Hom-sets in
\mathcal{C}
are
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules: for all objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)=\bigoplus_{e\in E}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y).

(b)

The composition in
\mathcal{C}
is grading-preserving:
for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
and
e,f\in E
, it sends
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(Y,Z)\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)
into
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{fe}(X,Z)
.

(c)

The identities have trivial degree: for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
,

\mathrm{id}_{X}\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(X,X),

where
1
denotes the unit element of
E
.

Note that for any object
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the monoid
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,X)
is an
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-algebra.

A morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in an
E
-Hom-graded category
\mathcal{C}
is
homogeneous
if

f\in\coprod_{e\in E}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y).

It is
homogeneous of degree
e\in E
if
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)
. Note that if
f
is nonzero, then such an
e\in E
is unique, is called the
degree
of
f
, and is denoted
e=|f|
. The objects of
\mathcal{C}
together with the homogenous morphisms of degree 1 form a
\Bbbk
-linear subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
called the
1-subcategory
of
\mathcal{C}
and denoted
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Given
e\in E
, by an
e
-isomorphism
we mean an isomorphism which is homogeneous of degree
e
. We say that an object
X
is
e
-isomorphic
to an object
Y
, and we write
X\cong_{e}Y
, if there is an
e
-isomorphism
X\to Y
. It is easy to see that for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
and
e,f\in E
,

(3)

X\cong_{1}X,\quad X\cong_{e}Y\Longrightarrow Y\cong_{e^{-1}}X,\quad X\cong_{e}Y\text{ and }Y\cong_{f}Z\Longrightarrow X\cong_{fe}Z.

Also, if
X\cong_{e}Y
, then for all
d\in E
, there are
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphisms

(4)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d}(Y,Z)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{de}(X,Z)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d}(Z,X)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{ed}(Z,Y)

which are induced by pre/post-composition with an
e
-isomorphism
X\to Y
.

3.3.
Example

A
\Bbbk
-module
M
is
E
-graded
if it has a direct sum decomposition

M=\bigoplus_{e\in E}M_{e}

by submodules indexed by
e\in E
. We say that such a
\Bbbk
-module
M
has
finite support
if
M_{e}=0
for all but a finite number of
e\in E
. For example, this is the case if
E
is finite or if
M
is free of finite rank.
A
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphism
\phi\colon M\to N
between
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules is
homogeneous of degree
d\in E
if
\phi(M_{e})\subset N_{de}
for all
e\in E
. Such homomorphisms form a submodule
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}^{d}(M,N)
of the
\Bbbk
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(M,N)
of
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms
M\to N
. For example, the identity
\mathrm{id}_{M}\colon M\to M
is homogenous of degree 1. Clearly, the composition of homogeneous homomorphisms is multiplicative with respect to the degree. This induces an
E
-Hom-graded category
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E}
whose objects are
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules and Hom-sets are

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E}}(M,N)=\bigoplus_{d\in E}\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}^{d}(M,N).

The 1-subcategory of
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E}
is the category of
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules and grading-preserving
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms.
Note that if
M
and
N
have finite support, then

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E}}(M,N)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(M,N)
.
Then
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules with finite support and
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms form an
E
-Hom-graded category (as a subcategory of
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E}
). In particular,
E
-graded free
\Bbbk
-modules of finite rank and
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms form an
E
-Hom-graded category.

3.4.
Direct sums in Hom-graded categories

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
E
-Hom-graded category. Given
e\in E
, an object
D
of
\mathcal{C}
is an
e
-direct sum
of a finite family
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
if there is a family
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
such that:

(a)

p_{\alpha}\colon D\to X_{\alpha}
is homogeneous of degree
e^{-1}
for all
\alpha\in A
;

(b)

q_{\alpha}\colon X_{\alpha}\to D
is homogeneous of degree
e
for all
\alpha\in A
;

(c)

\mathrm{id}_{D}=\sum_{\alpha\in A}q_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}
;

(d)

p_{\alpha}q_{\beta}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\,\mathrm{id}_{X_{\alpha}}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in A
, where
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
is the Kronecker symbol defined by
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}=1
if
\alpha=\beta
and
\delta_{\alpha,\beta}=0
otherwise.

Such an
e
-direct sum
D
, if it exists, is unique up to a 1-isomorphism and is denoted

D=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}^{e}X_{\alpha}.

It is easy to see that if a finite family
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
has an
e
-direct sum
and an
f
-direct sum with
e,f\in E
, then

\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}^{e}X_{\alpha}\cong_{fe^{-1}}\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}^{f}X_{\alpha}.

Also, for any finite families
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
and
(Y_{\beta})_{\beta\in B}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
and for any
d,e,f\in E
, there are
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphisms

(5)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{d}\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}^{e}X_{\alpha},\bigoplus_{\beta\in B}^{f}Y_{\beta}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in A\\
\beta\in B\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f^{-1}de}\bigl(X_{\alpha},Y_{\beta}\bigr).

By definition, a direct sum of an empty family of objects of a
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
is a
zero object
of
\mathcal{C}
, that is, an object
\mathbf{0}
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{0})=0
.

An
E
-Hom-graded category
\mathcal{C}
is
E
-additive
if any finite (possibly empty) family of objects of
\mathcal{C}
has an
e
-direct sum in
\mathcal{C}
for all
e\in E
.

3.5.
Semisimple Hom-graded categories

We say that an
E
-Hom-graded category
\mathcal{C}
is
E
-semisimple
if:

(a)

its 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is semisimple (see Section
3.1
);

(b)

for any
e\in E
, each object of
\mathcal{C}
is an
e
-direct sum of a finite family of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

For
E=1
, we recover the usual notion of a semisimple category.
Note that a simple object of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is nothing but an object of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(X)
is free of rank 1 (with basis
\mathrm{id}_{X}
). In particular, a simple object of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
may not be simple in
\mathcal{C}
. Consequently, an
E
-semisimple
E
-Hom-graded category is not necessarily semisimple (in the usual sense of Section
3.1
).

For example, the category of
E
-graded free
\Bbbk
-modules of finite rank and all
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms (see Section
3.3
) is an
E
-semisimple
E
-Hom-graded category.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
E
-semisimple
E
-Hom-graded category. Then:

(i)

For any simple object
i
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
e\in E
, there is a simple object
j
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
such that

i\cong_{e}j
.

(ii)

For any simple objects
i,j
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
e\in E
, the
\Bbbk
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(i,j)
is free of rank 1 if
i\cong_{e}j
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(i,j)=0
otherwise.

(iii)

Hom-sets in
\mathcal{C}
are free
\Bbbk
-modules of finite rank.

(iv)

For any objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
and
e,f\in E
, there is a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{fe}(X,Y)\simeq\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(i,Y)\otimes_{\Bbbk}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,i),

where
I
is any representative set of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Proof.

Let us prove Part (i). Pick a decomposition
(p_{\alpha}\colon i\to i_{\alpha},q_{\alpha}\colon i_{\alpha}\to i)_{\alpha\in A}
of
i
as an
e^{-1}
-direct sum of a finite family
(i_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of simple objects
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.
Formula (
5
) implies that

\bigoplus_{\alpha,\beta\in A}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i_{\alpha},i_{\beta})\simeq\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i)\quad\text{and so}\quad\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i_{\alpha})\subset\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i).

Since
i
and each
i_{\alpha}
are simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, taking the rank in the latter inclusion gives that the cardinal of
A
is less than or equal to 1. Since the set
A
is nonempty (otherwise
i
would be the zero object which is not simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
), it is a singleton
A=\{\alpha_{0}\}
. Set
j=i_{\alpha_{0}}
. Then
p=p_{\alpha_{0}}\colon i\to j
is a homogeneous morphism of degree
e
and
q=q_{\alpha_{0}}\colon j\to i
is a homogeneous morphism of degree
e^{-1}
such that
\mathrm{id}_{i}=qp
and
pq=\mathrm{id}_{j}
. Thus
p
is an
e
-isomorphism and so
i\cong_{e}j
.

Let us prove Part (ii). If
i\cong_{e}j
, then Formula (
4
) implies that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(i,j)\simeq\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i)
is free of rank 1 (since
i
is simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
). Assume that
i\not\cong_{e}j
. By Part (i), there is a simple object
k
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
such that
j\cong_{e^{-1}}k
. Then
i\not\cong_{1}k
(indeed, using (
3
) and since
k\cong_{e}j
, if

i\cong_{1}k
then
i\cong_{e}j
). Then the simple objects
i,j
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
are not isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and so
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i,k)=0
(since
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is semisimple). Hence, using (
4
), we conclude that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(i,j)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i,k)=0
.

Since each object of
\mathcal{C}
is a
1
-direct sum of a finite family of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, Part (iii) follows directly from (
5
) and Part (ii).

Let us prove Part (iv). Pick a decomposition
(p_{\alpha}\colon X\to i_{\alpha},q_{\alpha}\colon i_{\alpha}\to X)_{\alpha\in A}
of
X
as an
e^{-1}
-direct sum of a finite family
(i_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of objects in
I
. For
i\in I
, consider the subset
A_{i}
of
A
consisting of elements
\alpha\in A
such that
i_{\alpha}=i
. Note that
A_{i}
is empty for all but a finite number of
i\in I
. It follows from the definition of an
e^{-1}
-direct sum decomposition (see Section
3.4
) that the map

\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{fe}(X,Y)\mapsto\sum_{i\in I}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in A_{i}}(\phi q_{\alpha})\otimes_{\Bbbk}p_{\alpha}\right)\in\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(i,Y)\otimes_{\Bbbk}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,i)

is a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism with inverse defined on the summands by

v\otimes_{\Bbbk}u\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(i,Y)\otimes_{\Bbbk}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,i)\mapsto vu\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{fe}(X,Y).\qed

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
E
-Hom-graded category such that its 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is semisimple. Let
I
be a representative set of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

The category
\mathcal{C}
is
E
-semisimple.

(ii)

For all
i\in I
and
e\in E
, there exists a unique
j\in I
such that
i\cong_{e}j
.

(iii)

There is a left action
E\times I\to I
,
(e,i)\mapsto e\cdot i
, such that:

i\cong_{e}j\Longleftrightarrow j=e\cdot i\quad\text{for all $i,j\in I$ and $e\in E$.}

Proof.

Let us prove that (i) implies (ii). Let
i\in I
and
e\in E
. By Lemma
3.1
(i), there is a simple object
k
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
such that
i\cong_{e}k
. Since
I
is a representative set of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, there is
j\in I
such that
k\cong_{1}j
. Then
i\cong_{e}j
by (
3
). If
j^{\prime}\in I
is such that
i\cong_{e}j^{\prime}
, then
j\cong_{1}j^{\prime}
by (
3
), and so
j=j^{\prime}
since
I
is a representative set of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Let us prove that (ii) implies (iii). For any
i\in I
and
e\in E
, denote by
e\cdot i
the unique element of
I
verifying

i\cong_{e}e\cdot i
. This defines a map
E\times I\to I
. It follows from (
3
) and the uniqueness in (ii) that this map is a left action such that
i\cong_{e}j
if and only if
j=e\cdot i
.

Let us prove that (iii) implies (i). Let
X
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
e\in E
. We need to prove that
X
is an
e
-direct sum of a finite family of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Since
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is semisimple, there is a decomposition
(p_{\alpha}\colon X\to j_{\alpha},q_{\alpha}\colon j_{\alpha}\to i)_{\alpha\in A}
of
X
as a
1
-direct sum of a finite family
(j_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of simple objects. For any
\alpha\in A
, set
i_{\alpha}=(e^{-1}\cdot j_{\alpha})\in I
. Then
j_{\alpha}=e\cdot i_{\alpha}
and so
i_{\alpha}\cong_{e}j_{\alpha}
. Pick an
e
-isomorphism
\phi_{\alpha}\colon i_{\alpha}\to j_{\alpha}
and set

P_{\alpha}=\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}p_{\alpha}\colon X\to i_{\alpha}
and
Q_{\alpha}=q_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\colon i_{\alpha}\to X
. It is easy to verify that
(P_{\alpha},Q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
is a decomposition of
X
as an
e
-direct sum of the finite family
(i_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
.
∎

3.6.
Multiplicity numbers and partitions

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
E
-semisimple
E
-Hom-graded category. Given an object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
, a simple object
i
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, and
e\in E
, we denote by
N^{i,e}_{X}\geq 0
the number of simple objects 1-isomorphic to
i
in decomposition of
X
as an
e
-direct sum of a finite family of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. By (
5
) and Lemma
3.1
(ii), this number is equal to the ranks of the free
\Bbbk
-modules
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(i,X)
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e^{-1}}\!(X,i)
. We call
N^{i,e}_{X}
the
degree
e
multiplicity index of
i
in
X
. It follows from (
5
) that if
X
is a
f
-direct sum of a finite family
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
with
f\in E
, then

N^{i,e}_{X}=\sum_{\alpha\in A}N^{i,f^{-1}e}_{X_{\alpha}}.

If
I
is a representative set of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, then for all objects
X
of
\mathcal{C}
and
e\in E
, the family
\{N^{i,e}_{X}\}_{i\in I}
has only a finite number of nonzero terms, and Lemma
3.1
(iv) implies that
for all objects
X,Y
of
\mathcal{C}
and
e,f\in E
,

\mathrm{rank}_{\Bbbk}\bigl(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{fe}(X,Y)\bigr)=\sum_{i\in I}N^{i,f}_{Y}N^{i,e}_{X}.

Given a simple object
i
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
e\in E
, an
(i,e)
-partition
of an object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
is a family of morphisms
(p_{\alpha}\colon X\to i,\,q_{\alpha}\colon i\to X)_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
such that
(p_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is a basis of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e^{-1}}\!(X,i)
,
(q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is a basis of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(i,X)
, and
p_{\alpha}q_{\beta}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\,\mathrm{id}_{i}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda
. Note that the cardinality of
\Lambda
is equal to the multiplicity index
N^{i,e}_{X}
. The existence of
(i,e)
-partitions follows from
the fact that each object of
\mathcal{C}
is an
e
-direct sum of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Given a representative set
I
of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
e\in E
, an
(I,e)
-partition
of an object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
is a decomposition
(p_{\alpha}\colon X\to i_{\alpha},q_{\alpha}\colon i_{\alpha}\to X)_{\alpha\in A}
of
X
as an
e
-direct sum of a finite family
(i_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of objects in
I
. For such an
(I,e)
-partition and any
i\in I
, the family
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A,\,i_{\alpha}=i}
is an
(i,e)
-partition of
X
. Conversely, a union of
(i,e)
-partitions of
X
over
all
i\in I
is an
(I,e)
-partition of
X
.

4.
Monoidal categories graded by a crossed module

We introduce categories graded by a crossed module which are linear monoidal categories whose Hom-sets are graded by the source of the crossed module and whose objects are graded by the target of the crossed module in some compatible way (involving the crossed module axioms).

Throughout this section,
\chi\colon E\to H
denotes a crossed module. Recall from Section
2.1
that it comes equipped with a left action
(x,e)\in H\times E\mapsto{\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}^{x}{\hskip-0.80002pte}\in E
.

4.1.
\chi
-categories

A
\chi
-graded category (over
\Bbbk
)
, or shorter a
\chi
-category
, is a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
which is
E
-Hom-graded (see Section
3.2
) and is endowed with a subclass
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hom}}
of nonzero objects of
\mathcal{C}
, called
homogeneous objects
, and with a map
|\cdot|\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hom}}\to H
, called the
degree map
, such that:

(a)

Each object of
\mathcal{C}
is a
1
-direct sum of a finite family of homogeneous objects.

(b)

For all homogenous objects
X,Y
and
e\in E
such that
|Y|\neq\chi(e)|X|
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)=0.

(c)

The monoidal product
X\otimes Y
of any two homogeneous objects
X,Y
is a
1
-direct sum of homogeneous objects of degree
|X||Y|
.

(d)

The unit object
\mathbb{1}
is homogeneous with trivial degree (i.e.,
|\mathbb{1}|=1\in H
).

(e)

The monoidal product
\alpha\otimes\beta
of any two nonzero homogeneous morphisms
\alpha,\beta
is a homogeneous morphism of degree

|\alpha\otimes\beta|=|\alpha|\,{\vphantom{{|\beta|}}}^{|s(\alpha)|}{|\beta|},

whenever the source
s(\alpha)
of
\alpha
is a homogeneous object. In other words, for any objects
X,Y,Z,T
with
X
homogeneous and for any morphisms
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)
,
\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(Z,T)
with
e,f\in E
, we have:

\alpha\otimes\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e{\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{|X|}{\!f}}(X\otimes Z,Y\otimes T).

(f)

The associativity constraints
(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\cong X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)
and the unitality constraints
X\otimes\mathbb{1}\cong X\cong\mathbb{1}\otimes X
of
\mathcal{C}
are all homogenous of degree
1\in E
.

Note that we will suppress in our formulas the associativity and unitality constraints. Indeed, this does not lead to ambiguity because all legitimate ways of inserting these constraints give the same result (by MacLane’s coherence theorem) and do not affect the degree of morphisms (by the above axioms).

Axiom (a) implies that the Hom-sets in
\mathcal{C}
are fully determined by the Hom-sets between homogeneous objects.
Axiom (b) and (
5
) imply that if
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
,
(Y_{\beta})_{\beta\in B}
are finite families of homogeneous objects and
e\in E
is such that
|Y_{\beta}|\neq\chi(e)\,|X_{\alpha}|
for all
\alpha\in A
and
\beta\in B
, then

(6)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}^{1}X_{\alpha},\bigoplus_{\beta\in B}^{1}Y_{\beta}\right)=0.

Axiom (b) implies that for all homogenous objects
X,Y
, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)=\hskip-18.00005pt\bigoplus_{e\in\chi^{-1}(|Y||X|^{-1})}\hskip-18.00005pt\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(X)=\!\!\bigoplus_{e\in\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)}\!\!\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X).

In particular
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)=0
whenever
|Y||X|^{-1}\not\in{\mathrm{Im}}(\chi)
.
Also, if
\alpha\colon X\to Y
is a nonzero homogeneous morphism between homogeneous objects, then

(7)

|Y|=\chi(|\alpha|)\,|X|,

where
|\alpha|\in E
is the degree of
\alpha
(see Section
3.2
). In particular, 1-isomorphic homogenous objects have the same degree.

Axiom (e) implies that for any homogenous objects
X,Y,Z
of
\mathcal{C}
and
e\in E
,

X\cong_{e}Y\quad\Longrightarrow\quad X\otimes Z\cong_{e}Y\otimes Z\quad\text{and}\quad Z\otimes X\cong_{{\vphantom{{e}}}{}^{|Z|}{e}}Z\otimes Y.

4.2.
Relations with group-graded monoidal categories

Recall that a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category
\mathcal{D}
is
graded by the group
H
, or shorter
H
-graded
, if it decomposes as a direct sum

\mathcal{D}=\bigoplus_{h\in H}\mathcal{D}_{h},

of full subcategories such that
\mathbb{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{g}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{h}\subset\mathcal{D}_{gh}
for all
g,h\in H
(see for example
[
TVi1
]
or
[
EGNO
]
). The monoidal subcategory
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is called the
neutral component
of
\mathcal{D}
.

The 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
of a
\chi
-graded category
\mathcal{C}
is an
H
-graded
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category:

\mathcal{C}^{1}=\bigoplus_{h\in H}\mathcal{C}^{1}_{h}

where
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{h}
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
whose objects are (finite)
1
-direct sums of homogeneous objects of
\mathcal{C}
of degree
h
and whose morphisms are homogeneous morphisms of degree
1\in E
.

Also, any
\chi
-graded category
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathrm{Coker}(\chi)
-graded:

\mathcal{C}=\!\!\!\bigoplus_{p\in\mathrm{Coker}(\chi)}\!\!\!\mathcal{C}_{p}

where
\mathcal{C}_{p}
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
whose objects are (finite)
1
-direct sums of homogeneous objects of
\mathcal{C}
with degree in the preimage
\pi^{-1}(p)\subset H
of
p
under the canonical projection
\pi\colon H\to\mathrm{Coker}(\chi)=H/{\mathrm{Im}}(\chi)
.

4.3.
Particular cases

1. Given a group
H
, the trivial map
1\to H
is a crossed module and the notion of a
(1\to H)
-category agrees with that of an
H
-graded
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category (see Section
4.2
).

2. Given an abelian group
A
, the trivial map
A\to 1
is a crossed module and the notion of a
(A\to 1)
-category corresponds to that of a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category whose Hom-sets are
A
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules so that the identities and monoidal constraints have trivial degree and the composition and monoidal product of morphisms are both multiplicative with respect to the degree: for any
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{a}(X,Y)
,
\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{b}(Y,Z)
,
\gamma\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{c}(U,V)
with
a,b,c\in A
, we have:

\beta\circ\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{ba}(X,Z)\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\otimes\gamma\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{ac}(X\otimes U,Y\otimes V).

4.4.
Example

Recall from Section
2.5
that the crossed module
\chi
gives rise to a strict 2-group
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
.
Let us consider the linearization
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
of this 2-group. The objects of
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
are those of
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
, that is, the elements of
H
.
For any
x,y\in H
, the set
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}}(x,y)
is the free
\Bbbk
-module with basis
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}_{\chi}}(x,y)=\{e\in E\,|\,y=\chi(e)x\}
. The composition and monoidal product of
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
extend those of
\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
by bilinearity.
Then
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
is a
\Bbbk
-linear strict monoidal category. It is
\chi
-graded as follows: each object
x\in H
is homogeneous with degree
|x|=x
, and for any
x,y\in H
and
e\in E
,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}}^{e}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Bbbk e&\text{if $y=\chi(e)x$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

4.5.
Example

Let
E
be a normal subgroup of a group
H
. A
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphism
\alpha\colon M\to N
between
H
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules is
homogeneous of degree
e\in E
if
\alpha(M_{h})\subset N_{eh}
for all
h\in H
. Such homomorphisms form a submodule
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}^{e}(M,N)
of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(M,N)
. A
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphism
\alpha\colon M\to N
between
H
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules is
E
-graded
if it is a linear combination of homogeneous
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms with degree in
E
. Such homomorphisms form a submodule
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}^{E}(M,N)
of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(M,N)
. Clearly,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}^{E}(M,N)=\bigoplus_{e\in E}\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}^{e}(M,N).

With usual composition and identities,
H
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules and
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms form an
E
-Hom-graded category denoted
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E,H}
.
Note that
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E,E}
is the category
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E}
of Example
3.3
. We endow
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E,H}
with the monoidal product defined on objects by

M\otimes N=\bigoplus_{h\in H}\,(M\otimes N)_{h}\quad\text{where}\quad(M\otimes N)_{h}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in H\\
\,xy=h\end{subarray}}M_{x}\otimes_{\Bbbk}N_{y},

and on morphisms by
\alpha\otimes\beta=\alpha\otimes_{\Bbbk}\beta
. This gives a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category with unit object
\mathbb{1}
defined by
\mathbb{1}_{1}=\Bbbk
and
\mathbb{1}_{h}=0
for all
h\in H\setminus\{1\}
. An
H
-graded module
M=\bigoplus_{h\in H}M_{h}
is
homogeneous
if it is nonzero and there is
h\in H
such that
M_{x}=0
for all
x\in H\setminus\{h\}
. Such an
h
is then unique and called the
degree
of
M
. With these homogenous objects,
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E,H}
becomes a
(E\hookrightarrow H)
-category, where the inclusion
E\hookrightarrow H
is a crossed module with the conjugation action of
H
on
E
.

4.6.
Examples from representations

Recall that the category of representations of a Hopf algebra is monoidal (and closed). Examples of
\chi
-graded categories can be constructed similarly: in
[
SV
]
, we introduce Hopf
\chi
-(co)algebras and prove that their categories of representations are
\chi
-categories.

4.7.
Push-forward of
\chi
-categories

Let
\phi=(\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime},\varphi\colon H\to H^{\prime})
be a morphism from a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
to a crossed module
\chi^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to H^{\prime}
(see Section
2.3
). Every
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
determines a
\chi^{\prime}
-category
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
called the
push-forward
of
\mathcal{C}
. By definition,
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}
as monoidal categories. The
E^{\prime}
-Hom-grading of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is given for any objects
X,Y
and
e^{\prime}\in E
by

\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{e^{\prime}}(X,Y)=\bigoplus_{e\in\psi^{-1}(e^{\prime})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X,Y).

Homogenous objects of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
are the same as those of
\mathcal{C}
. The degree of a homogenous object
X
of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is computed from the degree of
X
in
\mathcal{C}
by

|X|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=\varphi(|X|_{\mathcal{C}}).

Note that 1-subcategory of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and its morphisms are the linear combinations of homogenous morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
with degree in
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
.

4.8.
Pivotal
\chi
-categories

Recall that a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbb{1})
is pivotal if it is endowed with a pivotal structure, that is, if each
object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
has a dual object
X^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
and four morphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{X}\colon X^{*}\otimes X\to\mathbb{1},\qquad\mathrm{coev}_{X}\colon\mathbb{1}\to X\otimes X^{*},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{X}\colon X\otimes X^{*}\to\mathbb{1},\qquad\widetilde{\mathrm{coev}}_{X}\colon\mathbb{1}\to X^{*}\otimes X,

called left/right (co)evaluations, satisfying several conditions which say, in summary, that the associated left/right dual functors coincide as monoidal functors (see
[
TVi4
]
for details). In particular, each morphism
\alpha\colon X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
has a dual morphism
\alpha^{*}\colon Y^{*}\to X^{*}
computed by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{ev}_{Y}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})(\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}\otimes\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})(\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{X})

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{Y})(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}})(\widetilde{\mathrm{coev}}_{X}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Y^{*}}).

The
left
and
right traces
of an endomorphism
\alpha
of an object

X
of a pivotal category
\mathcal{C}
are defined by

\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\alpha)=\mathrm{ev}_{X}(\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}\otimes\alpha)\widetilde{\mathrm{coev}}_{X}\quad{\text{and}}\quad\mathrm{tr}_{r}(\alpha)=\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{X}(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}})\mathrm{coev}_{X}.

Both traces take values in the commutative monoid
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{1})
and are symmetric:
\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\beta\gamma)=\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\gamma\beta)
for any morphisms
\beta\colon X\to Y
,
\gamma\colon Y\to X
in
\mathcal{C}

and similarly for
\mathrm{tr}_{r}
. Also
\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\alpha^{*})=\mathrm{tr}_{r}(\alpha)
and
\mathrm{tr}_{r}(\alpha^{*})=\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\alpha)
for any endomorphism in
\mathcal{C}
.
The
left
and
right dimensions
of an object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
are defined by

\dim_{l}(X)=\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\dim_{r}(X)=\mathrm{tr}_{r}(\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{1}).

Clearly,
\dim_{l}(X^{*})=\dim_{r}(X)
and
\dim_{r}(X^{*})=\dim_{l}(X)
for all
X
.

A
\chi
-category is
pivotal
if it is endowed with a pivotal structure such that:

(a)

The dual
X^{*}
of any homogenous object
X
is homogenous of degree

|X^{*}|=|X|^{-1}.

(b)

All (co)evaluations morphisms are homogenous of degree
1\in E
.

The second axiom implies that the 1-subcategory of a pivotal
\chi
-category is itself pivotal.
In a pivotal
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
, the dual
\alpha^{*}\colon Y^{*}\to X^{*}
of a nonzero homogenous morphism
\alpha\colon X\to Y
between homogenous objects is homogenous of degree

|\alpha^{*}|={\vphantom{{\!|\alpha|}}}^{|Y|^{-1}}{\!|\alpha|}={\vphantom{{\!|\alpha|}}}^{|X|^{-1}}{\!|\alpha|}.

This follows from the above expressions of the dual morphism. (Note that the latter equality may also be deduced from (
7
) and the crossed module axioms.) Moreover, for any
e\in E
and any homogeneous endomorphism
\alpha\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X)
of a homogenous object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
,

\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\alpha)\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bigl({\vphantom{{\!\!e}}}^{|X|^{-1}}{\!\!e}\bigr)}(\mathbb{1})\quad{\text{and}}\quad\mathrm{tr}_{r}(\alpha)\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1}).

This together with the fact that objects of
\mathcal{C}
are
1
-direct sum of homogeneous objects imply that both traces of homogeneous endomorphisms of degree 1 take values in
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})
. In particular, both dimensions of objects of
\mathcal{C}
take values in
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})
.

4.9.
Spherical
\chi
-categories

A
spherical
\chi
-category
is a pivotal
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
such that its 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is spherical (as a pivotal category), that is, if the left and right traces of any homogenous endomorphism of degree 1 are equal.

In a spherical
\chi
-category, the
trace
of a homogeneous endomorphism
\alpha
of degree 1 is defined by

\mathrm{tr}(\alpha)=\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\alpha)=\mathrm{tr}_{r}(\alpha)\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1}).

Likewise, the
dimension
of an object
X
is defined by

\dim(X)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{id}_{X})=\dim_{l}(X)=\dim_{r}(X)\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1}).

4.10.
\chi
-fusion categories

A
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
is
pre-fusion
if it is
E
-semisimple (see Section
3.5
) and the unit object
\mathbb{1}
is simple in the 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
of
\mathcal{C}
, that is,
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})
is free of rank 1. The map
\Bbbk\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})
,
k\mapsto k\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{1}}
is then a
\Bbbk
-algebra isomorphism
which we use to identify
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})=\Bbbk
. In a pre-fusion
\chi
-category, if an object
X
is a
1
-direct sum of homogeneous objects of degree in
D\subset H
and
i
is a homogeneous simple object of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, then
N^{i,e}_{X}=0
for all
e\in E
such that
\chi(e)|i|\not\in D
. The left and right dimensions of a simple object in the 1-subcategory of a pivotal pre-fusion
\chi
-category are invertible (see for example
[
TVi4
, Lemma 4.2]
).

A
\chi
-representative set
for a pre-fusion
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
is a set
I
of simple objects of the 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
such that
\mathbb{1}\in I
, all elements of
I
are homogeneous, and every simple object of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is 1-isomorphic to a unique element of
I
. Note that
I
is then representative set of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
in the sense of Section
3.1
.
Any such
\chi
-representative set
I
splits as a disjoint union
I=\amalg_{h\in H}\,I_{h}
where
I_{h}
is the set of all elements of
I
of degree
h
. Moreover, any homogeneous object of
\mathcal{C}
of degree
h\in H
is a 1-direct sum of a finite family of elements in
I_{h}
.

By a
\chi
-fusion category (over
\Bbbk
)
we mean a pre-fusion
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
(over
\Bbbk
) such that for any
h\in H
, the set of
1
-isomorphism classes of degree
h
homogeneous simple objects of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is finite and non-empty. In particular, any
\chi
-representative set
I=\amalg_{h\in H}\,I_{h}
for a
\chi
-fusion category is such that
I_{h}
is finite for all
h\in I
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pivotal
\chi
-fusion category. Recall from Section
4.2
that the 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
of
\mathcal{C}
decomposes as
\mathcal{C}^{1}=\bigoplus_{h\in H}\mathcal{C}^{1}_{h}
. Then
\mathcal{C}^{1}
is a pivotal
H
-fusion category in the sense of
[
TVi1
]
, and its neutral component
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}
is a pivotal fusion category in the usual sense.
The
dimension
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1}
(as a pivotal fusion category) is defined by

\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})=\sum_{i\in J}\dim_{l}(i)\dim_{r}(i)\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})=\Bbbk,

where
J
is any (finite) representative set of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1}
.
Note that if
\Bbbk
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, then
\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})\neq 0
(see
[
ENO
]
). By
[
TVi1
, Lemma 4.1]
, if
I=\amalg_{h\in H}\,I_{h}
is a
\chi
-representative set for
\mathcal{C}
, then for all
h\in H
,

(8)

\sum_{i\in I_{h}}\dim_{l}(i)\dim_{r}(i)=\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1}).

4.11.
Remark

If
\chi\colon E\to H
is injective (for example when
E=1
), then any
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
, endowed with the
\mathrm{Coker}(\chi)
-grading defined in Section
4.2
, is a
\mathrm{Coker}(\chi)
-fusion category (in the sense of
[
TVi1
]
) whose neutral component has dimension
\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})
.

4.12.
Example

The
\chi
-category
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
from Example
4.4
has a canonical pivotal structure: the dual of an object
h\in H
is
h^{*}=h^{-1}
with (co)evaluation morphisms all given by the unit element
1\in E
. Then
\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi}
is a spherical
\chi
-fusion category with
\chi
-representative set
I=H
and with
\dim((\Bbbk\mathcal{G}_{\chi})^{1}_{1})=1_{\Bbbk}
.

4.13.
Example

Let
E
be a normal subgroup of a group
H
. The inclusion
E\hookrightarrow H
is a crossed module with the conjugation action of
H
on
E
. Denote by
(E,H)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
the category of
H
-graded free
\Bbbk
-modules of finite rank and
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms. This is an
(E\hookrightarrow H)
-category (as a full subcategory of the category
\mathcal{M}_{\Bbbk}^{E,H}
from Example
4.5
).
It has a canonical pivotal structure: the dual of an object
M=\bigoplus_{h\in H}M_{h}
is

M^{*}=\bigoplus_{h\in H}(M^{*})_{h}\quad\text{where}\quad(M^{*})_{h}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(M_{h^{-1}},\Bbbk)

with evaluations
\mathrm{ev}_{M}\colon M^{*}\otimes M\to\mathbb{1}
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{M}\colon M\otimes M^{*}\to\mathbb{1}
given for any
h,k\in H
,
m\in M_{h}
, and
f\in(M^{\ast})_{k}
by

\mathrm{ev}_{M}(f\otimes_{\Bbbk}m)=\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{M}(m\otimes_{\Bbbk}f)=\delta_{k,h^{-1}}\,f(m).

Then
(E,H)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
is a spherical
(E\hookrightarrow H)
-fusion category. For any
h\in H
, let
\Bbbk_{h}
be the
H
-graded free
\Bbbk
-module of rank 1 defined by
(\Bbbk_{h})_{h}=\Bbbk
and
(\Bbbk_{h})_{x}=0
for
x\in H\setminus\{h\}
. Note that
\Bbbk_{h}
is homogeneous of degree
h
.
Then
\{\Bbbk_{h}\}_{h\in H}
is a
(E\hookrightarrow H)
-representative set for
(E,H)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
.
Clearly,
\dim(\Bbbk_{h})=1_{\Bbbk}\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})=\Bbbk
for all
h\in H
. In particular,
\dim\bigl(((E,H)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk})^{1}_{1}\bigr)=1_{\Bbbk}
. Also, for all
g,h\in H
and
e\in E
,

\Bbbk_{g}\otimes\Bbbk_{h}\cong_{1}\Bbbk_{gh},\quad\Bbbk_{h}^{*}\cong_{1}\Bbbk_{h^{-1}},\quad\text{and}\quad\Bbbk_{g}\cong_{e}\Bbbk_{h}\Longleftrightarrow\chi(e)=hg^{-1}.

Note that by Remark
4.11
,
(E,H)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
is
(H/E)
-fusion in the sense of
[
TVi1
]
with neutral component of dimension
1_{\Bbbk}
.

4.14.
Push forward of fusion
\chi
-categories

Let
\phi=(\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime},\varphi\colon H\to H^{\prime})
be a morphism from a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
to a crossed module
\chi^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to H^{\prime}
(see Section
2.3
). Assume that
\psi
and
\varphi
are surjective,
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)=1
, and
\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)
is finite. These conditions imply that the map
\chi
induces an injective group homomorphism
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)
, and so
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
is finite and its order divides that of
\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)
. Then the push-forward
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
of any
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
(defined in Section
4.7
) is a
\chi^{\prime}
-fusion category.
Also, if
\mathcal{C}
is pivotal or spherical, then so is
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
(with the same pivotal structure) and

\dim\bigr(\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}_{1}\bigl)=\frac{\mathrm{card}\bigr(\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)\bigl)}{\mathrm{card}\bigr(\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\bigl)}\,\dim(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}).

Indeed, for any homogeneous object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
, we have:

\mathrm{End}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{1}(X)=\hskip-20.00003pt\bigoplus_{e\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)=1}\hskip-20.00003pt\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(X)=\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(X),

and so an object is simple in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}
if and only if it is simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Then the semisimplicity of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}
follows from that of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Also, the
E
-semisimplicity of
\mathcal{C}
together with the surjectivity of
\psi
imply that
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is
E^{\prime}
-semisimple and so a pre-fusion
\chi^{\prime}
-category. Moreover, the facts that
\mathcal{C}
is a
\chi
-fusion and
\varphi
is surjective with finite kernel imply that
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is a
\chi^{\prime}
-fusion category. Finally, the formula for the dimension of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}_{1}
is proved in Claim
7.5
below.

4.15.
Example

A crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
comes with a left action of
H
on
E
and so induces the semidirect product
group
H\ltimes E=(H\times E,\ast)
where

(x,e)\ast(y,f)=(xy,e\hskip 1.00006pt{\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{x}{\!f}).

Then
E=1\times E
is a normal subgroup of
H\ltimes E
and so the inclusion
\iota\colon E\hookrightarrow H\ltimes E
is a crossed module (with the conjugation action of
H\ltimes E
on
E
). Consider spherical
\iota
-fusion category
(E,H\ltimes E)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
from Example
4.13
.
The pair

\phi=(\mathrm{id}_{E}\colon E\to E,\varphi\colon H\ltimes E\to H)\quad\text{with}\quad\varphi(h,e)=\chi(e)h

is a crossed module morphism from
\iota
to
\chi
. Therefore, by Section
4.7
, the push-forward
\phi_{*}((E,H\ltimes E)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk})
is a
\chi
-category denoted
\chi\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
. More explicitly, the objects of
\chi\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
are the

(H\ltimes E)
-graded free
\Bbbk
-modules of finite rank. The degree of a homogeneous object
M=M_{(h,e)}
is
|M|=\chi(e)h
. The morphisms of
\chi\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
are the
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms (see Example
4.5
).
Note that
\mathrm{id}_{E}
and
\varphi
are surjective,
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{id}_{E})\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)=1
, and there is a group isomorphism

\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)=\{(h,e)\in H\ltimes E\,|\,\chi(e)h=1\}\cong E,\quad(h,e)\mapsto e^{-1}.

Consequently, by Section
4.14
, if
E
is finite, then
\chi\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
is a spherical
\chi
-fusion category and

\dim\bigr((\chi\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk})^{1}_{1}\bigl)=\mathrm{card}(E)1_{\Bbbk}.

4.16.
Twisting
\chi
-categories by cocycles

By a
3-cocycle
for the crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
with values in an abelian group
A
, we mean a map
\omega\colon H^{3}\times E^{3}\to A
such that for all
(x,y,z,t)\in H^{4}
and
(a,b,c,d,e,f)\in E^{6}
,

\displaystyle\omega\bigl(x,y,z,a,b,c\bigr)\,\omega\bigl(x,\chi(c)yz,t,ba\hskip 1.00006pt{\vphantom{{\hskip-1.00006pt(}}}^{x}{\hskip-1.00006pt(}c^{-1}),f,d\bigr)\,\omega\bigl(y,z,t,c,d,e\bigr)

\displaystyle=\omega\bigl(\chi(a)xy,z,t,b,f,e\bigr)\,\omega\bigl(x,y,\chi(e)zt,a,fba\hskip 1.00006pt{\vphantom{{\hskip-1.00006pt(}}}^{xy}{\hskip-1.00006pt(}e^{-1})a^{-1},dc\hskip 1.00006pt{\vphantom{{\hskip-1.00006pt(}}}^{y}{\hskip-1.00006pt(}e^{-1})\bigr).

Note that the map
\widetilde{\omega}\colon H^{3}\to A
, defined by
\widetilde{\omega}(x,y,z)=\omega(x,y,z,1,1,1)
, is then a (usual) 3-cocycle for the group
H
with values in
A
.
A 3-cocycle
\omega
for
\chi
is
normalized
if for all
x,y\in H
and
e\in E
,

\omega(1,x,y,1,e,1)=1_{A}=\omega(x,1,y,1,1,1).

Normalized 3-cocycles for
\chi
with values in
A
can be used to describe the third cohomology classes in
H^{3}(B\chi,A)
, where
B\chi
is the classifying space of
\chi
(see
[
FP
]
for the unnormalized case).

Any normalized 3-cocycle
\omega
for
\chi
with values in the group
\Bbbk^{*}
can be used to twist the composition of morphisms, the monoidal product of morphisms, and the associativity constraints of a
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
(over
\Bbbk
) to obtain a new
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
(over
\Bbbk
). More precisely, the objects, the homogeneous objects, the degree of homogeneous objects, the monoidal product of objects, the unit object
\mathbb{1}
, the Hom-sets (as
E
-graded
\Bbbk
-modules), and the identity morphisms for
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
are the same as those for
\mathcal{C}
. The composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
is given, for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}^{e}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
and
\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}^{f}_{\mathcal{C}}(Y,Z)
with
X
homogeneous, by

\beta\circ^{\omega}\alpha=\omega\bigl(|X|,1,1,e,f,1\bigr)^{-1}\beta\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}^{fe}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Z).

The monoidal product of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
is given, for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}^{e}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
and
\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}^{f}_{\mathcal{C}}(Z,T)
with
X,Y
homogeneous, by

\alpha\otimes^{\omega}\beta=\dfrac{\omega\bigl(|X|,|Y|,1,e{\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{|X|}{\!f},1,f\bigr)}{\omega\bigl(|X|,1,\chi(f)|Y|,e,1,1\bigr)}\,\alpha\otimes\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}^{e{\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{|X|}{\!f}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X\otimes Z,Y\otimes T).

For any homogeneous objects
X,Y,Z
, the monoidal constraint

(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\cong X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)

of
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
is
\widetilde{\omega}(|X|,|Y|,|Z|)
times that of
\mathcal{C}
, where
\widetilde{\omega}\colon H^{3}\to\Bbbk^{*}
is the 3-cocycle for
H
derived from
\omega
as above. Since
\widetilde{\omega}
is normalized (because
\omega
is), the unitality constraints
X\otimes\mathbb{1}\cong X\cong X\otimes\mathbb{1}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
are those of
\mathcal{C}
.

Note that
(\mathcal{C}^{\omega})^{1}_{1}=\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}
as
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal categories. Also, recall that the 1-subcategory of a
\chi
-category is
H
-graded (see Section
4.2
). Then
(\mathcal{C}^{\omega})^{1}
is the twisted
H
-graded category
(\mathcal{C}^{1})^{\widetilde{\omega}}
, that is, the
H
-graded category
\mathcal{C}^{1}
with monoidal constraints twisted by the 3-cocycle
\widetilde{\omega}
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is pivotal, then so is
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
with the same dual objects and with the following twisted (co)evaluations: for any homogeneous object
X
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}^{\omega}_{X}=\mathrm{ev}_{X},

\displaystyle\mathrm{coev}^{\omega}_{X}=\widetilde{\omega}\bigl(|X|,|X|^{-1},|X|\bigr)^{-1}\mathrm{coev}_{X},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{coev}}^{\omega}_{X}=\widetilde{\mathrm{coev}}_{X},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}^{\omega}_{X}=\widetilde{\omega}\bigl(|X|,|X|^{-1},|X|\bigr)\,\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{X}.

If
\mathcal{C}
is spherical, then so is
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
. Also, if
\mathcal{C}
is pre-fusion, then so is
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
with the same
\chi
-representative sets. Finally, if
\mathcal{C}
is
\chi
-fusion, then so is
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
.

4.17.
Penrose graphical calculus

We will represent morphisms in a category
\mathcal{C}
by plane diagrams to be read from the bottom to the top.
The diagrams are made of oriented arcs colored by objects of
\mathcal{C}
and of boxes colored by morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
. The arcs connect the boxes and have no mutual intersections or self-intersections.
The identity
\mathrm{id}_{X}
of
X\in\mathcal{C}
, a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
, and the composition of two morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
are represented as follows:

\mathrm{id}_{X}=

,
f=

,
and

gf=

.

If
\mathcal{C}
is monoidal, then the monoidal product of two morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Z\to T
is represented by juxtaposition:

f\otimes g=

.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is pivotal. By convention, if an arc colored by
X\in\mathcal{C}
is oriented upwards,
then the corresponding object in the source/target of morphisms
is
X^{*}
. For example,
\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
and a morphism
f\colon X^{*}\otimes Y\to U\otimes V^{*}\otimes W
may be depicted as

\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}=

=

and

f=

.

The duality morphisms are depicted as follows:

\mathrm{ev}_{X}=

,
\mathrm{coev}_{X}=

,
\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{X}=

,
\widetilde{\mathrm{coev}}_{X}=

.

The dual of a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
and the traces of an endomorphism
\alpha\colon X\to X
can be depicted as
follows:

f^{*}=

=

and

\mathrm{tr}_{l}(\alpha)=

,
\mathrm{tr}_{r}(\alpha)=

.

The morphisms represented by such diagrams are invariant under isotopies of the diagrams in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
keeping the bottom and top endpoints fixed (see for example
[
JS
]
or
[
TVi4
, Theorem 2.6]
).

4.18.
Enriched graphical calculus

For a pivotal pre-fusion
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we enrich the Penrose graphical calculus of the previous section. Let
X
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
,
i
be a simple object of the 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
of
\mathcal{C}
, and
e\in E
.
Consider a (finite) formal sum of diagrams

(9)

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\,\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor0.eps}}}

where
(p_{\alpha}\colon X\to i,q_{\alpha}\colon i\to X)_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is an
(i,e)
-partition of
X
(see Section
3.6
) and the gray area represents a part of these diagrams independent of
\alpha\in\Lambda
(and, in particular, not involving
p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha}
). By the Penrose graphical calculus and the
\Bbbk
-linearity of
\mathcal{C}
, the sum (
9
) represents a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
.
Since the tensor

(10)

\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}p_{\alpha}\otimes q_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e^{-1}}\!(X,i)\otimes_{\Bbbk}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(i,X)

does not depend on the choice of the
(i,e)
-partition of
X
,
the morphism (
9
) in
\mathcal{C}
also does not
depend on this choice. We therefore can eliminate the colors
p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha}
of the two boxes, keeping in mind only the order of the boxes and the fact that they jointly stand for the tensor (
10
). We will graphically represent this pair of boxes by two curvilinear boxes (a semi-disk and a compressed rectangle) labeled by
e
and painted with the same
color which stand respectively for
p_{\alpha}
and
q_{\alpha}
where
\alpha
runs over
\Lambda
:

(11)

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor1e-col.eps}}}\,=\,\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\,\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor0.eps}}}\,.

The gray areas in the picture are the same as above.
We will also use similar notation obtained from (
11
) by reorienting the
X
-colored arcs upward and replacing
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
with an
(i,e)
-partition of
X^{*}
, or by reorienting the
i
-colored arcs upward and replacing
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
with an
(i^{*},e)
-partition of
X
.
We will allow several arcs to be attached to the bottom of the semi-disk
and to the top of the compressed rectangle in (
11
). The number of these arcs, their directions (up/down), and their colors should be the same. For example,

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-not-1-col.eps}}}\,=\,\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\,\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-not-2.eps}}}\,,

where
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is any
(i^{*},e)
-partition of
X^{*}\otimes Y
.
We will allow to erase
i
-colored arcs for
i=\mathbb{1}
. In particular,

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-not-3-col.eps}}}\,=\,\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda}\,\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-not-4.eps}}}\,,

where
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is any
(\mathbb{1},e)
-partition of
X
.
Furthermore, in accordance with the isotopy invariance,

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-dual-2-col.eps}}}\qquad\text{will stand for}\qquad\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-dual-1a-col.eps}}}\;=\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-dual-1b-col.eps}}}\;\;\phantom{.}

and

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-dual-4-col.eps}}}\qquad\text{will stand for}\qquad\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-dual-3a-col.eps}}}\;=\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-dual-3b-col.eps}}}\;\;.

Similar notation will be applied when the
X
-colored or
i
-colored arcs are oriented downward and when there are several arcs attached to the horizontal sides of the curvilinear boxes.

Every enriched diagram as above represents a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
which is invariant under ambient isotopies (relative endpoints) of the diagram in the plane.

We now state four properties of the above enriched graphical calculus associated with the pivotal pre-fusion
\chi
-category
\mathcal{C}
. First, for any object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
, any simple object
i
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, and any
e\in E
,

(12)

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-3-ng-col.eps}}}\;\,=\,N_{X}^{i,e}\;\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-4-ng.eps}}}

where
N^{i,e}_{X}
is the degree
e
multiplicity index of
i
in
X
. This equality follows from the fact that for any
(i,e)
-partition
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
of
X
, we have
p_{\alpha}q_{\alpha}=\mathrm{id}_{i}
for all
\alpha\in\Lambda
and
\mathrm{card}(\Lambda)=N_{X}^{i,e}
. Second, pick a
\chi
-representative set
I
for
\mathcal{C}
.
For any object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
and
e\in E
,

(13)

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\sum_{i\in I}\;\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-1-ng-col.eps}}}\;\,=\;\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-2-ng.eps}}}\;\,.

This follows from the fact that the union of
(i,e)
-partitions of
X
over all
i\in I
is an
(I,e)
-partition of
X
.
Third, for any homogenous morphisms
\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},X)
and
\beta\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e^{-1}}\!(X,\mathbb{1})
with
e\in E
, we have

(14)

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-1-un-col.eps}}}\;\,=\;\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-2-un-col.eps}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-3-un-col.eps}}}\;\,=\;\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-4-un-col.eps}}}\;\,.

These equalities follow from (
13
) and the fact
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1},i)=0=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i,\mathbb{1})
for all
i\in I\setminus\{\mathbb{1}\}
. Fourth, for any object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
and
e\in E
,

(15)

\dim_{l}(X)=\sum_{i\in I}\dim_{l}(i)N_{X}^{i,e}\quad\text{and}\quad\dim_{r}(X)=\sum_{i\in I}\dim_{r}(i)N_{X}^{i,e}.

Here, recall that
N^{i,e}_{X}=0
for all but a finite number of
i\in I
. These equalities follow from Formulas (
12
) and (
13
) together with the isotopy invariance of the enriched graphical calculus:

\displaystyle\dim_{l}(X)=\;\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-cor-3.eps}}}\;=\;\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\sum_{i\in I}\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-cor-1-col.eps}}}\;=\;\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\sum_{i\in I}\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-cor-4-col.eps}}}

\displaystyle\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag=\;\sum_{i\in I}\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-cor-2-col.eps}}}\;=\;\psfrag\sum_{i\in I}\;N^{i,e}_{X}\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-cor-3.eps}}}\;=\;\sum_{i\in I}\dim_{l}(i)N_{X}^{i,e}

and similarly for the right trace.
As an application, we prove the following claim.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pivotal
\chi
-fusion category and let
I=\amalg_{h\in H}\,I_{h}
be a
\chi
-
representative set for
\mathcal{C}
. Then, for any homogeneous objects
X,Y
of
\mathcal{C}
,
g,h\in H
, and
e\in E
such that
\chi(e)=|X|g|Y|h
, we have:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in I_{g}\\
n\in I_{h}\end{subarray}}\!\dim_{l}(m)\dim_{l}(n)N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y\otimes n}^{\mathbb{1},e}=\dim_{r}(X)\dim_{r}(Y)\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})

and similarly with the subscripts
l,r
exchanged.

Proof.

Observe first that for any object
Z
of
\mathcal{C}
,

N^{\mathbb{1},e}_{Z\otimes n}=N^{n^{*}\!,e}_{Z}.

Indeed the free
\Bbbk
-modules
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},Z\otimes n)
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(n^{*},Z)
have the same rank since the map

\alpha\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},Z\otimes n)\mapsto(\mathrm{id}_{Z}\otimes\widetilde{\mathrm{ev}}_{n})(\alpha\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n^{*}})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(n^{*},Z)
is a well-defined
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism. Thus, using the identity
\dim_{l}(n)=\dim_{r}(n^{*})
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in I_{g}\\
n\in I_{h}\end{subarray}}\!\dim_{l}(m)\dim_{l}(n)N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y\otimes n}^{\mathbb{1},e}=\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in I_{g}\\
n\in I_{h}\end{subarray}}\!\dim_{l}(m)\dim_{r}(n^{*})N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y}^{n^{*}\!,e}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in I_{g}}\!\dim_{l}(m)\!\sum_{i\in I_{h^{-1}}}\!\!\dim_{r}(i)N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y}^{i,e}.

Now, for any
m\in I_{g}
, since
X\otimes m\otimes Y
is a 1-direct sum of homogenous objects of degree
|X|g|Y|
, we have
N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y}^{i,e}=0
whenever

i\in I
satisfies
\chi(e)|i|\neq|X|g|Y|
or equivalently
|i|\neq h^{-1}
(see Section
4.10
) and so Formula (
15
) gives that

\sum_{i\in I_{h^{-1}}}\!\!\dim_{r}(i)N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y}^{i,e}=\sum_{i\in I}\!\dim_{r}(i)N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y}^{i,e}=\dim_{r}(X\otimes m\otimes Y).

Then, using the
\otimes
-multiplicativity of the dimensions (which follows from the fact that the unit object
\mathbb{1}
is simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, see Sections 2.6.2 and 4.2.2 of
[
TVi4
]
) and Formula (
8
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in I_{g}\\
n\in I_{h}\end{subarray}}\!\dim_{l}(m)\dim_{l}(n)N_{X\otimes m\otimes Y\otimes n}^{\mathbb{1},e}=\sum_{m\in I_{g}}\!\dim_{l}(m)\dim_{r}(X\otimes m\otimes Y)

\displaystyle=\dim_{r}(X)\dim_{r}(Y)\sum_{m\in I_{g}}\dim_{l}(m)\dim_{r}(m)=\dim_{r}(X)\dim_{r}(Y)\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1}).

A similar proof works when the subscripts
l,r
are exchanged.
∎

In the next lemma, each equality has gray areas which are supposed to be the same on both sides.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
, any homogeneous simple object
i
of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, and any
e\in E
, we have:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-new-col.eps}}}\,\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag=\,\dim_{l}(i)\;\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-13a-col.eps}}}\;\,.

Proof.

Set
a={\vphantom{{e}}}^{|i|}{e}
. Pick a
(\mathbb{1},e)
-partition
(p_{\alpha},q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
of
i^{*}\otimes X
. For each
\alpha\in\Lambda
, set

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag P_{\alpha}=\dim_{l}(i)\;\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-pf-2-col.eps}}}\,\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{{\vphantom{{e^{-1}}}}{}^{|i|}{e^{-1}}}\!(X,i)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{a^{-1}}\!(X,i)

and

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag Q_{\alpha}=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-pf-1-col.eps}}}\,\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{{\vphantom{{e}}}{}^{|i|}{e}}(i,X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{a}(i,X).

Since
(p_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is a basis of the
\Bbbk
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e^{-1}}\!(i^{*}\otimes X,\mathbb{1})
and
\dim_{l}(i)
is invertible in
\Bbbk
, the family
(P_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is a basis of the
\Bbbk
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{a^{-1}}\!(X,i)
. Since
(q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is a basis of the
\Bbbk
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},i^{*}\otimes X)
, the family
(Q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is a basis of the
\Bbbk
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{a}(i,X)
. Since the object
i
is simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda
, we have
P_{\alpha}Q_{\beta}\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(i)=\Bbbk\,\mathrm{id}_{i}
and so
P_{\alpha}Q_{\beta}=\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}\,\mathrm{id}_{i}
where

\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}\;\overset{(i)}{=}\;\frac{\mathrm{tr}_{l}(P_{\alpha}Q_{\beta})}{\dim_{l}(i)}\;\overset{(ii)}{=}\;\;\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-ppte-pf-4-col.eps}}}\;\;\overset{(iii)}{=}\;\delta_{\alpha,\beta}.

Here
(i)
follows from the invertibility of
\dim_{l}(i)
,
(ii)
from the definitions of
P_{\alpha}
,
Q_{\beta}
and the isotopy invariance of the graphical calculus, and
(iii)
from the equality
p_{\alpha}q_{\beta}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
. Thus
(P_{\alpha},Q_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is an
(i,a)
-partition of
X
. This directly implies the lemma.
∎

5.
Multiplicity modules and
\chi
-graphs

Throughout this section,
\chi\colon E\to H
is a crossed module and
\mathcal{C}
is a pivotal
\chi
-
category.
We first introduce
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-cyclic sets and their multiplicity modules. Then we define an isotopy invariant of
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs.

5.1.
Multiplicity modules of colored
\chi
-cyclic sets

A
\chi
-cyclic set
is a totally cyclically ordered finite set
S
endowed with maps
\alpha\colon S\to H
,
\beta\colon S\to E
, and
\varepsilon\colon S\to\{+,-\}
such that for any
s\in S
,

(16)

\chi(\beta(s))=\alpha(s_{1})^{\varepsilon(s_{1})}\cdots\alpha(s_{n})^{\varepsilon(s_{n})}

where
n
is the number of elements of
S
and
s=s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{n}
are the elements of
S
ordered in the given cyclic order starting from
s
, and

(17)

\beta(\mathrm{succ}(s))={\vphantom{{\!\beta(s)}}}^{\left(\alpha(s)^{-\varepsilon(s)}\right)}{\!\beta(s)}

where
\mathrm{succ}(s)
is the successor of
s
in the given cyclic order. Note that (
17
) implies that the map
\beta
is fully determined by its value on one element of
S
, and that if (
16
) holds for one element of
S
, then it holds for all elements of
S
.

A
\chi
-cyclic set
S
is
\mathcal{C}
-colored
if it is endowed with a map
c\colon S\to\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
such that for any
s\in S
, the object
c(s)
is homogeneous of degree
|c(s)|=\alpha(s)
. For shortness,
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-cyclic sets are also called
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-sets
.

Each
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set
S=(S,\alpha,\beta,\varepsilon,c)
determines a
\Bbbk
-module
H(S)
called the
multiplicity module
of
S
and defined as follows. For
s\in S
, set

H_{s}=H_{s}(S)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta(s)}(\mathbb{1},c(s_{1})^{\varepsilon(s_{1})}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(s_{n})^{\varepsilon(s_{n})}),

where
s=s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{n}
are the elements of
S
ordered in the given cyclic order starting from
s
. Here
X^{+}=X
and
X^{-}=X^{*}
for any object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
. If
t\in S\setminus\{s\}
, then
t=s_{k}

for some
k\in\{2,\dots,n\}
. Set

[st)=c(s_{1})^{\varepsilon(s_{1})}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(s_{k-1})^{\varepsilon(s_{k-1})}\quad\text{and}\quad[ts)=c(s_{k})^{\varepsilon(s_{k})}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(s_{n})^{\varepsilon(s_{n})}.

Clearly,

H_{s}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta(s)}(\mathbb{1},[st)\otimes[ts))\quad\text{and}\quad H_{t}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta(t)}(\mathbb{1},[ts)\otimes[st)).

Set
p_{s,s}=\mathrm{id}_{H_{s}}\colon{H_{s}}\to{H_{s}}
. For distinct
s,t\in S
, define
p_{s,t}\colon H_{s}\to H_{t}
by

(18)

v\in H_{s}\mapsto p_{s,t}(v)=\!\!\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{defsigma2n.eps}}}\;=\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{defsigman.eps}}}\!\!\!\in H_{t}.

It follows from (
16
), (
17
), and the axioms of a pivotal
\chi
-category that
p_{s,t}
is a well-defined
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism and
p_{s,t}\,p_{r,s}=p_{r,t}
for all
r,s,t\in S
.
Thus the family
(\{H_{s}\}_{s\in S},\{p_{s,t}\}_{s,t\in S})
is a projective system of
\Bbbk
-modules and
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphisms.
The multiplicity module
H(S)
is the projective limit of this system:

H(S)=\underleftarrow{\lim}\,H_{s}\,.

The module
H(S)
depends only on
S=(S,\alpha,\beta,\varepsilon,c)
and is equipped with a family of
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphisms
\{\tau_{s}\colon H(S)\to H_{s}\}_{s\in S}
such that
p_{s,t}\,\tau_{s}=\tau_{t}
for all
s,t\in S
. We call
\tau_{s}
the
cone isomorphism
and the family
\{\tau_{s}\}_{s\in S}
the
universal cone
.
Recall that
H(S)
may be realized as the following submodule of the
\Bbbk
-module
\prod_{s\in S}H_{s}
:

H(S)=\Bigl\{(x_{s})_{s\in S}\in\prod_{s\in S}H_{s}\;\Big|\;p_{s,t}(x_{s})=x_{t}\;\text{for all $s,t\in S$}\Bigr\}.

The cone isomorphism
\tau_{s}\colon H(S)\to H_{s}
associated with this realization is given by
\tau_{s}((x_{t})_{t\in S})=x_{s}
and its inverse is computed by
\tau_{s}^{-1}(v)=(p_{s,t}(v))_{t\in S}
.

Note that if the category
\mathcal{C}
is pre-fusion (see Section
4.10
), then the
\Bbbk
-module
H_{s}
is free of finite rank for all
s\in S
, and thus so is the multiplicity module
H(S)
.

An isomorphism of
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-sets
\phi\colon S\to S^{\prime}
is a bijection which preserves the cyclic order and commutes with the maps to
H
,
E
,
\{+,-\}
, and
\mathcal{C}
. Such a map
\phi
induces a
\Bbbk
-isomorphism
H(\phi)\colon H(S)\to H(S^{\prime})
in the obvious way.

The
dual
of a
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set
S=(S,\alpha,\beta,\varepsilon,c)
is the
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set

S^{\mathrm{op}}=(S^{\mathrm{op}},\alpha,\beta^{\mathrm{op}},-\varepsilon,c)

where
S^{\mathrm{op}}
is
S
with opposite cyclic order and, for any
s\in S
,
\beta^{\mathrm{op}}(s)=\beta(\mathrm{succ}(s))^{-1}
. Here,
\mathrm{succ}(s)
denotes the successor of
s
in the cyclic order of
S
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is spherical pre-fusion, then the
\Bbbk
-bilinear pairings

\left\{\omega_{S,s}\colon H_{\mathrm{pred}(s)}(S^{\mathrm{op}})\otimes_{\Bbbk}H_{s}(S)\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})=\Bbbk,\quad u\otimes_{\Bbbk}v\mapsto\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.35pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{Ev-CM.eps}}}\,\right\}_{\!\!s\in S},

where
\mathrm{pred}(s)
denotes the predecessor in the cyclic order of
S
,
are symmetric, non-degenerate, and compatible with the permutation maps (
18
), and so induce
a non-degenerate
\Bbbk
-bilinear pairing
\omega_{S}\colon H(S^{\mathrm{op}})\otimes H(S)\to\Bbbk
, where
\otimes
is the unordered tensor product of
\Bbbk
-modules. In this case, the vector

\ast_{S}=\Omega_{S}(1_{\Bbbk})\in H(S)\otimes H(S^{\mathrm{op}}),

where
