\Omega_{S}\colon\Bbbk\to H(S)\otimes H(S^{\mathrm{op}})
is the inverse pairing of
\omega_{S}
, is called the
contraction vector
of
S
. Note that it is induced from the cone isomorphisms and the vectors

\left\{\,\ast_{S,s}=\Omega_{S,s}(1_{\Bbbk})\in H_{s}(S)\otimes_{\Bbbk}H_{\mathrm{pred}(s)}(S^{\mathrm{op}})\,\right\}_{s\in S},

where
\Omega_{S,s}\colon\Bbbk\to H_{s}(S)\otimes_{\Bbbk}H_{\mathrm{pred}(s)}(S^{\mathrm{op}})
is the inverse pairing of
\omega_{S,s}
. A computation similar to
[
TVi4
, Lemma 4.8]
shows that

(19)

\ast_{S,s}=\;\,\psfrag\raisebox{-0.35pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{compute-inverse-s-1.eps}}}\;\,\otimes_{\Bbbk}\;\,\raisebox{-0.35pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{compute-inverse-s-2.eps}}}\;,

where
e=\beta(s)\in E
and the arcs are colored and oriented according to the maps
c\colon S\to\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
and
\varepsilon\colon S\to\{+,-\}
.

5.2.
Graphs

By a
graph
we mean a topological space
G
obtained from a finite number of disjoint copies of the closed interval
[0,1]
by identification of certain endpoints. The images of the copies of
[0,1]
in
G
are called
edges
of
G
. The endpoints
of the edges of
G
(that is, the images of
0,1\in[0,1]
) are called
vertices
of
G
. Each edge of
G

connects two (possibly, coinciding) vertices, and each vertex of
G
is incident to at least one edge. By
half-edges
of
G
, we mean the images of the closed intervals
[0,1/2]\subset[0,1]
and
[1/2,1]\subset[0,1]
in
G
. The number of half-edges of
G
incident to a vertex
v
of
G
is greater than or equal to
1
and is called the
valence
of
v
.

A graph is
oriented
if all its edges are oriented. The empty set is viewed as an oriented graph with no vertices and no edges.

5.3.
\chi
-graphs

Let
\Sigma
be an oriented surface. By a
graph
in
\Sigma
, we mean a graph embedded in
\Sigma
. A vertex
v
of a graph
G
in
\Sigma
determines a totally cyclically ordered set
G_{v}
consisting of the half-edges of
G
incident to
v
with cyclic order induced by the opposite orientation of
\Sigma
. If
G
is oriented, then we have a map
\varepsilon_{v}\colon G_{v}\to\{+,-\}
assigning
+
to the half-edges oriented towards
v
and
-
to the half-edges oriented away from
v
.

A
\chi
-graph
in
\Sigma
is an oriented graph
G
in
\Sigma
whose every edge is labeled with an element of
H
, called
H
-label
, and every half-edge is labeled with an element of
E
, called
E
-label
, in such a way that, for every vertex
v
of
G
, the cyclically ordered set
G_{v}
together with the maps
\alpha_{v}\colon G_{v}\to H
,
\beta_{v}\colon G_{v}\to E
induced by the labels and the map
\varepsilon_{v}\colon G_{v}\to\{+,-\}
defined above is a
\chi
-cyclic set. (Here, the
H
-label of a half-edge is the
H
-label of the edge containing it.) Explicitly, this means that for any vertex
v
of
G
and any half-edge
s_{1}
of
G
incident to
v
,

\chi(\beta_{v}(s_{1}))=\alpha_{v}(s_{1})^{\varepsilon_{v}(s_{1})}\cdots\alpha_{v}(s_{n})^{\varepsilon_{v}(s_{n})}\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{v}(s_{2})={\vphantom{{\!\beta_{v}(s_{1})}}}^{\left(\alpha_{v}(s_{1})^{-\varepsilon_{v}(s_{1})}\right)}{\!\beta_{v}(s_{1})},

where
s_{1}<s_{2}<\dots<s_{n}
are the half-edges of
G
incident to
v
ordered by the opposite orientation of
\Sigma

starting from
s_{1}
:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{Xi-graph-axiom.eps}}}\;.

When depicting a
\chi
-graph
G
in
\Sigma
, we draw the
H
-label of an edge next to it. By
(
17
), the
E
-labels of the half-edges incident to a vertex
v
are fully determined by the
E
-label of one half-edge incident to
v
. Now a dot next to
v
together with the orientation of
\Sigma
determine a half-edge incident to
v
: it is the first half-edge encountered while traversing from the dot a small loop negatively encircling
v
. Thus we draw next to each vertex of
G
a dot and the
E
-label of the half-edge determined by this dot. For example, consider the following trivalent vertex
v
of a
\chi
-graph
G
in an oriented surface:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{ijk-coln.eps}}}\;.

Here
h,k,\ell
are the
H
-labels of the edges incident to
v
, and
e
is the
E
-label of the half-edge determined by the dot, that is, of the half-edge labeled by
k
. Condition (
16
) reduces to
\chi(e)=k^{-1}\ell^{-1}h
. By (
17
), the
E
-labels of the half-edges labeled by
\ell
and
h
are
{\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}{}^{k}{\hskip-0.80002pte}
and
{\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}{}^{\ell k}{\hskip-0.80002pte}
, respectively.

5.4.
Grade of planar
\chi
-graphs

We always orient the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
counterclockwise. Let
G
be a
\chi
-graph in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Pick a point
m
in the unbounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G
.
For each vertex
v
of
G
, pick a small loop
\ell_{v}
negatively (i.e. clockwisely) encircling
v
and an arc
\gamma_{v}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

starting at
m
, ending in a point of
\ell_{v}\setminus G
, avoiding the vertices of
G
, and intersecting the edges of
G
transversally. We require that the arcs
\{\gamma_{v}\}_{v}
intersect each other only at their initial point
m
.
Denote by
e_{v}\in E
the
E
-label of the first encountered half-edge incident to
v
while traversing
\ell_{v}
from the endpoint of
\gamma_{v}
.
We assign an element
h_{v}\in H
to each arc
\gamma_{v}
as follows. Start with
h_{v}=1_{H}
. Go through the arc
\gamma_{v}
starting from
m
. Each time
\gamma_{v}
intersects an edge
s
of
G
at some point
c
, replace
h_{v}
by
h_{v}x^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon=+
if
(d_{c}s,d_{c}\gamma_{v})
is a positively oriented basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\varepsilon=-
otherwise, and
x
is the
H
-label of the edge
s
. Denote by
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
the vertices of
G

so that
\gamma_{v_{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v_{k}}
are the arcs successively encountered while traversing a small loop negatively encircling
m
(starting from any point on that loop).
We define the
grade
of
G
by

|G|={\vphantom{{e_{v_{1}}}}}^{(h_{v_{1}})}{e_{v_{1}}}\cdots{\vphantom{{e_{v_{k}}}}}^{(h_{v_{k}})}{e_{v_{k}}}\in E.

For example, consider the following
\chi
-graph in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag G=\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{exa-grade-1b.eps}}}\,\;.

Here
x,y,z,r,s,t
are the
H
-labels of the edges and
e,f,g,k
are the
E
-labels of the half-edges determined by the dots (with the graphical convention of Section
5.3
).
The conditions for
G
to be a
\chi
-graph reduce to

\chi(e)=y^{-1}rx^{-1},\quad\chi(f)=z^{-1}s^{-1}y,\quad\chi(g)=xt^{-1}z,\quad\chi(k)=tr^{-1}s.

Pick the following point
m
, loops
\{\ell_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
, and arcs
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{exa-grade-2nb.eps}}}\,\;.

For any
1\leq i\leq 4
, denote by
e_{i}
the element of
E
associated with the vertex encircled by
\ell_{i}
and by
h_{i}
the element of
H
associated to the arc
\gamma_{i}
as described above. Using (
17
), we obtain:

\displaystyle e_{1}=e,

\displaystyle e_{2}={\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{y}{\!f},

\displaystyle e_{3}={\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002ptk}}}^{t^{-1}}{\hskip-0.80002ptk},

\displaystyle e_{4}={\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002ptg}}}^{z}{\hskip-0.80002ptg},

\displaystyle h_{1}=1,

\displaystyle h_{2}=xr^{-1},

\displaystyle h_{3}=x,

\displaystyle h_{4}=xt^{-1}.

Then the grade of
G
is

|G|=({\vphantom{{e}}}^{1}{e})({\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{xr^{-1}y}{\!f})({\vphantom{{\!k}}}^{xt^{-1}}{\!k})({\vphantom{{g}}}^{xt^{-1}z}{g})=e({\vphantom{{\!f}}}^{\chi(e^{-1})}{\!f})({\vphantom{{\!k}}}^{\chi(g)z^{-1}}{\!k})({\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002ptg}}}^{\chi(g)}{\hskip-0.80002ptg})=feg({\vphantom{{\!k}}}^{z^{-1}}{\!k})

where the last equality follows from (
2
). Note that this final expression for
|G|
corresponds to the following choice of loops and arcs:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{exa-grade-3nb.eps}}}\,\;.

Lemma 5.1
.

The grade
|G|
is well-defined and
|G|\in\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
.

In particular,
|G|
is a central element of
E
(because
\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
is central in
E
). Also, the grade is multiplicative with respect to the disjoint union: if
G
and
G^{\prime}
are
\chi
-graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
lying in disjoint disks, then
|G\amalg G^{\prime}|=|G||G^{\prime}|
.

Proof.

We first prove that, for any choice (as above) of the arcs
\gamma_{v_{1}},\dots,\gamma_{v_{k}}
, we have
|G|\in\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
.
Denote by
G_{0}
the set of vertices of
G
.
To any oriented arc
\lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G_{0}
intersecting the edges of
G
transversally, we associate an element
\phi(\lambda)\in H
as follows. Start with
\phi(\lambda)=1\in H
. Go through the arc
\lambda
starting from the initial point of
\lambda
. Each time
\lambda
intersects an edge
s
of
G
at some point
c
, replace
\phi(\lambda)
by
\phi(\lambda)x^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon=+
if
(d_{c}s,d_{c}\gamma)
is a positively oriented basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\varepsilon=-
otherwise, and
x
is the
H
-label of the edge
s
. In particular
\phi(\lambda)=1
if
\lambda
does not intersect any edge of
G
. Clearly,
\phi
is multiplicative with respect to the concatenation of arcs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G_{0}
:
\phi(\lambda\mu)=\phi(\lambda)\phi(\mu)
if the endpoint of
\lambda
is the starting point of
\mu
. Moreover, if
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are homotopic (relative endpoints) in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G_{0}
, then
\phi(\lambda)=\phi(\lambda^{\prime})
. For example, for any vertex
v
of
G
, we have
\phi(\gamma_{v})=h_{v}
(by definition of
h_{v}
). Also, viewing the loop
\ell_{v}
as an oriented arc with starting point the endpoint of
\gamma_{v}
, it follows from
(
16
)
that
\phi(\ell_{v})=\chi(e_{v})
. Then

\displaystyle\chi(|G|)\overset{(i)}{=}\chi\left({\vphantom{{e_{v_{1}}}}}{}^{(h_{v_{1}})}{e_{v_{1}}}\cdots{\vphantom{{e_{v_{k}}}}}^{(h_{v_{k}})}{e_{v_{k}}}\right)\overset{(ii)}{=}h_{v_{1}}\chi(e_{v_{1}})h_{v_{1}}^{-1}\cdots h_{v_{k}}\chi(e_{v_{k}})h_{v_{k}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{=}\phi(\gamma_{v_{1}})\phi(\ell_{v_{1}})\phi(\gamma_{v_{1}})^{-1}\cdots\phi(\gamma_{v_{k}})\phi(\ell_{v_{k}})\phi(\gamma_{v_{k}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{=}\phi\bigl(\gamma_{v_{1}}\ell_{v_{1}}\gamma_{v_{1}}^{-1}\cdots\gamma_{v_{k}}\ell_{v_{k}}\gamma_{v_{k}}^{-1}\bigr)\overset{(v)}{=}1.

Here
(i)
follows from the definition of
|G|
,
(ii)
from the multiplicativity of
\chi
and (
1
),
(iii)
from
the fact that
h_{v}=\phi(\gamma_{v})
and
\chi(e_{v})=\phi(\ell_{v})
for any vertex
v
of
G
,
(iv)
from the multiplicativity of
\phi
with respect to the concatenation of arcs, and
(v)
from the fact that the concatenated arc
\gamma_{v_{1}}\ell_{v_{1}}\gamma_{v_{1}}^{-1}\cdots\gamma_{v_{k}}\ell_{v_{k}}\gamma_{v_{k}}^{-1}
is homotopic (relative endpoints) in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G_{0}
to an arc not intersecting the edges of
G
(namely, to the boundary of a disk containing
m
in its boundary and
G
in its interior).

Next,
|G|
is independent of the choice of the starting point on the small loop negatively encircling
m
used to enumerate the vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
of
G
. This follows from the fact that for any
1\leq r\leq k
,

{\vphantom{{e_{v_{r}}}}}{}^{(h_{v_{r}})}{e_{v_{r}}}\cdots{\vphantom{{e_{v_{k}}}}}^{(h_{v_{k}})}{e_{v_{k}}}{\vphantom{{e_{v_{1}}}}}^{(h_{v_{1}})}{e_{v_{1}}}\cdots{\vphantom{{e_{v_{r-1}}}}}^{(h_{v_{r-1}})}{e_{v_{r-1}}}\overset{(i)}{=}w_{r}^{-1}|G|w_{r}\overset{(ii)}{=}|G|,

where
w_{r}={\vphantom{{e_{v_{1}}}}}^{(h_{v_{1}})}{e_{v_{1}}}\cdots{\vphantom{{e_{v_{r-1}}}}}^{(h_{v_{r-1}})}{e_{v_{r-1}}}\in E
. Here
(i)
follows from the definition of
|G|
and
(ii)
from the fact that
|G|
is central in
E
(since
|G|\in\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
).

Let us prove the invariance of
|G|
on the choice of the system of arcs
\{\gamma_{v}\}_{v\in G_{0}}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k})
where
\gamma_{i}=\gamma_{v_{i}}
. Any two such systems of arcs are related by a finite sequence of homotopies (relative endpoints) in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G_{0}
and of the following moves:

(a)

local move around a vertex
v
of
G
:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{grade-local-move.eps}}}\,\;.

(b)

global exchange move for
1\leq i<k
:

which replaces the arcs
(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i+1})
by the arcs
(\gamma^{\prime}_{i},\gamma^{\prime}_{i+1})

where
\gamma^{\prime}_{i+1}=\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
is the concantenation
\gamma_{i}\ell_{i}\gamma_{i}^{-1}\gamma_{i+1}
(sligthly homotoped so that it becomes disjoint from
\gamma_{i}
except at
m
).

Invariance of
|G|
under homotopies (relative endpoints) in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G_{0}
of the arcs
\gamma_{v}
follows directly from the invariance of the elements
h_{v}\in H
and
e_{v}\in E
under such homotopies. To prove the invariance under the move (a), denote by
h^{\prime}_{v}\in H
and
e^{\prime}_{v}\in E
the elements associated (as above) to
\gamma^{\prime}_{v}
.
Set
\varepsilon=+
if the edge labeled with
x\in H
is oriented towards
v
and
\varepsilon=-
otherwise. It follows directly from the definitions that
h^{\prime}_{v}=h_{v}x^{-\varepsilon}
. Also
e_{v}={\vphantom{{\!e^{\prime}_{v}}}}^{x^{-\varepsilon}}{\!e^{\prime}_{v}}
by (
17
), and so

e^{\prime}_{v}={\vphantom{{\!e_{v}}}}^{x^{\varepsilon}}{\!e_{v}}
. Then

{\vphantom{{e^{\prime}_{v}}}}{}^{h^{\prime}_{v}}{e^{\prime}_{v}}={\vphantom{{\!e_{v}}}}^{h_{v}x^{-\varepsilon}x^{\varepsilon}}{\!e_{v}}={\vphantom{{e_{v}}}}^{h_{v}}{e_{v}}.

This proves the invariance of
|G|
under the move (a). To prove the invariance under the move (b), denote by
h_{r}\in H
,
e_{r}\in E
the elements associated (as above) to
\gamma_{r}
and by
h^{\prime}_{r}\in H
,
e^{\prime}_{r}\in E
those associated to
\gamma^{\prime}_{r}
for
r\in\{i,i+1\}
. Clearly,
h^{\prime}_{i+1}=h_{i}
,
e^{\prime}_{i+1}=e_{i}
, and
e^{\prime}_{i}=e_{i+1}
. It follows from (
16
) that
h^{\prime}_{i}=h_{i}\chi(e_{i})h_{i}^{-1}h_{i+1}
, and so
h^{\prime}_{i}=\chi({\vphantom{{e_{i}}}}^{h_{i}}{e_{i}})h_{i+1}
by (
1
). Then, using (
2
), we obtain

{\vphantom{{e^{\prime}_{i}}}}{}^{h^{\prime}_{i}}{e^{\prime}_{i}}\,{\vphantom{{e^{\prime}_{i+1}}}}^{h^{\prime}_{i+1}}{e^{\prime}_{i+1}}={\vphantom{{e_{i+1}}}}^{\chi({\vphantom{{e_{i}}}}^{h_{i}}{e_{i}})h_{i+1}}{e_{i+1}}\,{\vphantom{{e_{i}}}}^{h_{i}}{e_{i}}={\vphantom{{e_{i}}}}^{h_{i}}{e_{i}}\,{\vphantom{{e_{i+1}}}}^{h_{i+1}}{e_{i+1}}.

This proves the invariance of
|G|
under the move (b).

Finally, the invariance of
|G|
on the choice of the point
m
in the unbounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus G
follows from the fact that this component is path connected.
∎

5.5.
Colored
\chi
-graphs

Let
\Sigma
be an oriented surface. A
\chi
-graph in
\Sigma
is
\mathcal{C}
-colored
if each edge is endowed with a homogeneous object of
\mathcal{C}
, called its
\mathcal{C}
-color
, whose degree is the
H
-label of this edge.

Let
G\subset\Sigma
be a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph. The
\chi
-cyclic set
G_{v}
associated with a vertex
v
of
G
is
\mathcal{C}
-colored by the map
c_{v}\colon G_{v}\to\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
assigning to each half-edge the
\mathcal{C}
-color of the edge containing it. Let

H_{v}(G)=H(G_{v})

be the multiplicity module of the
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set
G_{v}
. It can be described as follows. Let
n\geq 1
be the valence of
v
and let
s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{n}<s_{1}
be the half-edges of
G
incident to
v
with cyclic order induced by the opposite orientation of
\Sigma
. Let
X_{r}=c_{v}(s_{r})
,
e_{r}=\beta(s_{r})
, and
\varepsilon_{r}=\varepsilon_{v}(s_{r})
be the
\mathcal{C}
-color, the
E
-label, and the sign of
s_{r}
, respectively. Then we have the cone isomorphism

\tau^{v}_{s_{1}}\colon H_{v}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e_{1}}(\mathbb{1},X_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n}^{\varepsilon_{n}}).

By the definition of
H_{v}(G)
, the cone isomorphisms determined by different elements of
G_{v}
are related via composition with the permutation maps (
18
). We set

H(G)=\otimes_{v}\,H_{v}(G),

where
v
runs over all vertices of
G
and
\otimes
is the unordered tensor product of
\Bbbk
-modules. To
emphasize the role of
\Sigma
, we sometimes write
H_{v}(G;\Sigma)
for
H_{v}(G)
and
H(G;\Sigma)
for
H(G)
. By definition, for
G=\emptyset
, we have
H(G)=\Bbbk
.

Any orientation preserving embedding
f
of
\Sigma
into an oriented surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
carries a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G\subset\Sigma
into a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G^{\prime}=f(G)\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
preserving the vertices, the edges, the
E
-labels of the half-edges, and the orientations,
H
-labels, and
\mathcal{C}
-colors of the edges. The map
f
induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism

H(f)\colon H(G;\Sigma)\to H(G^{\prime};\Sigma^{\prime})

in the obvious way. This applies, in particular, when
f
is an orientation preserving self-homeomorphism of
\Sigma
.

Given
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
, by an
isotopy
of
G
to
G^{\prime}
, we mean an ambient isotopy of
G
to
G^{\prime}
in
\Sigma
preserving the vertices, the edges, the
E
-labels of the half-edges, and the orientations,
H
-labels, and
\mathcal{C}
-colors of the edges. Such an isotopy
\iota
induces an orientation preserving homeomorphism
\Sigma\to\Sigma
carrying
G
to
G^{\prime}
. This homeomorphism induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
H(G)\to H(G^{\prime})
denoted
H(\iota)
.

Let
-\Sigma
be
\Sigma
with opposite orientation.
The
dual
of
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in
\Sigma
is the
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G^{\mathrm{op}}
in
-\Sigma
obtained from
G
by reversing the orientation of all edges, keeping the
H
-labels and
\mathcal{C}
-colors of the edges, and defining the
E
-labels of half-edges of
G^{\mathrm{op}}
as follows: the
E
-label of a half-edge
\ell
of
G^{\mathrm{op}}
incident to a vertex
v
is the inverse of the
E
-label of the successor of
\ell
in
G_{v}
. Observe that
(G^{\mathrm{op}})_{v}=(G_{v})^{\mathrm{op}}
for any vertex
v
of
G
, where
(G_{v})^{\mathrm{op}}
is the dual of
G_{v}
in the sense of Section
5.1
. If
\mathcal{C}
is spherical pre-fusion, then the (unordered) tensor product of the non-degenerate
\Bbbk
-bilinear pairings

\omega_{G_{v}}\colon H(G_{v}^{\mathrm{op}})\otimes H(G_{v})\to\Bbbk
(see Section
5.1
)
over all vertices
v
of
G
yields a non-degenerate
\Bbbk
-bilinear pairing

H(G^{\mathrm{op}})\otimes H(G)\to\Bbbk
,
and so these pairings induce canonical
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphisms

H_{v}(G^{\mathrm{op}})\simeq H_{v}(G)^{\star}\quad{\text{and}}\quad H(G^{\mathrm{op}})\simeq H(G)^{\star}.

Here we denote by
N^{\star}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(N,\Bbbk)
the dual of a
\Bbbk
-module
N
.

5.6.
An invariant of planar
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs

Let
G
be a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For
each vertex
v
of
G
, pick a half-edge
s_{v}
incident to
v
and deform
G
near
v
so that the half-edges incident to
v
lie above
v
with respect to the second coordinate of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
s_{v}
is the leftmost of them. Pick any
\alpha_{v}\in H_{v}(G)
and replace
v
by a box colored with
\tau^{v}_{s_{v}}(\alpha_{v})
, where
\tau^{v}
is the universal cone of
H_{v}(G)
:

.

This transforms
G
into a diagram without free ends. Let
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)(\otimes_{v}\alpha_{v})\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{1})
be the associated morphism computed via the Penrose graphical calculus. Since the (co)evaluations and the morphisms
\tau^{v}_{s_{v}}(\alpha_{v})
are homogeneous morphisms, so is
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)(\otimes_{v}\alpha_{v})
. It follows from the axioms of a pivotal
\chi
-category and of the definition of
H_{v}(G)
that the homogeneous morphism
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)(\otimes_{v}\alpha_{v})
is independent of the choice of the half-edges
s_{v}
and its degree is the grade
|G|\in\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
of
G
. Then
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)(\otimes_{v}\alpha_{v})\in\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{|G|}(\mathbb{1})
. This extends by linearity to a
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphism

\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\colon H(G)=\otimes_{v}\,H_{v}(G)\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{|G|}(\mathbb{1}).

For
G=\emptyset
, we have
|G|=1
and the map
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\colon H(G)=\Bbbk\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})
is the
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphism carrying
1_{\Bbbk}
to
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{1}}
.

The homomorphism
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\colon H(G)\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{|G|}(\mathbb{1})
is a well-defined isotopy invariant of the
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. More precisely, for any isotopy
\iota
between
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
we have
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime})\,H(\iota)=\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)
, where
H(\iota)\colon H(G)\to H(G^{\prime})
is the
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism induced by
\iota
. The invariance under isotopies follows from the isotopy invariance of the Penrose graphical calculus.

We state a few simple properties of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
:

(i)

(Naturality) If a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is obtained from a

\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
through the replacement of the
\mathcal{C}
-color
X
of an edge by a 1-isomorphic object
X^{\prime}
, then any 1-isomorphism
\phi\colon X\to X^{\prime}
induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
\Phi\colon H(G^{\prime})\to H(G)
and
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime})=\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\,\Phi
.

(ii)

If a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is obtained from a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

by reversing the orientation of an edge and replacing the
\mathcal{C}
-color of this edge by the dual object of
\mathcal{C}
, then
the pivotal structure of
\mathcal{C}
induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
\Psi\colon H(G^{\prime})\to H(G)
and
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime})=\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\,\Psi
.

(iii)

If an edge
e
of a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is colored with
\mathbb{1}
and the endpoints of
e
are also endpoints of other edges of
G
, then
G^{\prime}=G\setminus\mathrm{Int}(e)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
inherits from
G
the structure of a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph, there is a canonical
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
\Delta\colon H(G^{\prime})\to H(G)
, and
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime})=\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\,\Delta
.

(iv)

(
\otimes
-multiplicativity) If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are disjoint
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
lying on different sides of a straight line, then there is a canonical
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
\Theta\colon H(G\amalg G^{\prime})\to H(G)\otimes H(G^{\prime})
and

\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G\amalg G^{\prime})=\mu\bigl(\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G^{\prime})\bigr)\Theta,

where
\mu
is the multiplication of the
\Bbbk
-algebra
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{1})
.

5.7.
Example

Consider the following
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with two vertices and two edges
H
-labeled with
h,k\in H
and
\mathcal{C}
-colored with homogenous objects
X,Y
:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag G=\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-exa-col.eps}}}\,.

There are 3 half-edges
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
incident to the leftmost vertex
u
and one half-edge
t
incident to rightmost vertex
v
:

s_{1}=\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-ex-ed1-col.eps}}}\,,\quad s_{2}=\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-ex-ed2-col.eps}}}\,,\quad s_{3}=\,\raisebox{-0.2pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-ex-ed3-col.eps}}}\;,\quad t=\,\raisebox{-0.2pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-ex-ed4-col.eps}}}\;.

The cyclic order on
G_{u}=\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}
is
s_{1}<s_{2}<s_{3}<s_{1}
. By the convention of Section
5.4
, the elements
e
and
f
of
E
are the
E
-labels of
s_{2}
and
t
, respectively. The conditions for
G
to be a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph reduce to

\chi(e)=h^{-1}k^{-1}h,\quad\chi(f)=k,\quad|X|=h,\quad\text{and}\quad|Y|=k.

The degree of
G
is
|G|={\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}^{h}{\hskip-0.80002pte}f
. By (
17
), the
E
-labels of the half-edges
s_{1}
and
s_{3}
are
{\vphantom{{e}}}{}^{h}{e}
and
{\vphantom{{e}}}{}^{kh}{e}
, respectively.
The cone isomorphisms associated with the half-edges are:

\displaystyle\tau^{u}_{s_{1}}\colon H_{u}(G)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{{\vphantom{{\!e}}}{}^{h}{\!e}}(\mathbb{1},X\otimes X^{*}\otimes Y^{*}),

\displaystyle\tau^{u}_{s_{2}}\colon H_{u}(G)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},X^{*}\otimes Y^{*}\otimes X),

\displaystyle\tau^{u}_{s_{3}}\colon H_{u}(G)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{{\vphantom{{\!e}}}{}^{kh}{\!e}}(\mathbb{1},Y^{*}\otimes X\otimes X^{*}),

\displaystyle\tau^{v}_{t}\colon H_{v}(G)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(\mathbb{1},Y).

The first three isomorphisms are related to each other via composition with the permutation maps (
18
). For instance, for any
\alpha\in H_{u}(G)
,

\tau^{u}_{s_{2}}(\alpha)=\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.39pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-exe-col.eps}}}\;.

By definition,
H(G)=H_{u}(G)\otimes H_{v}(G)
. For any
\alpha\in H_{u}(G)
and
\beta\in H_{v}(G)
,

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)(\alpha\otimes\beta)\,=\,\psfrag\raisebox{-0.39pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-exb-col.eps}}}\,=\,\psfrag\raisebox{-0.39pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-exc-col.eps}}}\,=\,\psfrag\raisebox{-0.39pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-R2-exd-col.eps}}}\;.

5.8.
The spherical case

Consider the 2-sphere
S^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\cup\{\infty\}
endowed with the orientation extending the
counterclockwise orientation in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We say that a
\chi
-graph
G
in
S^{2}
is
1-spherical
if when pushing
G
away from
\infty
, we obtain a
\chi
-graph
G_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with trivial grade.
Note that this notion does not depend on the way
G
is pushed away from
\infty
. Indeed, if
G^{\prime}_{0}
is another
\chi
-graph in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
obtained by pushing
G
away from
\infty
, then it follows from Section
5.4
that
|G^{\prime}_{0}|={\vphantom{{|G_{0}|}}}^{x}{|G_{0}|}
for some
x\in H
, and so
|G^{\prime}_{0}|=1
if and only
|G_{0}|=1
.

Assume
\mathcal{C}
is spherical (see Section
4.9
).
Then the invariant
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
of
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
induces an isotopy invariant of 1-spherical
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs in
S^{2}
. Indeed, consider a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in
S^{2}
. Pushing
G
away from
\infty
by an isotopy, we obtain a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The isotopy induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
H(G;S^{2})\simeq H(G_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Composing with
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G_{0})\colon H(G_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1})
we obtain a
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphism

\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)\colon H(G;S^{2})\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1}).

The sphericity of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
ensures that
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)
is a well-defined isotopy invariant of
G
. The properties of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
formulated in Section
5.6
extend to 1-spherical
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs in
S^{2}
in the obvious way. The condition in (iv) involving a straight line may be dropped here because for any pair of disjoint connected graphs in
S^{2}
, there is an isotopy of
S^{2}
in itself carrying these graphs into new positions in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
separated by a line.

The invariant
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
further extends to 1-spherical
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs in an arbitrary oriented surface
\Sigma
homeomorphic to
S^{2}
. Given a 1-spherical
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in
\Sigma
, pick an orientation preserving homeomorphism
f\colon\Sigma\to S^{2}
and set

\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)=\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(f(G))\,H(f)\colon H(G;\Sigma)\to\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{1}).

Since all orientation preserving homeomorphisms
\Sigma\to S^{2}
are isotopic, the homomorphism
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(G)
does not depend on the choice of
f
.

5.9.
Dual vertices

Consider a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G
in an oriented surface
\Sigma
and a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
G^{\prime}
in an oriented surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
By a
duality
between a vertex
u
of
G
and a vertex
v
of
G^{\prime}
, we mean an isomorphism of
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-sets
\phi\colon G_{u}^{\mathrm{op}}\to G_{v}^{\prime}
, where
G_{u}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the dual of
G_{u}
(see Section
5.1
).
When
\mathcal{C}
is spherical pre-fusion, the
contraction vector
of a duality
\phi\colon G_{u}^{\mathrm{op}}\to G_{v}^{\prime}
is the vector

\ast_{\phi}=\bigl(\mathrm{id}_{H(G_{u})}\otimes H(\phi)\bigr)(\ast_{G_{u}})\in H(G_{u})\otimes H(G_{v}^{\prime})=H_{u}(G)\otimes H_{v}(G^{\prime}),

where

H(\phi)\colon H(G_{u}^{\mathrm{op}})\to H(G_{v}^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad\ast_{G_{u}}\in H(G_{u})\otimes H(G_{u}^{\mathrm{op}})

are respectively the
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism induced by
\phi
and the contraction vector of the
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set
G_{u}
(see Section
5.1
).
Clearly,
\ast_{\phi}=\ast_{\phi^{-1}}
.

The next lemma formulates a local relation for the invariant
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
of
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
involving dual vertices and contraction vectors.

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is spherical pre-fusion and let
I
be a
\chi
-representative set for
\mathcal{C}
. The pictures below stands for a pieces of
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The orientation of the edges
\mathcal{C}
-colored by the object
X_{i}
should match in both sides of the equality, and let
\varepsilon_{i}=+
if this orientation is downwards and
\varepsilon_{i}=-
otherwise.
Set
h=|X_{1}|^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots|X_{n}|^{\varepsilon_{n}}\in H
.
Then, for all
e\in E
,

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\ \raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-graph-prefusion-col-1.eps}}}\,\right)=\sum_{i\in I_{h\chi(e^{-1})}}\dim(i)\;\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-graph-prefusion-col-2.eps}}}\,\right)(\ast).

Here,
e^{\pm 1}
are the
E
-labels of the half-edges determined by the points (with the graphical convention of Section
5.3
) and
\ast
is the contraction vector provided by the duality between the two vertices induced by the symmetry with respect to a horizontal line.

Proof.

We only need to compare the contributions to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
of the depicted pieces of
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graphs. The contribution of the left-hand piece is
\mathrm{id}_{X}
where
X=X_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n}^{\varepsilon_{n}}
.
Using the definitions of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\ast
, Formula (
19
), and considering the isotopy

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-graph-prefusion-3dcol.eps}}}\,\;\simeq\;\,\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{inv-graph-prefusion-3ccol.eps}}}\;,

we obtain that the contribution of the right-hand piece is equal to

\displaystyle\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\sum_{i\in I_{g}}\dim(i)\!\!\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-contract-i-4-col.eps}}}\;\overset{(i)}{=}\;\sum_{i\in I_{g}}\dim(i)\;\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-contract-i-5-col.eps}}}\;\overset{(ii)}{=}\;\psfrag\sum_{i\in I_{g}}\;\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{tensor-contract-i-6-col.eps}}}\;\overset{(iii)}{=}\,\mathrm{id}_{X}

where
g=h\chi(e^{-1})
.
Here
(i)
follows from the isotopy invariance of the graphical calculus,
(ii)
from Lemma
4.2
, and
(iii)
from (
13
) and the fact that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{{\vphantom{{\hskip-0.65556pte}}}{}^{g}{\hskip-0.65556pte}}(i,X)=0
for all
i\in I\setminus I_{g}
(since
X
is a 1-direct sum of homogenous objects of degree
h
).
∎

6.
Skeletons and maps

Throughout this section,
\chi\colon E\to H
is a crossed module and
B\chi
is the classifying space of
\chi
(see Section
2.4
). We first recall the notions of stratified 2-polyhedra and skeletons of 3-manifolds (referring to
[
TVi4
]
for details). Then we explain how to encode maps from closed 3-manifolds to
B\chi
in terms of labelings of skeletons.

6.1.
Stratified 2-polyhedra

A
2-polyhedron
is a compact topological space that can be triangulated using a finite number of simplices of dimension
\leq 2
so that all
0
-simplices and
1
-simplices are faces of
2
-simplices. The interior
\mathrm{Int}(P)
of a
2
-polyhedron
P
consists of all points which have a neighborhood homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By the definition of a 2-polyhedron, the surface
\mathrm{Int}(P)
is dense in
P
.

A
stratified polyhedron
is a
2
-polyhedron
P
endowed with a graph
P^{(1)}
embedded in
P
such that
P\setminus\mathrm{Int}(P)\subset P^{(1)}
. The vertices and edges of
P^{(1)}
are called respectively the
vertices
and
edges
of
P
. Any 2-polyhedron
P
can be stratified. For example, the 0-simplices and the 1-simplices of a triangulation of
P
form a graph which is a stratification of
P
. Any (possibly, empty) graph embedded in a compact surface without boundary is a stratification of this surface.

An
orientation
of a stratified 2-polyhedron
P
is an orientation of the surface
P\setminus P^{(1)}
. A stratified 2-polyhedron is
oriented
if it is endowed with an orientation. Cutting a stratified 2-polyhedron
P
along the graph
P^{(1)}\subset P
, we obtain a compact surface
\widetilde{P}
with interior
P\setminus P^{(1)}
.
The connected components of
\widetilde{P}
are called the
regions
of
P
. Each component of
P\setminus P^{(1)}\subset\widetilde{P}
is the interior of a unique region. The set
\mathrm{Reg}(P)
of the regions of
P
is finite. To orient
P
, one must orient all its regions.

A
branch
of a stratified 2-polyhedron
P
at a vertex
v
of
P
is a germ at
v
of an adjacent region.
Similarly, a
branch
of
P
at an edge
e
of
P
is a germ at
e
of an adjacent region.
The set of branches of
P
at
e
is denoted
P_{e}
. This set is finite and
non-empty. The number of elements of
P_{e}
is called the
valence
of
e
.

The edges of a stratified 2-polyhedron
P
of valence 1 together with their vertices form a graph called the

boundary
of
P
and denoted
\partial P
. An orientation of
P
induces an orientation of
\partial P
: each edge
e
of
\partial P
is oriented so that the orientation of the
unique region of
P
adjacent to
e
is determined by a pair (a vector directed outward at an interior point of
e
, a positive tangent vector of
e
).

6.2.
Skeletons of closed 3-manifolds

A
skeleton
of a closed oriented
(possibly, non-connected) 3-dimensional manifold
M
is an oriented stratified 2-polyhedron
P\subset M
such that
\partial P=\emptyset
and
M\setminus P
is a disjoint union of open 3-balls. These open 3-balls are called
P
-balls
. The condition
\partial P=\emptyset
ensures that all edges of
P
have valence
\geq 2
.
An example of a skeleton of
M
is provided by the
(oriented) 2-skeleton
t^{(2)}
of a triangulation
t
of
M
, where
the edges of
t^{(2)}
are the edges of
t
.

Given a skeleton
P
of
M
, a
P
-ball branch
at an edge
e
of
P
is a germ at
e
of an adjacent
P
-ball.

Any vertex
x
of a skeleton
P
of
M
has a closed ball neighborhood
B_{x}\subset M
such that
\Gamma_{x}=P\cap\partial B_{x}
is a non-empty graph and
P\cap B_{x}
is the cone over
\Gamma_{x}
with summit
x
. The vertices of
\Gamma_{x}
are the intersection points of the 2-sphere
\partial B_{x}
with the edges of
P
incident to
x
.
Each edge of
\Gamma_{x}
is the intersection of
\partial B_{x}
with a branch
b
of
P
at
x
, and we endow this edge with the orientation induced by that of
b
restricted to
b\setminus\mathrm{Int}(B_{x})
.
We endow
\partial B_{x}\cong S^{2}
with the orientation induced by that of
M
restricted to
M\setminus\mathrm{Int}(B_{x})
. In this way,

\Gamma_{x}
becomes an oriented graph in the oriented 2-sphere
\partial B_{x}
. We call
B_{x}
a
P
-cone neighborhood
of
x
and call
\Gamma_{x}
the
link graph
of
x
. The pair
(B_{x},\Gamma_{x})
is determined by the triple
(M,P,x)
uniquely up to homeomorphism. Since all edges of
P
have valence
\geq 2
, so do all vertices of
\Gamma_{x}
.

Figure 1.
The link graph of a vertex

An example is given in Figure
1
where there are 10 branches of
P
at
x
, 6
P
-ball branches at
x
, and
\partial B_{x}
is identified with
S^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\cup\{\infty\}
via the stereographic projection from a pole in the upper left quarter.

6.3.
\chi
-labelings

Let
P
be a skeleton of an oriented closed 3-dimensional manifold
M
.
By an
oriented edge
of
P
, we mean an edge of
P
endowed with an orientation.
For each oriented edge
e
of
P
, the orientations of
e
and
M
determine a positive
direction on a small loop in
M
encircling
e
(so that the linking number of this loop with
e
is

+1
). The resulting oriented loop determines cyclic orders on the set
P_{e}
of branches of
P
at
e
and on the set

P^{\text{ball}}_{e}
of
P
-ball branches at
e
.
Any
P
-ball branch
B\in P^{\text{ball}}_{e}
determines a branch
b_{B}\in P_{e}
which is the first branch of
P
at
e
encountered while traversing the oriented loop starting from
B
. This induces a bijection
P^{\text{ball}}_{e}\to P_{e}
preserving the cyclic order.
In particular, any
P
-ball branch
B\in P^{\text{ball}}_{e}
turns the cyclic order on
P_{e}
into a linear order so that the first element is
b_{B}
.
To each branch
b\in P_{e}
we assign a sign equal to
+
if the orientation of
b
induces the one of
e\subset\partial b
(that is, the orientation of
b
is given by the orientation of
e
followed by a vector at a point of
e
directed inside
b
) and equal to
-
otherwise. This gives a map
\varepsilon_{e}\colon P_{e}\to\{+,-\}
. When orientation of
e
is reversed, the cyclic orders on
P_{e}
and
P^{\text{ball}}_{e}
are reversed and
\varepsilon_{e}
is multiplied by
-
.

Recall that
\mathrm{Reg}(P)
denotes the (finite) set of regions of
P
(see Section
6.1
).
Let
\mathrm{EB}(P)
be the set of pairs
(e,B)
where
e
is an oriented edge of
P
and
B
is a
P
-ball branch at
e
. Note that

\mathrm{EB}(P)=\bigcup_{e}\,\{e\}\times P_{e}^{\text{ball}}

where
e
runs over all oriented edges of
P
.
A
pre-
\chi
-labeling
of
P
is a pair

\bigl(\alpha\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to H,\beta\colon\mathrm{EB}(P)\to E\bigr)

of maps such that for any
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
,

(20)

\displaystyle\chi(\beta(e,B))

\displaystyle=\alpha(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{1})}\cdots\alpha(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{n})},

(21)

\displaystyle\beta(e,\mathrm{succ}(B))

\displaystyle={\vphantom{{\!\beta(e,B)}}}^{\left(\alpha(b_{1})^{-\varepsilon_{e}(b_{1})}\right)}{\!\beta(e,B)},

(22)

\displaystyle\beta(-e,B)

\displaystyle=\beta(e,B)^{-1},

where
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{n}
are the elements of
P_{e}
enumerated in the linear order determined by
B
,
\mathrm{succ}(B)
is the successor of
B
in the cyclic order on
P^{\text{ball}}_{e}
, and
-e
is
e
with opposite orientation:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{neighborhood-edge.eps}}}\;.

Here and in the sequel, for any branch
b
of
P
at a vertex or an edge of
P
, we let
\alpha(b)=\alpha(r_{b})
where
r_{b}
is the unique region of
P
containing
b
.
It follows from (
21
) that for any oriented edge
e
of
P
, the map
B\in P^{\text{ball}}_{e}\mapsto\beta(e,B)\in E
is fully determined by its value on one element of
P^{\text{ball}}_{e}
. Also, if (
20
) holds for one
P
-ball branch at
e
, then it holds for all
P
-ball branches at
e
. Similarly, (
22
) implies that if (
20
) or (
21
) holds for
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
, then it holds for
(-e,B)
.

A pre-
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
turns the set
P_{e}
of branches of
P
at an oriented edge
e
of
P
into a
\chi
-cyclic set
P_{e}=(P_{e},\alpha_{e},\beta_{e},\varepsilon_{e})
as follows. For any branch
b\in P_{e}
, set
\alpha_{e}(b)=\alpha(b)\in H
and
\beta_{e}(b)=\beta(e,B)\in E
, where
B
is the
P
-ball branch at
e
such that
b=b_{B}
(in the above notation).

A pre-
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
turns the link graph
\Gamma_{x}
of a vertex
x
of
P
into a
\chi
-graph in
\partial B_{x}\cong S^{2}
as follows, where
B_{x}
is a
P
-cone neighborhood of
x
(see Section
6.2
).
Each edge of
\Gamma_{x}
is the intersection of
\partial B_{x}
with a branch
b
of
P
at
x
and we endow this edge with the
H
-label
\alpha(b)\in H
. Each half-edge
h
of
\Gamma_{x}
is uniquely determined by an oriented edge
e
of
P
incident to
x
and a
P
-ball branch
B
at
e
. Indeed, the vertex
v
of
\Gamma_{x}
to which
h
is incident is the first intersection point of
\partial B_{x}
with
e
while traversing
e
following its orientation. The orientation of
e
and
M
determine as above a
branch
b_{B}
of
P
at
e
. The half-edge
h
is then the connected component containing
v
of the intersection of
\partial B_{x}
with
b_{B}
. We endow the half-edge
h
with the
E
-label
\beta(e,B)\in E
. It follows from (
20
)-(
22
) that these labels of (half-)edges make
\Gamma_{x}
a
\chi
-graph (see Section
5.3
).

A
\chi
-labeling
of
P
is a pre-
\chi
-labeling of
P
such that, for any vertex
x
of
P
, the
\chi
-graph
\Gamma_{x}
in
B_{x}\cong S^{2}
is 1-spherical (see Section
5.8
).

Let us define the
gauge group
of
\mathcal{G}_{P}
of
P
as follows. As a set,

\mathcal{G}_{P}=\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{Ball}(P),H)\times\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{Reg}(P),E)

where
\mathrm{Ball}(P)
is the set of
P
-balls. For any
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
, let
r_{\pm}
be the
P
-balls (possibly coinciding) adjacent to the region
r
and indexed so that the orientation of
r
followed by a normal vector pointing from
r_{-}
to
r_{+}

yields the given orientation of
M
. The product in
\mathcal{G}_{P}
is defined by

(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\cdot(\lambda,\mu)=(\lambda^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime})

with

\lambda^{\prime\prime}(B)=\lambda^{\prime}(B)\lambda(B)\quad\text{and}\quad\mu^{\prime\prime}(r)=\mu(r)\left({\vphantom{{\!\!\mu^{\prime}(r)}}}{}^{\lambda(r_{+})^{-1}}{\!\!\mu^{\prime}(r)}\right)

for any
P
-ball
B
and any region
r
of
P
. The group
\mathcal{G}_{P}
acts (on the left) on the set of
\chi
-labelings of
P
. To define this action we need some notations. Pick a point, called the
center
, in every
P
-ball. For each region
r
of
P
, pick an oriented arc
\gamma_{r}
in
M
connecting the center of
r_{-}
to the center of
r_{+}
and intersecting
r
transversely at a single point. We choose the arcs
\{\gamma_{r}\}_{r}
so that they intersect with each other only at their endpoints. For any branch
b
of
P
, we set
\gamma_{b}=\gamma_{r_{b}}
where
r_{b}
is the unique region of
P
containing
b
. Each
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
determines a loop
\gamma_{(e,B)}
in
M
based at the center of
B
defined by the concatenation

\gamma_{(e,B)}=\gamma_{b_{1}}^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{1})}\cdots\gamma_{b_{n}}^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{n})},

where
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{n}
are the elements of
P_{e}
enumerated in the linear order determined by
B
. For example, we have
\gamma_{(e,B)}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}
for the following trivalent oriented edge
e
and the
P
-ball branch
B
:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{neighborhood-edge-loop-new.eps}}}\;.

The action of
\mathcal{G}_{P}
on the set of
\chi
-labelings of
P
is then defined as follows. For any
(\lambda,\mu)\in\mathcal{G}_{P}
and any
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
, the
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\lambda,\mu)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
is defined by

(23)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle=\lambda(r_{-})\alpha(r)\chi(\mu(r))\lambda(r_{+})^{-1},

(24)

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(e,B)

\displaystyle={\vphantom{{\!{\big(\beta(e,B)\mu_{\alpha}(\gamma_{(e,B)})\big)}}}}^{\lambda(B)}{\!{\big(\beta(e,B)\mu_{\alpha}(\gamma_{(e,B)})\big)}},

for all
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
and
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
.
Here,
\mu_{\alpha}
extends
\mu
to the groupoid freely generated by the morphisms
\{\gamma_{r}\colon r_{-}\to r_{+}\}_{r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)}
via the following rules:

\mu_{\alpha}(\emptyset)=1
and for any region
r
of
P
and any morphism
\omega
in this groupoid,

\mu_{\alpha}(\omega\ast\gamma_{r})=\left({\vphantom{{\!\!\mu_{\alpha}(\omega)}}}{}^{\alpha(r)^{-1}}{\!\!\mu_{\alpha}(\omega)}\right)\!\mu(r)\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{\alpha}(\omega\ast\gamma_{r}^{-1})={\vphantom{{\!\left(\mu_{\alpha}(\omega)\mu(r)^{-1}\right)}}}^{\alpha(r)}{\!\left(\mu_{\alpha}(\omega)\mu(r)^{-1}\right)},

where the symbol
\ast
denotes the concatenation of the edges of a graph (which is opposite to the composition of morphisms in this groupoid). Two
\chi
-labelings of
P
are
gauge equivalent
if they belong to the same orbit under the action of the gauge group
\mathcal{G}_{P}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There is an injective
map from the set of gauge equivalence classes of
\chi
-labelings of
P
to the set of homotopy classes of maps
M\to B\chi
which is bijective when the regions of
P
are disks.

We prove Lemma
6.1
in Section
6.5
. The next example illustrates the lemma for lens spaces.

6.4.
Example

Consider the lens space
L(p,q)
where
p,q
are coprime integers with
1\leq q<p
.
It is a closed connected oriented 3-dimensional manifold obtained by gluing two solid tori as follows. Let
D=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|z|\leq 1\}
be the unit disk in
\mathbb{C}
with counterclockwise orientation. Let
S^{1}=\partial D
be the unit circle with counterclockwise orientation.
Let
U,V
be two copies of the solid torus
S^{1}\times D
. The boundaries of
U
and
V
are copies of
S^{1}\times S^{1}
. Then
L(p,q)=U\cup_{\varphi}V
is obtained by gluing
U
to
V
along a homeomorphism
\varphi\colon\partial U\to\partial V
such that
\varphi(1,t)=(t^{p},t^{q})
for all
t\in S^{1}
. We provide
L(p,q)
with the orientation extending the product orientation of
V=S^{1}\times D
.

A skeleton
P
of
L(p,q)
is obtained from the disk
\{1\}\times D\subset U
and the circle
S^{1}\times\{0\}\subset V
by pulling the boundary of this disk towards this circle in
V
. More precisely,

P=(\{1\}\times D)\cup_{\varphi}\{(t^{p},kt^{q})\,|\,t\in S^{1},k\in[0,1]\}\subset U\cup_{\varphi}V=L(p,q).

The polyhedron
P
contains the circle
S^{1}\times\{0\}\subset V
which, viewed as a loop based at the point
(1,0)
, determines a stratification of
P
. The stratified polyhedron
P
has one vertex, one edge, and one disk region which we orient by extending the orientation of
D=\{1\}\times D\subset U
. In this way,
P
becomes a skeleton of
L(p,q)
with a single
P
-ball. Note that there are
p
branches of
P
at the edge of
P
.

Any pre-
\chi
-labeling of
P
is determined by a pair
(h,e)\in H\times E
with
\chi(e)=h^{p}
. Here
h
is the
H
-label of the disk region and
e
is the
E
-label of the edge (oriented as
S^{1}=S^{1}\times\{0\}\subset V
and with respect to some chosen adjacent
P
-ball branch). The
\chi
-graph
\Gamma
in
S^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\cup\{\infty\}
associated with the vertex of
P
is

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\Gamma=\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{Xi-graph-lens.eps}}}

with
q
edges below and
(p-q)
edges above the horizontal axis. By Section
5.4
, the grade of
\Gamma
is

|\Gamma|=e\left({\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pt(e^{-1})}}}{}^{(h^{-q})}{\hskip-0.80002pt(e^{-1})}\right).

Then
\Gamma
is 1-spherical (that is,
|\Gamma|=1
) if and only if
{\vphantom{{e}}}{}^{(h^{q})}{e}=e
. Since
p,q
are coprime and
{\vphantom{{e}}}{}^{(h^{p})}{e}={\vphantom{{e}}}^{\chi(e)}{e}=eee^{-1}=e
, this condition is equivalent to
{\vphantom{{e}}}{}^{h}{e}=e
. Consequently any
\chi
-labeling of
P
is fully determined by a pair
(h,e)\in H\times E
such that

(25)

\chi(e)=h^{p}\quad\text{and}\quad{\vphantom{{e}}}^{h}{e}=e.

Note that the gauge group of
P
is
\mathcal{G}_{P}=H\times E
with product

(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\ast(\lambda,\mu)=\left(\lambda^{\prime}\lambda,\,\mu{\vphantom{{\!\mu^{\prime}}}}^{(\lambda^{-1})}{\!\mu^{\prime}}\right)

and that it acts on
\chi
-labelings as:

(\lambda,\mu)\cdot(h,e)=\left(\lambda h\chi(\mu)\lambda^{-1},\,{\vphantom{{(ef_{p})}}}^{\lambda}{(ef_{p})}\right)\quad\text{where}\quad f_{p}=\left({\vphantom{{\mu}}}{}^{(h^{-(p-1)})}{\mu}\right)\cdots\left({\vphantom{{\mu}}}{}^{(h^{-1})}{\mu}\right)\mu.

By Lemma
6.1
, the
\chi
-labeling of
P
induced by a pair
(h,e)\in H\times E
satisfying (
25
) determines a homotopy class
g_{h,e}\in[L(p,q),B\chi]
which depends only on the
\mathcal{G}_{P}
-orbit of
(h,e)
. Moreover, since the region of
P
is a disk, any
g\in[L(p,q),B\chi]
is of the form
g=g_{h,e}
for some
(h,e)\in H\times E
satisfying (
25
).

6.5.
Proof of Lemma
6.1

For each (unoriented) edge
e
of
P
, pick a
P
-ball branch
B_{e}
at
e
, an orientation for
e
, and a disk
\delta_{e}
embedded in
M
which bounds
\gamma_{(e,B_{e})}
, intersects
e
transversely at a single point, and intersects
P
transversely in arcs lying in the regions adjacent to
e
. We endow
\delta_{e}
with the basepoint given by the center
c_{e}
of
B_{e}
and orient
\delta_{e}
so that its orientation followed by that of
e
yields the given orientation of
M
. The condition
\delta_{e}
bounds
\gamma_{(e,B_{e})}
means that
\partial\delta_{e}=\gamma_{(e,B_{e})}
as loops in
M
based at
c_{e}
. (Here we endow
\partial\delta_{e}
with the boundary orientation using the first outward pointing convention.) For example, we have
\partial\delta_{e}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{3}
for the following trivalent edge
e
and
P
-ball branch
B_{e}
:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{neighborhood-edge-disk-new.eps}}}\;.

Each region
r
of
P
is a disk with holes and may be collapsed onto a wedge of circles
W_{r}\subset r\setminus\partial r
based at the point
\alpha_{r}\cap r
. (Here, a circle corresponding to a hole
h
is chosen to be isotopic to

p_{h}(\partial h)p_{h}^{-1}
, where
p_{h}
is a path from the point
\alpha_{r}\cap r
to some vertex of
h
.)
Let
S_{r}
be obtained from
W_{r}\times[-1,1]
by contracting the sets
W_{r}\times\{-1\}
and
W_{r}\times\{1\}
into points which are called the summits of
S_{r}
. (If the two
P
-balls adjacent to
r
are equal, then we identify the two summits of
S_{r}
.) We embed
S_{r}
into
M
as a union of two cones with base
W_{r}
and with summits
in the centers of the
P
-balls adjacent to
r
. We can assume that
\gamma_{r}\subset S_{r}
and that
S_{r}
does not meet
S_{r^{\prime}}
for
r\neq r^{\prime}
except possibly in the summits. Note that
S_{r}=\gamma_{r}
when
r
is a disk. Set
S=\cup_{r}S_{r}
. Then the pair
(M,S)
has a relative CW-decomposition with only 2-cells and 3-cells given by the disks
\{\delta_{e}\}_{e}
and ball neighborhoods in
M
of the vertices of
P
. Let
D=S\cup(\cup_{e}\delta_{e})
be the relative 2-skeleton of
(M,S)
and
C
be the set of centers of
P
-balls.
Consider the following filtration of
M
:

M_{*}=(C\subset S\subset D\subset M).

By Section
2.4
, the classifying space
B\chi
of
\chi
has a canonical filtration

(B\chi)_{*}=(\{x\}\subset BH\subset B\chi)

where
x
is the unique 0-cell of
B\chi
(serving as basepoint) and
BH
is a subcomplex of
B\chi
which is a classifying space of the group
H
, contains all 1-cells of
B\chi
, and whose associated boundary map
\partial\colon\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)\to\pi_{1}(BH,x)
is the crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
.

We denote by
\mathrm{FTop}(M_{*},(B\chi)_{*})
the set of filtered maps
M_{*}\to(B\chi)_{*}
, that is, of continuous maps
f\colon M\to B\chi
such that
f(C)=\{x\}
and
f(S)\subset BH
.

Claim 6.2
.

Any
f\in\mathrm{FTop}(M_{*},(B\chi)_{*})
defines a
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha_{f},\beta_{f})
of
P
such that:

•

\alpha_{f}(r)=f_{*}([\gamma_{r}])\in\pi_{1}(BH,x)=H
for every region
r
of
P
,

•

\beta_{f}(e,B_{e})=f_{*}([\delta_{e}])\in\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)=E
for every edge
e
of
P
.

The induced map
f\in\mathrm{FTop}(M_{*},(B\chi)_{*})\mapsto(\alpha_{f},\beta_{f})\in\{\text{$\chi$-labelings of $P$}\}
has a section
(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto f_{\alpha,\beta}
.

Proof.

For each (unoriented) edge
e
of
P
, the above chosen orientation for
e
and
P
-ball branch
B_{e}
at
e
makes
(e,B_{e})
an element of
\mathrm{EB}(P)
. Denote by
\mathrm{EB}_{0}(P)
the subset of
\mathrm{EB}(P)
made of pairs
(e,B_{e})
as
e
runs over all edges of
P
.
The definitions in the claim give well defined maps
\alpha_{f}\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to H
and
\beta_{f}\colon\mathrm{EB}_{0}(P)\to E
.
For any edge
e
of
P
, recall that
\partial\delta_{e}=\gamma_{b_{1}}^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{1})}\cdots\gamma_{b_{n}}^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{n})}

where
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{n}
are the branches of
P
at
e
enumerated in the linear order determined by
B_{e}
(and the chosen orientation of
e
), and so

\displaystyle\chi(\beta_{f}(e,B_{e}))

\displaystyle=\chi(f_{*}([\delta_{e}]))=f_{*}([\partial\delta_{e}])=\alpha_{f}(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{1})}\cdots\alpha_{f}(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{e}(b_{n})}.

Thus (
20
) is satisfied for all elements in
\mathrm{EB}_{0}(P)
. Now, one easily verifies that any pair of maps
(\alpha\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to H,\beta\colon\mathrm{EB}_{0}(P)\to E)
satisfying (
20
) for all elements in
\mathrm{EB}_{0}(P)
extends uniquely to a pre-
\chi
-labeling of
P
(by extending
\beta
to
\mathrm{EB}(P)
using (
21
) and (
22
)). Then
(\alpha_{f},\beta_{f})
extends uniquely to a pre-
\chi
-labeling of
P
also denoted by
(\alpha_{f},\beta_{f})
. This pre-
\chi
-labeling is a
\chi
-labeling since the restriction of
f
to the boundary of any
P
-cone neighborhood of a vertex of
P
(see Section
6.2
) is null-homotopic.

Let us prove the second assertion of the claim. For each region
r
of
P
, the projection
W_{r}\times[-1,1]\to[-1,1]
induces a retraction
S_{r}\to\gamma_{r}
. Composing with loops
\gamma_{r}\to BH
representing the elements
\alpha(r)\in H=\pi_{1}(BH,x)
, we obtain a map
S=\cup_{r}S_{r}\to BH\subset B\chi
carrying the centers of the
P
-balls to the basepoint
x
of
B\chi

and carrying each
\gamma_{r}
to a loop in
BH
representing
\alpha(r)
. Condition (
20
) ensures that this map extends to the relative 2-skeleton
D=S\cup(\cup_{e}\delta_{e})
of
(M,S)
by carrying each pointed disk
\delta_{e}
to a disk in
B\chi
representing the element
\beta(e,B_{e})\in E=\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)
.
The
1
-sphericality of the
\chi
-graphs
associated with the vertices of
P
implies that the restriction of this map to the boundary of each
3
-cell is null-homotopic. Therefore, there is a further extension to
M
producing a filtered map
f_{\alpha,\beta}\colon M_{*}\to(B\chi)_{*}
. The conditions (
21
) and (
22
) ensure that the map

f_{\alpha,\beta}
does not depend on the choices of the adjacent
P
-ball branches and orientations for the edges of
P
. Finally, it follows directly from the definitions that
(\alpha_{f_{\alpha,\beta}},\beta_{f_{\alpha,\beta}})=(\alpha,\beta)
.
∎

Note that for any
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
, the homotopy class
[f_{\alpha,\beta}]\in[M,B\chi]

of the map
f_{\alpha,\beta}
defined in Claim
6.2
depends only on
P
and
(\alpha,\beta)
since any two systems of arcs
\{\gamma_{r}\}_{r}
, disks
\{\delta_{e}\}_{e}
, and suspensions
\{S_{r}\}_{r}
are isotopic in
M
.

Claim 6.3
.

Two
\chi
-labelings
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
of
P
are gauge equivalent if and only if their associated maps
f_{\alpha,\beta}
and
f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
are homotopic.

Proof.

Assume first that
f_{\alpha,\beta}
and
f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
are homotopic. Set
I=[0,1]
with its canonical filtration
I_{*}=(\{0,1\}\subset I)
. Let
G\colon M\times I\to B\chi
be a homotopy from
f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
to
f_{\alpha,\beta}
. Note that
G
is filtered (where
M\times I
is endowed with the product filtration) if and only if and

G(C\times I)\subset BH
. The surjectivity of the map
\pi_{1}(BH,x)\to\pi_{1}(B\chi,x)=\text{Coker}(\chi)
implies that for each
c\in C
, the loop
G_{|\{c\}\times I}
in
B\chi
is homotopic relative
BH
to a loop in
BH
.
Consequently, any homotopy among filtered maps
M_{*}\to(B\chi)_{*}
can be realized by a filtered homotopy. Thus, without loss of generality, we can assume that
G
is filtered.
For any
P
-ball
B
, let
c_{B}\in C
be the center of
B
. The restriction of
\theta_{B}=G_{|\{c_{B}\}\times I}
is a loop in
BH
and represents a class
\lambda(B)\in\pi_{1}(BH,x)=H
. For any region
r
of
P
, viewing the square
\gamma_{r}\times I
(with product orientation) as a disk pointed at
(c_{r_{+}},1)
, the restriction
u_{r}=G_{|\gamma_{r}\times I}
represents a class
\mu(r)\in\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)=E
. These assignments give rise to an element
(\lambda,\mu)\in\mathcal{G}_{P}
.
Let us check that
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\lambda,\mu)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)
.
For any region
r
of
P
, we represent
u_{r}
as

where
a_{r}=G_{|\gamma_{r}\times\{1\}}=(f_{\alpha,\beta})_{|\gamma_{r}}
and
a^{\prime}_{r}=G_{|\gamma_{r}\times\{0\}}=(f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})_{|\gamma_{r}}
are loops in
BH
.
By Claim
6.2
, the homotopy classes of
a_{r}
and
a^{\prime}_{r}
are
\alpha(r)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(r)
, respectively. Since the loop
\partial u_{r}=G_{|\partial(\gamma_{r}\times I)}
is homotopic to the concatenation

a_{r}^{-1}\theta_{r_{-}}^{-1}a^{\prime}_{r}\theta_{r_{+}}
, we obtain that

\chi(\mu(r))=\alpha(r)^{-1}\lambda(r_{-})^{-1}\alpha^{\prime}(r)\lambda(r_{+})
, and so (
23
) is satisfied.
As explained in the proof of Claim
6.2
, we only need to verify (
24
) for
(e,B_{e})\in EB(P)
where
e
is any edge of
P
. Recall that the disk
\delta_{e}
is based at the center
c_{e}
of
B_{e}
and that
\partial\delta_{e}=\gamma_{(e,B_{e})}=\gamma_{r_{1}}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{r_{n}}^{\varepsilon_{n}}
with
r_{i}\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
and
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{+,-\}
. Consider the restrictions
b_{e}=G_{|\delta_{e}\times\{1\}}=(f_{\alpha,\beta})_{|\delta_{e}}
and

b^{\prime}_{e}=G_{|\delta_{e}\times\{0\}}=(f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})_{|\delta_{e}}
.
Then
b_{e}
is homotopic (relative boundary) to
the restriction of
G
to
(\cup_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{r_{i}}\times I)\cup(\delta_{e}\times\{0\})
depicted as:

Choosing
(c_{e},1)
as basepoint, they thus represent the same class in
\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)
.
Now it follows from Claim
6.2
, the definition of
\mu_{\alpha}
, and the definition of the action of
\pi_{1}(BH,x)
on
\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)
that
b_{e}
represents the class
\beta(e,B_{e})
and the latter restriction of
G
represents the class
{\vphantom{{\!\!\beta^{\prime}(e,B_{e})}}}{}^{\lambda(B_{e})^{-1}}{\!\!\beta^{\prime}(e,B_{e})}\,\mu_{\alpha}(\gamma_{(e,B_{e})})^{-1}
. Then the equality of these classes implies that (
24
) is satisfied for
(e,B_{e})
.
Hence
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\lambda,\mu)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)
. In particular,
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
are gauge equivalent.

Conversely, assume that
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
are gauge equivalent. Pick
(\lambda,\mu)\in\mathcal{G}_{P}
such that
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\lambda,\mu)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)
. For any
c\in C
, let
B_{c}
be the
P
-ball whose center is
c
. Pick a loop
\theta_{c}
in
BH
representing
\lambda(B_{c})\in H=\pi_{1}(BH,x)
. For any region
r
of
P
,
a_{r}=(f_{\alpha,\beta})_{|\gamma_{r}}
and
a^{\prime}_{r}=(f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})_{|\gamma_{r}}
are loops in
BH
whose homotopy classes are
\alpha(r)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(r)
, respectively (see Claim
6.2
). Using (
23
), the homotopy class of the loop
a_{r}^{-1}(\theta_{c_{r_{-}}})^{-1}a^{\prime}_{r}\theta_{c_{r_{+}}}
is

\alpha(r)^{-1}\lambda(r_{-})^{-1}\alpha^{\prime}(r)\lambda(r_{+})=\chi(\mu(r)).

Thus, viewing the square
\gamma_{r}\times I
as a disk pointed at
(c_{r_{+}},1)
, there is a map

u_{r}\colon\gamma_{r}\times I\to B\chi
which represents
\mu(r)\in E=\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)
and such that

(u_{r})_{|\{c_{r_{+}}\}\times I}=\theta_{c_{r_{+}}},\quad(u_{r})_{|\{c_{r_{-}}\}\times I}=\theta_{c_{r_{-}}},\quad(u_{r})_{|\gamma_{r}\times\{1\}}=a_{r},\quad(u_{r})_{|\gamma_{r}\times\{0\}}=a^{\prime}_{r}

as depicted above. We now construct a (filtered) homotopy
f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
to
f_{\alpha,\beta}
via successive extensions.
First, we define a map
G_{1}\colon(M\times\{0,1\})\cup(S\times I)\to B\chi
as follows: set
(G_{1})_{|M\times\{0\}}=f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
,
(G_{1})_{|M\times\{1\}}=f_{\alpha,\beta}
, and for any region
r
of
P
,
let
(G_{1})_{|S_{r}\times I}
be the composition of the map
S_{r}\times I\to\gamma_{r}\times I
(induced by the canonical retraction
S_{r}\to\gamma_{r}
and
\mathrm{id}_{I}
) with the map
u_{r}
. For any edge
e
of
P
, (
24
) applied to
(e,B_{e})
implies that
the restriction of
G_{1}
to the boundary of the 3-cell
\delta_{e}\times I
is null-homotopic, and so
G_{1}
can be extended to
\delta_{e}\times I
. Pick then an extension
G_{2}\colon(M\times\{0,1\})\cup(D\times I)\to B\chi
of
G_{1}
. Note that
G_{2}
can further be extended to
M\times I
since the target
B\chi
is a
2
-type. Any such extension
G\colon M\times I\to B\chi
is then a (filtered) homotopy from
f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
to
f_{\alpha,\beta}
.
∎

Claims
6.2
and
6.3

imply that the assignment
(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto f_{\alpha,\beta}
induces a well-defined injective map
\{\chi\text{-labelings of }P\}/\mathcal{G}_{P}\to[M,B\chi]
which maps the gauge equivalence class of a
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
to the homotopy class of the map
f_{\alpha,\beta}
.

Finally, assume that the regions of
P
are disks. Then the above filtration
M_{*}=(C\subset S\subset D\subset M)
is the skeletal filtration of the CW-decomposition of
M
dual to
P
(since
S=\cup_{r}\gamma_{r}
).
By the cellular approximation theorem, any map
f\colon M\to B\chi
is homotopic to a cellular map
f^{\prime}\colon M\to B\chi
. Any such map
f^{\prime}
is then a filtered map
M_{*}\to(B\chi)_{*}
since
f^{\prime}(C)=\{x\}
and
f^{\prime}(S)\subset\{\text{1-cells of $B\chi$}\}\subset BH
. Thus any map
M\to B\chi
is homotopic to a filtered one
M_{*}\to(B\chi)_{*}
. Also, any filtered map
f\colon M_{*}\to(B\chi)_{*}
is homotopic to the map
f_{\alpha_{f},\beta_{f}}
(because the
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha_{f},\beta_{f})
encodes the restriction of these maps on the 2-skeleton of
M
and
B\chi
is a 2-type). Consequently, the map
\{\chi\text{-labelings of }P\}/\mathcal{G}_{P}\to[M,B\chi]
is surjective.

6.6.
Remark

As in the end of the above proof, assume that the regions of
P
are disks. Let
\Pi(P)
be the crossed complex associated with the skeletal filtration of the CW-decomposition of
M
dual to
P
. Then
\chi
-labelings of
P
correspond to crossed complex morphisms
\Pi(P)\to\chi
(called formal maps in
[
Po
]
) and the last claim of Lemma
6.1
follows from the homotopy classification theorem
[
BH
, Theorem A]
.

6.7.
Closed
\chi
-manifolds and their
\chi
-skeletons

By a
closed
\chi
-manifold
, we mean a pair
(M,g)
where
M
is a closed oriented 3-dimensional manifold and
g
is a homotopy class of maps
M\to B\chi
.

A
\chi
-skeleton
of a closed
\chi
-manifold
(M,g)

is a skeleton
P
of
M
endowed with a
\chi
-labeling of
P
representing
g\in[M,B\chi]
.
For brevity,
\chi
-skeleton will be often denoted by the same letter
P
as the underlying skeleton. Lemma
6.1
shows that any closed
\chi
-
manifold has a
\chi
-skeleton (since any oriented closed 3-dimensional manifold always has a skeleton with disk regions, such as the 2-skeleton of a triangulation).

6.8.
Moves on
\chi
-skeletons

Let
M
be a closed oriented 3-dimensional manifold. We consider four local moves
T_{0},T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
on a skeleton
P
of
M
transforming
P
into a new skeleton of
M
(see
[
TVi4
]
). Each of these moves modifies
P
inside a closed 3-ball in
M
as in Figure
2
where the shaded horizontal plane represents (a piece of)
P
and the upper half-space represents an adjacent
P
-ball branch
B
. The edges shown in the horizontal plane before the moves may be adjacent to regions of
P
lying in the lower half-space (and not shown in the picture).

The
bubble move

T_{0}
adds to
P
an embedded disk
D_{+}\subset M
such that
\partial D_{+}=D_{+}\cap P\subset P\setminus P^{(1)}
, the circle
\partial D_{+}
bounds a disk
D_{-}

in
P\setminus P^{(1)}
, and the 2-sphere
D_{+}\cup D_{-}
bounds a
ball in
M
meeting
P
precisely along
D_{-}
. A point of the circle
\partial D_{+}=\partial D_{-}
is chosen as a vertex of the skeleton
T_{0}(P)=P\cup D_{+}
, and the circle itself is an edge of
T_{0}(P)
. The disks
D_{+}
and
D_{-}
become regions of
T_{0}(P)
, and all other regions of
T_{0}(P)
correspond bijectively to the regions of
P
.

The
phantom edge move

T_{1}
keeps
P
as a polyhedron and adds a new 2-valent edge meeting

P^{(1)}
solely at its endpoints which must be distinct vertices of
P
.
The
contraction move

T_{2}
collapses an edge of
P
with distinct endpoints into a point.
This move is allowed only when at least one endpoint of the collapsing edge is an endpoint of some other edge. (The valence of the collapsing edge may be arbitrary.)
The
percolation move

T_{3}
pushes a branch of
P

through a vertex
x
of
P
. The branch is pushed across a small
disk
D
lying in another branch of
P
at
x
so that these branches are adjacent to the same component of
M\setminus P
and

D\cap P^{(1)}=\partial D\cap P^{(1)}=\{x\}
.
The loop
\partial D
based at
x
becomes an edge of the resulting 2-polyhedron. The disk
D
becomes a region of the resulting skeleton
T_{3}(P)
and all other regions of
T_{3}(P)
correspond bijectively to the regions of
P
in the obvious way.

Figure 2.
Local moves on skeletons

In the pictures of
T_{0}-T_{3}
, we
distinguish the “small” regions and edges entirely contained in the
3-ball where the move proceeds, from the “big” regions and edges not
entirely contained in that 3-ball. Recall that all regions of a skeleton are oriented. The moves
T_{0}-T_{3}
preserve orientation in the big regions while orientation of the small disk regions created by
T_{0}
and
T_{3}
may be arbitrary.

The moves
T_{0}-T_{3}
lift to
\chi
-labelings as follows. The
H
-labels of all big regions and
E
-labels of all big edges are preserved under the moves.
Under
T_{0}
, the
H
-label of
D_{+}
and the
E
-label of the edge
\partial D_{+}
(for some orientation of
\partial D_{+}
and with respect to
B
) may be chosen arbitrarily, and then the
H
-label of
D_{-}
is determined uniquely by (
20
).
One easily verifies that different choices for the labels of
D_{+}
and
\partial D_{+}
give rise to gauge equivalent
\chi
-labelings.
Under
T_{1}
, the
E
-label of the added 2-valent edge should be
1\in E
.
For
T_{2}
, there are no conditions on the
E
-label of the collapsed edge.
Under
T_{3}
, the
E
-label of the edge
\partial D
(for some orientation of
\partial D
and with respect to
B
) may be chosen arbitrarily and the
H
-label of
D
is then determined uniquely by (
20
). Different choices for the
E
-label of
\partial D
give rise to gauge equivalent
\chi
-labelings.
Note that the link graphs of the vertices created by these moves are indeed 1-spherical
\chi
-graphs. These moves lifted to
\chi
-labelings are called
\chi
-moves
.

The
\chi
-moves
T_{0}-T_{3}
have inverses. The
\chi
-move
T_{0}^{-1}
is obvious from the picture of
T_{0}
. The
\chi
-move
T_{1}^{-1}
deletes a 2-valent edge with distinct endpoints which are also endpoints of some other edges. This move is allowed only when the
E
-label (with respect to
B
) of the edge is
1
and the orientations of the two regions adjacent to that edge are compatible. The
\chi
-move
T_{2}^{-1}
stretches a vertex into an edge and the
E
-label (with respect to
B
) of this new edge is uniquely determined by the 1-sphericality of the
\chi
-graph associated to a created vertex.
The
\chi
-move
T_{3}^{-1}
collapses an embedded disk region whose boundary is formed by a single 3-valent edge with coinciding endpoints.

The
\chi
-moves
T_{0}^{\pm 1},T_{1}^{\pm 1},T_{2}^{\pm 1},T_{3}^{\pm 1}
together with the label-preserving
ambient isotopies of
\chi
-skeletons in
M
are called
primary
\chi
-moves
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Any primary
\chi
-move transforms a
\chi
-skeleton of a closed
\chi
-manifold
(M,g)
into a
\chi
-skeleton of
(M,g)
.
Moreover, any two
\chi
-skeletons of
(M,g)
can be related by a finite sequence of primary
\chi
-moves.

We prove Lemma
6.4
in the next section.

6.9.
Proof of Lemma
6.4

If a skeleton
P
of
M
is transformed into a skeleton
P^{\prime}
of
M
via a move
T_{i}
with
i\in\{0,2,3\}
, then any lift of this move to a
\chi
-move induces a bijection

\{\text{$\chi$-labelings of $P$}\}_{/{\mathcal{G}_{P}}}\xrightarrow{{\;\cong\;}}\{\text{$\chi$-labelings of $P^{\prime}$}\}_{/{\mathcal{G}_{P^{\prime}}}}.

Also, if a skeleton
P
of
M
is transformed into a skeleton
P^{\prime}
of
M
via the move
T_{1}
by adding an edge
e
inside a region
r
of
P
, then the lift of this move to a
\chi
-move (by labeling
e
with
1\in E
) induces a bijection

\{\text{$\chi$-labelings of $P$}\}_{/{\mathcal{G}_{P}}}\xrightarrow{{\;\cong\;}}\{\text{$\chi$-labelings of $P^{\prime}$ with $e$ labeled by $1$}\}_{/{\mathcal{G}_{P^{\prime}}}}.

It is worth to notice that if adding the edge
e
transforms
r
into two distinct regions, then

\{\text{$\chi$-labelings of $P^{\prime}$ with $e$ labeled by $1$}\}_{/{\mathcal{G}_{P^{\prime}}}}=\{\text{$\chi$-labelings of $P^{\prime}$}\}_{/{\mathcal{G}_{P^{\prime}}}}.

The existence and injectivity of these maps follow from the fact that two
\chi
-labelings of
P
give rise to gauge equivalent
\chi
-labelings of
P^{\prime}
if and only if they are gauge equivalent. The surjectivity of these maps follow from the existence of inverse
\chi
-moves. Moreover, these bijections commutes with the maps to the set of homotopy classes of maps from
M
to
B\chi
given by Lemma
6.1
. This implies the first assertion of the lemma.

To prove the second assertion of the lemma, we need further
\chi
-moves on
\chi
-skeletons.
The lune
\chi
-move
\mathcal{L}
is defined as:

and, for any non-negative integers
m,n
with
m+n\geq 1
, the
\chi
-move
T^{m,n}
is defined as:

\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\psfrag\raisebox{-0.175pt}{\scalebox{.9}{\includegraphics[]{movemn-col.eps}}}\;.

The
\chi
-move
\mathcal{L}
creates a small disk region. The
\chi
-move
T^{m,n}
destroys
\max(m-1,0)
small disk regions and creates
\max(n-1,0)
small disk regions. These
\chi
-moves preserve orientation in the big regions. The orientation of a small
created disk is induced by the orientation of the corresponding big region before the move. As above, the
H
-labels of all big regions and
E
-labels of all big edges are preserved under the move. The
E
-labels of the created edges depicted in red are all equal to
1\in E
. This uniquely determines the
H
-labels of the small created disk regions (using (
20
)) as well as the
E
-labels of the created edges depicted in blue (using the 1-sphericity of the
\chi
-graphs associated to the created vertices).
The
\chi
-move
\mathcal{L}
has an obvious inverse
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
. It is allowed only
when the orientations and
H
-labels of two regions united under this move as well as the
E
-labels of two edges united under this move are compatible. The
\chi
-moves
\mathcal{L}^{\pm 1}
and
T^{m,n}
transform a
\chi
-skeleton of
(M,g)
into a
\chi
-skeleton of
(M,g)
. Indeed, they are compositions of the primary
\chi
-moves
T_{1}^{\pm 1}
,
T_{2}^{\pm 1}
, and
T_{3}
for which the
E
-label of the created edge is
1\in E
. (These compositions lift the decomposition of the corresponding moves on skeletons given in
[
TVi4
, Section 11.3.2]
.)

We also need the
\chi
-move
T^{\prime}_{0}
defined as:

and viewed as the composition of the
\chi
-moves
T_{0}
,
T_{1}
, and then
T_{2}
(first apply a bubble move to the left region, then connect the two vertices with an edge, and lastly contract this edge). The inverse of
T^{\prime}_{0}
always exists. This follows from the fact that the 1-sphericality of the
\chi
-graph associated to the vertex of the right-hand side of the picture always implies the 1-sphericality of the
\chi
-graph associated to the vertex of the left-hand side.
As above, we will denote the moves on skeletons underlying the
\chi
-moves
T^{m,n}
and
(T^{\prime}_{0})^{\pm 1}
by the same letters.

Claim 6.5
.

Two skeletons of
M
with disk regions can be related by a finite sequence of the moves
(T_{0}^{\prime})^{\pm 1}
, T
{}_{2}^{\pm 1}
, and
T_{3}^{\pm 1}
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
TVi4
, Section 11.1.5]
that an
s
-skeleton of
M
is a skeleton
P
of
M
such that any point of
P
has an open neighborhood homeomorphic to an open subset of the set

\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,|\,\,x_{3}=0,\,{\text{or}}\,\,x_{1}=0\,\,{\text{and}}\,\,x_{3}>0,\,\,{\text{or}}\,\,x_{2}=0\,\,{\text{and}}\,\,x_{3}<0\}

depicted as

.

The proof of
[
TVi4
, Lemma 11.4]
implies that any skeleton of
M
with disk regions can be turned into an
s
-skeleton of
M
with disk regions by finitely many
(T_{0}^{\prime})^{\pm 1},T_{2}^{\pm 1},T_{3}^{\pm 1}
-moves.
Next, by
[
Mat
, Theorem 1.2.30]
, any two
s
-skeletons of
M
with disk regions can be related by finitely many
T^{1,2}
and
T^{2,1}
-moves which can be expressed using
T_{2}^{\pm 1}
- and
T_{3}^{\pm 1}
-moves.
∎

Claim 6.6
.

Two
\chi
-skeletons of
(M,g)
with the same underlying skeleton of
M
are related by a finite sequence of primary
\chi
-moves.

Proof.

Let
(P,(\alpha,\beta))
and
(P,(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}))
be
\chi
-skeletons of
(M,g)
with the same underlying skeleton
P
of
M
. By Lemma
6.1
, the
\chi
-labelings
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
are gauge equivalent (since they represent the same homotopy class
g
).
Moreover, the gauge group
\mathcal{G}_{P}
is generated by the set

\bigl\{(\lambda_{B,h},\mu_{r,e})\,\big|\,\text{B is a $P$-ball, $r$ is a region adjacent to $B$, $h\in H$, $e\in E$}\bigr\},

where
\lambda_{B,h}\colon\pi_{0}(M\setminus P)\to H
carries
B
to
h
and carries all other
P
-balls to
1
, while

\mu_{r,e}\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E
carries
r
to
e
and carries all other regions to
1
. Thus, it is enough to show that there is a finite sequence of primary
\chi
-moves between
(P,(\alpha,\beta))
and
(P,(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}))

when
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\lambda_{B,h},\mu_{r,e})\cdot(\alpha,\beta)
. To prove this, we
first apply a bubble move which adds to
P
a 2-disk
D_{+}\subset\overline{B}
such that the circle
\partial D_{+}
lies in
r
and bounds a 2-disk
D_{-}\subset r
.
We endow
D_{-}
with the orientation induced by that of
r
. We orient
D_{+}
so that the orientation of
D_{+}
followed by a normal vector pointing towards
B
yields the given orientation of
M
. We endow the small region
D_{+}
with the
H
-label
h
. We endow the oriented edge
\partial D_{-}
(relative to
B
) with
E
-label
e
if
r_{+}=B
and with the
E
-label
{\vphantom{{\hskip-0.80002pte}}}{}^{\alpha(r)}{\hskip-0.80002pte}
otherwise. The
H
-label of
D_{-}
, uniquely determined by (
20
), is then equal to
\alpha^{\prime}(r)
.
Next, we isotop the disk
D_{+}
in
B
so that it sweeps
B
almost entirely while its boundary slides along
\partial\overline{B}
. We arrange that in the terminal position
D^{\prime}_{+}
of the moving disk, its boundary circle lies in
r\setminus D_{-}
and bounds there a 2-disk. This isotopy of
D_{+}
can be performed using a finite sequence of the
\chi
-moves
\mathcal{L}^{\pm 1}
and
T^{m,n}
. Next we remove the disk
D^{\prime}_{+}
using the inverse bubble
\chi
-move. This sequence transforms the
\chi
-skeleton
(P,(\alpha,\beta))
into a
\chi
-skeleton
(P,(\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}))
of
(M,g)
. Finally, it follows from the choices of labelings of the first bubble
\chi
-move that
(\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime})=(\lambda_{B,h},\mu_{r,e})\cdot(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
.
∎

Let us prove the second assertion of the lemma. Let
(P,(\alpha,\beta))
and
(P^{\prime},(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}))
be two
\chi
-skeletons of
(M,g)
.
If necessary, apply finitely many
T_{1}
-moves to transform non-disk regions of
P
and
P^{\prime}
into disk regions, obtaining then skeletons
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
of
M
with disk regions. These
T_{1}
-moves lift to
\chi
-moves by labeling added edges with
1\in E
and we obtain
\chi
-skeletons
(Q,(\gamma,\delta))
and
(Q^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}))
of
(M,g)
. Note that these
\chi
-moves have inverses. In particular, there are finite sequences of primary
\chi
-moves from
(P,(\alpha,\beta))
to
(Q,(\gamma,\delta))
and from
(Q^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}))
to
(P^{\prime},(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}))
.
By Claim
6.5
, there is a finite sequence of moves
(T_{0}^{\prime})^{\pm 1}
,
T_{2}^{\pm 1}
, and
T_{3}^{\pm 1}
transforming
Q
into
Q^{\prime}
. Since all these moves can always be lifted to
\chi
-moves (because they transform a skeleton with disk regions into a skeleton with disk regions), the
\chi
-labeling
(\gamma,\delta)
of
Q
can be transported under this sequence (by lifting successively the moves to
\chi
-moves) to a
\chi
-labeling
(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime})
of
Q^{\prime}
. In particular, there is a sequence of primary
\chi
-moves between
(Q,(\gamma,\delta))
and
(Q^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}))
.
By the first assertion of the lemma,
(Q^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}))
and
(Q^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}))
are
\chi
-skeletons of
(M,g)
.
Then Claim
6.6
implies that there is a finite sequence of primary
\chi
-moves between
(Q^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}))
and
(Q^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}))
. Hence, by composing the above sequences, we obtain a finite sequence of primary
\chi
-moves between
(P,(\alpha,\beta))
and
(P^{\prime},(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}))
.

7.
State-sum invariants of closed
\chi
-manifolds

We fix throughout this section a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
, a spherical
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
(over
\Bbbk
), and a
\chi
-representative set
I=\amalg_{h\in H}\,I_{h}
for
\mathcal{C}
.
We assume that the dimension
\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})
of the neutral component
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1}
of the 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
of
\mathcal{C}
is invertible in the ground ring
\Bbbk
(see Section
4.10
). We derive from this data a scalar topological invariant of closed
\chi
-manifolds (see Section
6.7
) defined as a state sum on
\chi
-skeletons.

7.1.
The state sum invariant

Let
(M,g)
be a closed
\chi
-manifold.
Pick a
\chi
-skeleton
P
of
M
(see Section
6.7
). Denote by
(\alpha,\beta)
the
\chi
-labeling of
P
and recall from Section
6.1
that
\mathrm{Reg}(P)
is the (finite) set of regions of
P
.

A
coloring
of
P
is a map
c\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to I
such that
c(r)\in I_{\alpha(r)}
for all
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
.
The object
c(r)\in I
assigned to a region
r
of
P
is called the
c
-color
of
r
.
Note that there are finitely many colorings of
P
(because the sets
I_{h}
with
h\in H
and
\mathrm{Reg}(P)
are finite).
For a coloring
c
of
P
, we set

(26)

\dim(c)=\prod_{r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)}(\dim c(r))^{\mathrm{Eul}(r)}\in\Bbbk,

where
\mathrm{Eul}(r)
is the Euler characteristic of the region
r
. Note that
\dim(c)
is well-defined since the dimension of a simple object in the 1-subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{1}
of
\mathcal{C}
is invertible (see Section
4.10
).

Next, we define a scalar
|c|\in\Bbbk
for any coloring
c
of
P
.
For each oriented edge
e
of
P
, the coloring
c
of
P
turns the
\chi
-cyclic set
P_{e}
of branches of
P
at
e
(see Section
6.3
) into a
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set by assigning to each branch
b\in P_{e}
the
c
-color of the region of
P
containing
b
. Let
H_{c}(e)=H(P_{e})
be its multiplicity module (see Section
5.1
). It is a free
\Bbbk
-module of finite rank (by Lemma
3.1
).
Let

H_{c}=\underset{e}{\bigotimes}\,H_{c}(e)

be the unordered tensor product of the
\Bbbk
-modules
H_{c}(e)
over all oriented edges
e
of
P
.

Each unoriented edge
e
of
P
gives rise to two opposite oriented edges
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
of
P
. The
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set
P_{e_{1}}
and
P_{e_{2}}
are dual:
P_{e_{2}}=(P_{e_{1}})^{\mathrm{op}}
. This yields a vector

\ast_{e}=\ast_{P_{e_{1}}}\in H_{c}(e_{1})\otimes H_{c}(e_{2})

independent of the numeration of
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
