44.5487pt\raise-13.11674pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.5487pt\raise-19.91684pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.97916pt\raise-27.25015pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.48442pt\raise-32.43764pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\chi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.98622pt\raise-27.25015pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.98622pt\raise-27.25015pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right)

with
\iota(e)=(1,e)
and
\varphi(h,e)=\chi(e)h
. Note that
B\iota
is a
K(H,1)
space (since
\iota
is injective and
\mathrm{Coker}(\iota)\simeq H
). For any
\tilde{g}\in[M,B\iota]_{*}
,
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\iota),\tilde{g})=1
(because
B\iota
is a
K(H,1)
space). Also,

|M,\tilde{g}|_{(E,H\ltimes E)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}}\overset{(i)}{=}|M,\tilde{g}|_{H\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}}\overset{(ii)}{=}1_{\Bbbk}.

Here
(i)
follows from the remark after Theorem
7.1
relating the invariants of Theorem
7.1
and
[
TVi1
]
(using the injectivity of
\iota
) and the fact that
(E,H\ltimes E)\,\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}
is isomorphic (as a
H
-fusion category) to the category of finite-dimensional
H
-graded
\Bbbk
-vectors spaces, and
(ii)
follows from
[
TVi1
, Example 8.6]
(with
\theta=1
). Then, since
d_{\phi}=\mathrm{card}(E)
, Theorem
7.2
implies that

|M,g|_{\chi\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}}=\dfrac{\mathrm{card}\bigl(\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi),g)\bigr)}{\mathrm{card}(E)}\,\mathrm{card}\bigl(\{\tilde{g}\in[M,B\iota]_{*}\,|\,B\phi\circ\tilde{g}=g\}\bigr)\,1_{\Bbbk}.

Since
\Bbbk
is a field of characteristic zero, we obtain that
|M,g|_{\chi\mbox{-}\,\mathrm{vect}_{\Bbbk}}\neq 0
if and only if there is

\tilde{g}\in[M,B\iota]_{*}
such that
B\phi\circ\tilde{g}=g
. This proves (
31
) using that
B\phi
is pointed homotopic to the canonical inclusion
BH\hookrightarrow B\chi
(up to a pointed homotopy equivalence
B\iota\simeq BH
).

7.12.
Proof of Theorem
7.2

For
X\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hom}}=\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{\mathrm{hom}}
, the degree of
X
in
\mathcal{C}
is denoted by
|X|
and the degree of
X
in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is then
\varphi(|X|)
.
Let
I=\amalg_{h\in H}\,I_{h}
be a
\chi
-representative set for
\mathcal{C}
. Consider the left action
E\times I\to I
,
(e,i)\mapsto e\cdot i
given by Lemma
3.2
(iii). Recall that
j=e\cdot i
if and only if

i\cong_{e}j
, and so
|e\cdot i|=\chi(e)|i|
. This together with the equality
\varphi\chi=\chi^{\prime}\psi
implies that for all
e\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
and
i\in I
,

(32)

\varphi(|e\cdot i|)=\varphi(|i|).

Claim 7.3
.

The subgroup
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
of
E
acts freely on
I
.

Proof.

Let
e\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
and
i\in I
such that
i=e\cdot i
. Then
|i|=|e\cdot i|=\chi(e)|i|
and so
\chi(e)=1
. Therefore
e\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)=1
and
e=1
.
∎

Pick a complete set
J\subset I
of representatives of
I/\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
such that
\mathbb{1}\in J
.

Claim 7.4
.

We have:

(i)

For all
i\in I
, there is a unique
(e,j)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\times J
such that
i=e\cdot j
.

(ii)

J
is a
\chi^{\prime}
-representative set for
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
splitting as
J=\amalg_{h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}}\,J_{h^{\prime}}
with

J_{h^{\prime}}=\amalg_{h\in\varphi^{-1}(h^{\prime})}\,J\cap I_{h}.

(iii)

For any
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
, the map

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\times J_{h^{\prime}}&\to&\amalg_{h\in\varphi^{-1}(h^{\prime})}I_{h}\\
(e,j)&\mapsto&e\cdot j\end{array}\right.

is a bijection.

Proof.

(i) follows directly from the definition of
J
and Claim
7.3
. Let us prove (ii). By definition,
J\subset I\subset\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hom}}=\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{\mathrm{hom}}
and
\mathbb{1}\in J
. Recall from Section
4.14
that an object is simple in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}
if and only if it is simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. In particular
J\subset I
is made of simple objects of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}
. Next, let
X
be a simple object of
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}
. By definition of
I
and since
X
is simple in
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, there is
i\in I
such that
X
is
1
-isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}
to
i
. By (i), there is
(e,j)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\times J
such that
i=e\cdot j
, that is,
i
is
e
-isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}
to
j
. Then, by (
3
),
X
is
e
-isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}
to
j
. Since
\psi(e)=1
, we deduce that
X
is
1
-isomorphic in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
to
j
. To prove the uniqueness of such a
j\in J
, consider another
k\in J
such that
X
is
1
-isomorphic in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
to
k
. Then
k
is
1
-isomorphic in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
to
j
and so, by Lemma
3.1
(ii), the
\Bbbk
-module
\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{1}(k,j)
is free of rank 1. Since

\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{1}(k,j)=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{a}(k,j),

there is
a\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{a}(k,j)\neq 0
. Lemma
3.1
(ii) implies that
k
is
a
-isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}
to
j
, and so
j=a\cdot k
. Then
j
and
k
belong to the same class in
I/\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
and so are equal (by definition of
J
). Then
X
is
1
-isomorphic in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
to a unique element of
J
. Hence
J
is a
\chi^{\prime}
-representative set for
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
. The splitting of
J
is derived directly from the splitting of
I
and the fact that the degree in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
of
j\in J
is
\varphi(|j|)
.

Let us prove (iii). The map is well-defined since for any
(e,j)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\times J_{h^{\prime}}
, (
32
) gives that
\varphi(|e\cdot j|)=\varphi(|j|)=h^{\prime}
and so
|e\cdot j|\in\varphi^{-1}(h^{\prime})
. Let
i\in I_{h}
with
h\in\varphi^{-1}(h^{\prime})
. By (i), there is a unique
(e,j)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\times J
such that
i=e\cdot j
. Now
j\in J_{h^{\prime}}
since

j=e^{-1}\cdot i
and
\varphi(|e^{-1}\cdot i|)=\varphi(|i|)=\varphi(h)=h^{\prime}
by (
32
).
Thus there is a unique
(e,j)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\times J_{h^{\prime}}
such that
i=e\cdot j
. Hence the map is bijective.
∎

Claim 7.5
.

\dim\bigr(\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}_{1}\bigl)=d_{\phi}\dim(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
.

Proof.

The bijection of Claim
7.4
(iii) for
h^{\prime}=1
gives that

\sum_{(e,j)\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\times J_{1}}\hskip-16.99998pt\dim(e\cdot j)^{2}\;=\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{i\in\amalg_{h\in\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)}I_{h}}\hskip-15.00002pt\dim(i)^{2}.

Note that
\dim(e\cdot j)=\dim(j)
because
e\cdot j
and
j
are isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}
, the left-hand side is equal to

\mathrm{card}\bigr(\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\bigl)\dim\bigr(\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}_{1}\bigl)
. By (
8
),
the right-hand side is equal to
\mathrm{card}\bigr(\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)\bigl)\dim(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
. We conclude by using the definition of
d_{\phi}
.
∎

Let
M
be a connected pointed oriented closed 3-dimensional manifold. Since
M
is connected, there is a skeleton
P
of
M
with disk regions and with a single
P
-ball containing the basepoint of
M
. (An example of such a skeleton is provided by a spine of
M\setminus B
where
B
is an open 3-ball in
M
containing the basepoint of
M
.) Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{P,\chi}
the gauge group of
\chi
-colorings of
P
(see Section
6.3
) and consider its subgroup

\mathcal{G}_{P,\chi}^{*}=\{1\}\times\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{Reg}(P),E),

where
1
is the trivial map (sending the unique
P
-ball to
1\in H
). Two
\chi
-labelings of
P
are
pointed gauge equivalent
if they belong to the same
\mathcal{G}_{P,\chi}^{*}
-orbit. The next claim is a version of Lemma
6.1
for pointed homotopy classes.

Claim 7.6
.

There is a bijection from the set of pointed gauge equivalence classes of
\chi
-labelings of
P
to the set of pointed homotopy classes of pointed maps
M\to B\chi
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.1
by picking the basepoint of
M
as the center of the unique
P
-ball and picking arcs
\{\gamma_{r}\}_{r}
and disks
\{\delta_{e}\}_{e}
as in Section
6.5
.
For any
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
, the pointed homotopy class of the map
f_{\alpha,\beta}
defined in Claim
6.2
depends only on
P
and
(\alpha,\beta)
since any two systems of arcs
\{\gamma_{r}\}_{r}
and disks
\{\delta_{e}\}_{e}
are isotopic in
M
via an isotopy preserving the basepoint of
M
.
Also, the fact that two
\chi
-labelings
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
of
P
are pointed gauge equivalent if and only if their associated maps
f_{\alpha,\beta}
and
f_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
are pointed homotopic is shown similarly to Claim
6.3
. Thus the assignment
(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto f_{\alpha,\beta}
induces a well-defined injective map
\{\chi\text{-labelings of }P\}/\mathcal{G}_{P,\chi}^{*}\to[M,B\chi]_{*}
which maps the pointed gauge equivalence class of a
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
to the pointed homotopy class of the map
f_{\alpha,\beta}
. Since regions of
P
are disks, this map is surjective by the same arguments of the end of Section
6.5
.
∎

Let
g^{\prime}\in[M,B\chi^{\prime}]_{*}
. By Claim
7.6
, the pointed homotopy class
g^{\prime}
is encoded by a
\chi^{\prime}
-labeling
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
of
P
. Note that it follows from the definition of a crossed module morphism that if
(\alpha,\beta)
is a
\chi
-labeling of
P
, then
(\varphi\alpha,\psi\beta)
is a
\chi^{\prime}
-labeling of
P
. Consider the set

\mathcal{L}=\bigl\{(\alpha,\beta)\text{ $\chi$-labeling of $P$}\,\big|\,(\varphi\alpha,\psi\beta)=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\bigr\}.

For a
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
, denote by
g_{\alpha,\beta}\in[M,B\chi]_{*}
the pointed homotopy class determined by the pointed gauge equivalence class of
(\alpha,\beta)
as in Claim
7.6
.

Claim 7.7
.

For any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
, we have
B\phi\circ g_{\alpha,\beta}=g^{\prime}
. Conversely, for any
g\in[M,B\chi]_{*}
such that
B\phi\circ g=g^{\prime}
, there is
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
such that
g=g_{\alpha,\beta}
.

Proof.

Notice first that if
(\alpha,\beta)
is a
\chi
-labeling of
P
, then
B\phi\circ g_{\alpha,\beta}=g_{\varphi\alpha,\psi\beta}
. In particular, for any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
, we have:

B\phi\circ g_{\alpha,\beta}=g_{\varphi\alpha,\psi\beta}=g_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}=g^{\prime}
.
Conversely, let
g\in[M,B\chi]_{*}
such that
B\phi\circ g=g^{\prime}
. By Claim
7.6
, there is a
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
of
P
such that
g=g_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
. Since

g_{\varphi\alpha_{0},\psi\beta_{0}}=B\phi\circ g_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}=B\phi\circ g=g^{\prime}=g_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}},

Claim
7.6
implies that
(\varphi\alpha_{0},\psi\beta_{0})
is pointed gauge equivalent to
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
, and so there is a map
\mu^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E^{\prime}
such that

(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(1,\mu^{\prime})\cdot(\varphi\alpha_{0},\psi\beta_{0})
. Since
\psi
is surjective, there is a map

\mu\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E
such that
\mu^{\prime}=\psi\mu
.
Consider the
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,\mu)\cdot(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
of
P
. By Claim
7.6
,
g_{\alpha,\beta}=g_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}=g
. Also, for any
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
,

\varphi\alpha(r)\overset{(i)}{=}\varphi\bigl(\alpha_{0}(r)\chi(\mu(r))\bigr)\overset{(ii)}{=}\varphi\alpha_{0}(r)\,\varphi\chi(\mu(r))\overset{(iii)}{=}\varphi\alpha_{0}(r)\,\chi^{\prime}(\mu^{\prime}(r))\,\overset{(iv)}{=}\alpha^{\prime}(r).

Here
(i)
and
(iv)
follow from (
23
),
(ii)
from the multiplicativity of
\varphi
, and
(iii)
from the facts that
\varphi\chi=\chi^{\prime}\psi
and
\psi\mu=\mu^{\prime}
.
Moreover, for any
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
,

\displaystyle\psi\beta(e,B)\overset{(i)}{=}\psi\big(\beta_{0}(e,B)\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(\gamma_{(e,B)})\big)\overset{(ii)}{=}\psi\beta_{0}(e,B)\,\psi(\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(\gamma_{(e,B)}))

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{=}\psi\beta_{0}(e,B)\,\mu^{\prime}_{\varphi\alpha_{0}}(\gamma_{(e,B)})\overset{(iv)}{=}\beta^{\prime}(e,B).

Here
(i)
and
(iv)
follow from (
24
),
(ii)
from the multiplicativity of
\psi
, and
(iii)
from the facts that
\psi(\mu_{\alpha_{0}})=(\psi\mu)_{\varphi\alpha_{0}}
(see Section
6.3
) and
\psi\mu=\mu^{\prime}
. Thus we get that
(\varphi\alpha,\psi\beta)=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
. Hence
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
.
∎

For
\mathcal{A}\subset\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})_{\mathrm{hom}}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hom}}
, let

\mathrm{Col}_{\mathcal{A}}=\{c\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to\mathcal{A}\,|\,\varphi(|c(r)|)=\alpha^{\prime}(r)\text{ for all }r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)\}.

For
c\in\mathrm{Col}_{\mathcal{A}}
, we define the
\Bbbk
-module
H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=H_{c}
and the scalars
\dim(c)
and
|c|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=V_{c}(\ast_{c})
as in Section
7.1
but using the
\chi^{\prime}
-labeling
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
and the spherical
\chi^{\prime}
-fusion category
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
. Clearly,
|c|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=0
whenever
H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=0
. Set

\mathrm{Col}^{*}_{\mathcal{A}}=\{c\in\mathrm{Col}_{\mathcal{A}}\,|\,H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}\neq 0\}.

To maps
\eta\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
and
d\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to J
, we associate the map

\eta\cdot d\colon\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}\mathrm{Reg}(P)&\to&I\\
r&\mapsto&\eta(r)\cdot d(r).\end{array}\right.

Denote by
\mathcal{M}
the set of maps from
\mathrm{Reg}(P)
to
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
.

Claim 7.8
.

The map
\mathcal{M}\times\mathrm{Col}_{J}\to\mathrm{Col}_{I}
, defined by
(\eta,d)\mapsto\eta\cdot d
, is bijective.

Proof.

Note that
\eta\cdot d\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}
for any
(\eta,d)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathrm{Col}_{J}
since, by using (
32
),

\varphi(|(\eta\cdot d)(r)|)=\varphi(|\eta(r)\cdot d(r)|)=\varphi(|d(r)|)=\alpha^{\prime}(r)

for all
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
. Hence the map is well-defined. Let
c\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}
. Claim
7.4
(i) implies that there is a unique pair of maps
(\eta\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to\mathrm{Ker}(\psi),d\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to J)
such that
c(r)=\eta(r)\cdot d(r)
for all
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
. Note that
d\in\mathrm{Col}_{J}
since, by using (
32
),

\varphi(|d(r)|)=\varphi(|\eta(r)^{-1}\cdot c(r)|)=\varphi(|c(r)|)=\alpha^{\prime}(r)

for all
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
. Thus there is a unique
(\eta,d)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathrm{Col}_{J}
such that
c=\eta\cdot d
. Hence the map is bijective.
∎

Claim 7.9
.

For all
\eta\in\mathcal{M}
and
d\in\mathrm{Col}_{J}
,

\dim(\eta\cdot d)=\dim(d)\quad\text{and}\quad|\eta\cdot d|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=|d|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}.

Proof.

For any
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
, the objects
\eta(r)\cdot d(r)
and
d(r)
are isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}
and so have same dimension. Then

\dim(\eta\cdot d)=\dim(d)
.

For each
j\in J
and
e\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
, pick an
e
-isomorphism
\theta_{e,j}\colon j\to e\cdot j
in
\mathcal{C}
. Note that different choices of such an isomorphism differ by multiplication of an invertible scalar since
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(j,e\cdot j)
is free of rank 1 (see Lemma
3.1
(ii)). For any
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
, define the isomorphism
\theta_{r,\pm}\colon d(r)^{\pm}\to(\eta(r)\cdot d(r))^{\pm}
by

\theta_{r,+}=\theta_{\eta(r),d(r)}\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{r,-}=\bigl(\theta^{*}_{\eta(r),d(r)}\bigr)^{-1}.

Note that
\theta_{r,+}
has degree
\psi(\eta(r))=1
in
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
, as well as
\theta_{r,-}=(\theta^{*}_{r,+})^{-1}
.
For any oriented edge
e
of
P
, the above isomorphisms induce a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism

\theta_{e}\colon H_{d}(e)\xrightarrow{\simeq}H_{\eta\cdot d}(e)

such that for any
P
-ball branch
B
at
e
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{H_{d}(e)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{e}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{\beta^{\prime}(e,B)}\bigl(\mathbb{1},d(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes d(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{e,B}}
\textstyle{H_{\eta\cdot d}(e)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{\beta^{\prime}(e,B)}\left(\mathbb{1},(\eta(b_{1})\cdot d(b_{1}))^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\eta(b_{n})\cdot d(b_{n}))^{\varepsilon_{n}}\right).}

Here, the horizontal maps are the cone isomorphisms,
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{n}
are the branches of
P
at
e
enumerated in the linear order determined by
B
,
\varepsilon_{i}=\varepsilon_{e}(b_{i})\in\{+,-\}
, and
\theta_{e,B}
is defined by

\theta_{e,B}(\alpha)=(\theta_{b_{1},\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\theta_{b_{n},\varepsilon_{n}})\circ\alpha.

For any vertex
x
of
P
, the unordered tensor product
\otimes_{e_{x}}\,\theta_{e_{x}}
, where
e_{x}
runs over all edges of
P
incident to
x
and oriented away from
x
, induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
\theta_{x}\colon H(\Gamma^{d}_{x})\to H(\Gamma^{\eta\cdot d}_{x})
satisfying

(33)

\mathbb{F}_{\!\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}\bigl(\Gamma^{d}_{x}\bigr)=\mathbb{F}_{\!\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}\bigl(\Gamma^{\eta\cdot d}_{x}\bigr)\circ\theta_{x}.

This follows from the naturality of
\mathbb{F}_{\!\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}
(see Section
5.6
). Also, as in Section
7.1
, each unoriented edge
e
of
P
gives rise to two opposite oriented edges
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
of
P
and to the vectors
\ast^{d}_{e}\in H_{d}(e_{1})\otimes H_{d}(e_{2})
and
\ast^{\eta\cdot d}_{e}\in H_{\eta\cdot d}(e_{1})\otimes H_{\eta\cdot d}(e_{2})
satisfying

(34)

(\theta_{e_{1}}\otimes\theta_{e_{2}})(\ast^{d}_{e})=\ast_{e}^{\eta\cdot d}.

Then the equality
|\eta\cdot d|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=|d|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}
follows directly from (
33
) and (
34
).
∎

For a
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
, let

\mathrm{Col}(\alpha,\beta)=\bigl\{c\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to I\,\big|\,|c(r)|=\alpha(r)\text{ for all }r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)\bigr\}.

For
c\in\mathrm{Col}(\alpha,\beta)
, we define the
\Bbbk
-module
H_{c,\mathcal{C}}=H_{c}
and the scalar
|c|_{\mathcal{C}}=V_{c}(\ast_{c})
as in Section
7.1
by using the
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
and the spherical
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
.
Clearly,
|c|_{\mathcal{C}}=0
whenever
H_{c,\mathcal{C}}=0
. Set

\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha,\beta)=\{c\in\mathrm{Col}(\alpha,\beta)\,|\,H_{c,\mathcal{C}}\neq 0\}.

The next claim is instrumental to define a map
\mathrm{Col}^{*}_{I}\to\mathcal{L}
in Claim
7.11
.

Claim 7.10
.

Let
X
be a
1
-direct sum in
\mathcal{C}
of homogeneous objects of degree
h\in H
. Let
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{e^{\prime}}(\mathbb{1},X)\neq 0
. Then there is a unique
e\in\psi^{-1}(e^{\prime})
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{e^{\prime}}(\mathbb{1},X)=\mathrm{Hom}^{e}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{1},X).

Moreover
\chi(e)=h
.

Proof.

Assume that
X
is a 1-direct sum of a finite family
(X_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of homogeneous objects of
\mathcal{C}
of degree
h
. Since

\bigoplus_{e\in\psi^{-1}(e^{\prime})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{e^{\prime}}(\mathbb{1},X)\neq 0,

there is
e\in\psi^{-1}(e^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},X)\neq 0
. Then
\chi(e)=h
. Indeed, since by (
5
)

\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},X_{\alpha})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},X)\neq 0,

there is
\alpha\in A
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{e}(\mathbb{1},X_{\alpha})\neq 0
, and so
\chi(e)=|X_{\alpha}|=h
. If
f\in\psi^{-1}(e^{\prime})
is such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(\mathbb{1},X)\neq 0
, then
\chi(f)=h
(as above) and so
f=e
because
fe^{-1}\in\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)=1
. Thus
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(\mathbb{1},X)=0
for all
f\in\psi^{-1}(e^{\prime})
with
f\neq e
. Hence
\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{e^{\prime}}(\mathbb{1},X)=\bigoplus_{f\in\psi^{-1}(e^{\prime})}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}(\mathbb{1},X)=\mathrm{Hom}^{e}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbb{1},X)
.
∎

Claim 7.11
.

Let
c\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}^{*}
. Then there is a unique
(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})\in\mathcal{L}
with
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
. Moreover
|c|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=|c|_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

The condition
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
implies that
\alpha_{c}(r)=|c(r)|
for all
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
. Let
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
. The cone isomorphism gives a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism

H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}(e)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{\beta^{\prime}(e,B)}\bigl(\mathbb{1},c(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}\bigr),

where
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{n}
are the branches of
P
at
e
enumerated in the linear order determined by
B
and
\varepsilon_{i}=\varepsilon_{e}(b_{i})\in\{+,-\}
.
Since
H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}(e)\neq 0

and the object
c(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}
is a
1
-direct sum in
\mathcal{C}
of homogeneous objects of degree
|c(b_{1})|^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots|c(b_{n})|^{\varepsilon_{n}}
by (c) of Section
4.1
,
Claim
7.10
gives that there is a unique

\beta_{c}(e,B)\in\psi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime}(e,B))
such that

(35)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{\beta^{\prime}(e,B)}\bigl(\mathbb{1},c(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}\bigr)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta_{c}(e,B)}\bigl(\mathbb{1},c(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}\bigr),

and moreover

\chi(\beta_{c}(e,B))=|c(b_{1})|^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots|c(b_{n})|^{\varepsilon_{n}}=\alpha_{c}(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{c}(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}.

Let
\mathrm{succ}(B)
be as in Section
6.3
. Since the cone isomorphisms are compatible with the map
p_{b_{1},b_{2}}
as in (
18
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{\beta^{\prime}(e,\mathrm{succ}(B))}\bigl(\mathbb{1},c(b_{2})^{\varepsilon_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}\otimes c(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{f}\bigl(\mathbb{1},c(b_{2})^{\varepsilon_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}}\otimes c(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\bigr)

with
f={\vphantom{{\!\beta_{c}(e,B)}}}^{\left(|c(b_{1})|^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\right)}{\!\beta_{c}(e,B)}
by (e) of Section
4.1
. The uniqueness in Claim
7.10
implies that

\beta_{c}(e,\mathrm{succ}(B))=f={\vphantom{{\!\beta_{c}(e,B)}}}^{\left(\alpha_{c}(b_{1})^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\right)}{\!\beta_{c}(e,B)}.

Therefore
(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
is a
\chi
-labeling of
P
. By construction,
(\varphi\alpha_{c},\psi\beta_{c})=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
, so that
(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})\in\mathcal{L}
, and
c\in\mathrm{Col}(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
. It follows from (
35
) that there is a canonical
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}(e)\simeq H_{c,\mathcal{C}}(e)
for each oriented edge
e
of
P
, and so
H_{c,\mathcal{C}}\simeq H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}\neq 0
. Hence
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
.
The uniqueness of
(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
follows from the fact that
\alpha_{c}
is fully determined by the condition
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
and from the uniqueness in Claim
7.10
.

The fact that
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}
as pivotal categories implies that, using
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
and
c\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}^{*}
on the one hand and using
\mathcal{C}
and
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})
on the other, the contraction vectors associated with the unoriented edges of
P
are the same and that the vectors associated with the vertices of
P
are the same (up to the canonical isomorphism
H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}\simeq H_{c,\mathcal{C}}
). Consequently,
|c|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=|c|_{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

Claim 7.12
.

The map

\mathrm{Col}^{*}_{I}\to\hskip-3.00003pt\coprod_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}}\hskip-6.00006pt\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha,\beta),\quad c\mapsto c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})

is bijective.

Proof.

The uniqueness in Claim
7.11
implies that if
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
and
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha,\beta)
, then
(\alpha_{c},\beta_{c})=(\alpha,\beta)
. In particular, this gives that
\bigcup_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}}\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha,\beta)
is a disjoint union. Then the map is well-defined and injective. Let us prove that it is surjective. Let
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
and
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha,\beta)
. We need to prove that
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}_{I}
.
Since
\alpha^{\prime}(r)=\varphi(\alpha(r))=\varphi(|c(r)|)
for all
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
, we get that
c\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}
. Let
e
be an oriented edge of
P
. Pick a
P
-ball branch
B
at
e
and set

X=c(b_{1})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes c(b_{n})^{\varepsilon_{n}},

where
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{n}
are the branches of
P
at
e
enumerated in the linear order determined by
B
and
\varepsilon_{i}=\varepsilon_{e}(b_{i})\in\{+,-\}
. Using that
\beta(e,B)\in\psi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime}(e,B))
and
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha,\beta)
, we obtain

H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}(e)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}^{\beta^{\prime}(e,B)}(\mathbb{1},X)\supset\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta(e,B)}(\mathbb{1},X)\simeq H_{c,\mathcal{C}}(e)\neq 0.

Thus
H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=\bigotimes_{e}H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}(e)\neq 0
and so
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}_{I}
.
∎

By definition, since
|M\setminus P|=1
,

|M,g^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=\dim\bigr(\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}_{1}\bigl)^{-1}\sum_{d\in\mathrm{Col}_{J}}\hskip-3.00003pt\dim(d)\,|d|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}.

Now, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}\\
c\in\mathrm{Col}(\alpha,\beta)\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.99994pt\dim(c)\,|c|_{\mathcal{C}}\,\overset{(i)}{=}\hskip-10.00002pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}\\
c\in\mathrm{Col}^{*}(\alpha,\beta)\end{subarray}}\hskip-11.00008pt\dim(c)\,|c|_{\mathcal{C}}\,\overset{(ii)}{=}\sum_{c\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}^{*}}\hskip-3.00003pt\dim(c)\,|c|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{=}\sum_{c\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}}\hskip-3.00003pt\dim(c)\,|c|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}\overset{(iv)}{=}\hskip-18.00005pt\sum_{(\eta,d)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathrm{Col}_{J}}\hskip-18.00005pt\dim(\eta\cdot d)\,|\eta\cdot d|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}

\displaystyle\overset{(v)}{=}\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})\hskip-3.00003pt\sum_{d\in\mathrm{Col}_{J}}\hskip-3.00003pt\dim(d)\,|d|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}.

Here
(i)
follows from the fact that
|c|_{\mathcal{C}}=0
for all
c\in\mathrm{Col}(\alpha,\beta)
with
H_{c,\mathcal{C}}=0
,

(ii)
from the bijection of Claim
7.12
and the last assertion of Claim
7.11
,
(iii)
from the fact that
|c|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=0
for all
c\in\mathrm{Col}_{I}
with
H_{c,\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=0
,
(iv)
from the bijection in Claim
7.8
, and
(v)
from
Claim
7.9
. Therefore

|M,g^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=\dim\bigr(\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}_{1}\bigl)^{-1}\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})^{-1}\hskip-8.99994pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}\\
c\in\mathrm{Col}(\alpha,\beta)\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.99994pt\dim(c)\,|c|_{\mathcal{C}}

and so, using Claim
7.5
, we obtain

|M,g^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}}d_{\phi}^{-1}\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})^{-1}\dim(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})^{-1}\hskip-8.99994pt\sum_{c\in\mathrm{Col}(\alpha,\beta)}\hskip-8.99994pt\dim(c)\,|c|_{\mathcal{C}}.

Then, it follows from the definition of
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
that

|M,g^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}}d_{\phi}^{-1}\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})^{-1}|M,g_{\alpha,\beta}|_{\mathcal{C}}.

Now the first assertion of Claim
7.7
gives that for any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
, the pointed homotopy class
g_{\alpha,\beta}\in[M,B\chi]_{*}
satisfies
B\phi\circ g_{\alpha,\beta}=g^{\prime}
. Thus

(36)

|M,g^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=d_{\phi}^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in[M,B\chi]_{*}\\
B\phi\circ g=g^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.0pt\lambda_{g}\,|M,g|_{\mathcal{C}}\quad\text{where}\quad\lambda_{g}=\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}\\
g_{\alpha,\beta}=g\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.0pt\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})^{-1}.

Let
g\in[M,B\chi]_{*}
such that
B\phi\circ g=g^{\prime}
. By the second assertion of Claim
7.7
, there is
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})\in\mathcal{L}
such that
g=g_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
. Set

\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})}=\bigl\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}\,\big|\,\text{$(\alpha,\beta)$ is pointed gauge equivalent to $(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})$}\bigr\}.

For any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})}
, Claim
7.6
gives that
g_{\alpha,\beta}=g
if and only if
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})}
. Thus

(37)

\lambda_{g}=\dfrac{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})})}{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})}.

Consider the sets

\mathcal{E}=\left\{\mu\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E\;\left|\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathrm{Im}}(\mu)\subset\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi\chi),\\
(\psi\mu)_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\gamma_{(e,B)})=1_{E^{\prime}}\text{ for all $(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)$}\end{array}\!\!\right\}\right.

and

\mathcal{S}=\left\{\mu\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E\;\left|\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathrm{Im}}(\mu)\subset\mathrm{Ker}(\chi),\\
\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(\gamma_{(e,B)})=1_{E}\text{ for all $(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)$}\end{array}\!\!\right\}.\right.

Claim 7.13
.

Let
\mu\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E
be a map and let
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
. Then
\mu\in\mathcal{E}
if and only if
(1,\mu)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

Consider the
\chi
-labeling
(\delta,\varepsilon)=(1,\mu)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
. As in the proof of Claim
7.7
, we get that for any
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
and
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
,

\varphi\delta(r)=\alpha^{\prime}(r)\varphi\chi(\mu(r))\quad\text{and}\quad\psi\varepsilon(e,B)=\beta^{\prime}(e,B)\,(\psi\mu)_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\gamma_{(e,B)}).

Then
(\delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{L}\Leftrightarrow(\varphi\delta,\psi\varepsilon)=(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow\mu\in\mathcal{E}
.
∎

Claim 7.14
.

The set
\mathcal{E}
is a group (with opposite pointwise product) left acting transitively on
\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})}
and
\mathcal{S}
is the stabilizer of
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
under this action.

Proof.

Let
\eta,\mu\in\mathcal{E}
. By Claim
7.13
,
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,\mu)\cdot(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})\in\mathcal{L}
and
(1,\eta)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}
. Using the definition of the action of
\mathcal{G}_{P,\chi}^{*}\subset\mathcal{G}_{P,\chi}
on
\chi
-labelings, we obtain:

(1,\mu\eta)\cdot(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})=\bigl((1,\eta)(1,\mu)\bigr)\cdot(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})=(1,\eta)\cdot(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}.

Thus
\mu\eta\in\mathcal{E}
by Claim
7.13
. We deduce from this that
\mathcal{E}
is a group.

Next, let
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})}
. Since
(\alpha,\beta)
is pointed gauge equivalent to
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
, there is a map
\mu\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E
such that
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,\mu)\cdot(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
. Since
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
and
(\alpha,\beta)
are both in
\mathcal{L}
, Claim
7.13
implies that
\mu\in\mathcal{E}
. Thus
\mathcal{E}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})}
.
Finally, let
\mu\in\mathcal{E}
. Set
(\alpha,\beta)=(1,\mu)\cdot(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
. For any
r\in\mathrm{Reg}(P)
and
(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)
,

\alpha(r)=\alpha_{0}(r)\chi(\mu(r))\quad\text{and}\quad\beta(e,B)=\beta_{0}(e,B)\,\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(\gamma_{(e,B)}).

Then
(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})\Leftrightarrow\mu\in\mathcal{S}
. Hence
\mathcal{S}
is the stabilizer of
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
.
∎

Note that Claim
7.14
implies in particular that
\mathcal{S}
is a group (for the opposite pointwise product). Similarly, the set

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\left\{\mu^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E^{\prime}\;\left|\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathrm{Im}}(\mu^{\prime})\subset\mathrm{Ker}(\chi^{\prime}),\\
\mu^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\gamma_{(e,B)})=1_{E^{\prime}}\text{ for all $(e,B)\in\mathrm{EB}(P)$}\end{array}\!\!\right\}\right.

is a group (with opposite pointwise product).

Claim 7.15
.

The map
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,
\mu\mapsto\psi\mu
, is a surjective group homomorphism with kernel
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

The map is well defined since for any
\mu\in\mathcal{E}
,

\chi^{\prime}({\mathrm{Im}}(\psi\mu))=\chi^{\prime}\psi({\mathrm{Im}}(\mu))=\varphi\chi({\mathrm{Im}}(\mu))=\{1_{H^{\prime}}\}.

The fact that it is a group homomorphism follows from the multiplicativity of
\psi
. Its surjectivity follows from the surjectivity of
\psi
. Using that a map
\mu\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E
verifies
\psi\mu=1
if and only if
{\mathrm{Im}}(\mu)\subset\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
, we obtain that the Kernel of the map is
\mathcal{M}
(identified with the set of maps
\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to E
with image in
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)
).
∎

Claim 7.16
.

The group
\mathcal{S}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi),g)
. Similarly, the group
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi^{\prime}),g^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Using the notation of the proof of Claim
7.6
, consider the pointed map
f=f_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\colon M\to B\chi
whose pointed homotopy class is
g_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}=g
. As in the proof of Claim
6.3
, any
\mu\in\mathcal{S}
defines a pointed homotopy
G_{\mu}
from
f
to itself and so a loop in
\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi)
based at
f
whose homotopy class
\delta_{\mu}
depends only on
\mu
. The assignment
\mu\mapsto\delta_{\mu}
is a group homomorphism (by definition of the product of
\mathcal{G}_{P,\chi}
). The injectivity of this assignment follows from the facts that
B\chi
is a 2-type and the filtration of
M
used in the construction of
G_{\mu}
has a unique 0
-
cell. The surjectivity of the map follows from the fact that any pointed homotopy from
f
to itself is homotopic to
G_{\mu}
for some
\mu\in\mathcal{S}
(as in the proof of Claim
6.3
). Therefore
\mathcal{S}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi),f)
. Note that the
isomorphism type of
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi),f)
only depend on the path component of
f
in
\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi)
, which is
g
, and is denoted
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi),g)
.

The proof of the second statement of the claim is similar (by exchanging
\chi
with
\chi^{\prime}
and
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
with
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
).
∎

We can now compute:

\displaystyle\lambda_{g}\;\overset{(i)}{=}\dfrac{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{L}_{(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})})}{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})}\overset{(ii)}{=}\dfrac{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{E})}{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{S})\,\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{M})}\overset{(iii)}{=}\dfrac{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})}{\mathrm{card}(\mathcal{S})}

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{=}\dfrac{\mathrm{card}\bigl(\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi^{\prime}),g^{\prime})\bigr)}{\mathrm{card}\bigl(\pi_{1}(\mathrm{TOP}_{*}(M,B\chi),g)\bigr)}.

Here
(i)
follows from (
37
),
(ii)
from Claim
7.14
,
(iii)
from Claim
7.15
, and
(iv)
from Claim
7.16
. This together with (
36
) concludes the proof of Theorem
7.2
.

8.
3-dimensional HQFTs with target
B\chi

In this section, given a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
, we define
3
-dimensional HQFTs with target the classifying space
B\chi
. We adapt here the definition given in
[
Tu
,
TVi1
]
by replacing pointed homotopies (which are used for aspherical targets) by
\sigma
-homotopies where
\sigma
is a graph.

Recall from Section
2.4
that the space
B\chi
has a canonical filtration

(B\chi)_{*}=(\{x\}\subset BH\subset B\chi)

where
x
is the unique 0-cell of
B\chi
(serving as a basepoint) and
BH
is a subcomplex of
B\chi
which is a classifying space of the group
H
and whose associated boundary crossed module
\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)\to\pi_{1}(BH,x)
is
\chi\colon E\to H
.

8.1.
\sigma
-homotopies

Any graph
\sigma
embedded in a topological space
X
yields a filtration
X_{\sigma}=(\sigma_{0}\subset\sigma\subset X)
of
X
, where
\sigma_{0}
is the set of vertices of
\sigma
.
Recall that a filtered map
u\colon X_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{\ast}
is a continuous map
u\colon X\to B\chi
such that

u(\sigma_{0})=\{x\}\quad\text{and}\quad u(\sigma)\subset BH.

By a
\sigma
-homotopy
between filtered maps
u,v\colon X_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{\ast}
, we mean a homotopy from
u
to
v
which is filtered at each time, i.e., a continuous map
H\colon X\times[0,1]\to B\chi
such that
H(\cdot,0)=u
,
H(\cdot,1)=v
, and for all
t\in[0,1]
,

H(\sigma_{0},t)=\{x\}\quad\text{and}\quad H(\sigma,t)\subset BH.

We denote by
[X,B\chi]_{\sigma}
the set of
\sigma
-homotopy classes of filtered maps
X_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{\ast}
. If
A
is a subspace of
X
containing
\sigma
, then the restriction to
A
induces a canonical map
[X,B\chi]_{\sigma}\to[A,B\chi]_{\sigma}
.

Note that if
\sigma=\emptyset
, then
\sigma
-homotopy classes of filtered maps
X_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{\ast}
correspond to (standard) homotopy classes of maps
X\to B\chi
. Also, if
\sigma
has no edges (that is,
\sigma=\sigma_{0}
is a set of points of
X
), then
\sigma
-homotopy classes of filtered maps
X_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{\ast}
correspond to pointed homotopy classes of pointed maps
(X,\sigma_{0})\to(B\chi,x)
.

8.2.
Skeletons of surfaces and their duals

Let
\Sigma
be a closed oriented surface. A
skeleton
of
\Sigma
is an oriented graph
A\subset\Sigma
such that all components of
\Sigma\setminus A
are open disks and all vertices of
A
have valence
\geq 2
.

An oriented graph
\sigma
in
\Sigma
is
dual
to a skeleton
A
of
\Sigma
if

(a)

the vertices of
\sigma
lie in
\Sigma\setminus A
and each connected component of
\Sigma\setminus A
contains exactly one vertex of
\sigma
;

(b)

each edge
s
of
\sigma
intersects
A
transversely at a single point in the interior of a (unique) edge
s^{*}
of
A
and the map
s\mapsto s^{*}
induces a bijection between the edges of
\sigma
and those of
A
;

(c)

for each edge
s
of
\sigma
, the orientation of
s^{*}
followed by that of
s
yields the orientation of
\Sigma
.

Note that the skeleton
A
of
\Sigma
gives a cellular decomposition of
\Sigma
(with 0-cells the vertices of
A
, 1-cells the edges of
A
, and 2-cells corresponding to the connected components of
\Sigma\setminus A
) and that
\sigma
is then the 1-skeleton of a cellular decomposition dual to the one given by
A
. In particular the number of connected components of
\Sigma\setminus A
is equal to the number of vertices of
\sigma
.

For example, the 1-skeleton of a triangulation of
\Sigma
is a graph in
\Sigma
dual to a skeleton of
\Sigma
.
Two dual graphs of a skeleton of
\Sigma
are isotopic, and two skeletons of
\Sigma
having isotopic duals are isotopic.

8.3.
Preliminaries on
\chi
-surfaces and
\chi
-manifolds

By a
\chi
-surface
, we mean a triple
(\Sigma,\sigma,f)
consisting of a closed oriented surface
\Sigma
, an oriented graph
\sigma
in
\Sigma
which is dual to some skeleton of
\Sigma
, and
f\in[\Sigma,B\chi]_{\sigma}
, see Section
8.1
.
(The graph
\sigma
will play the role of basepoints in
[
Tu
,
TVi1
]
.) The
opposite
of a
\chi
-surface
(\Sigma,\sigma,f)
is the
\chi
-surface
(-\Sigma,\sigma,f)
where
-\Sigma
is
\Sigma
with the opposite orientation.

A
\chi
-manifold
is a triple
(M,\sigma,g)
where
M
is a compact oriented
3
-dimensional manifold,
\sigma
is an oriented graph in the boundary
\partial M
of
M
which is dual to a skeleton of
\partial M
, and
g\in[M,B\chi]_{\sigma}
.
The
boundary
of such a
\chi
-manifold is the
\chi
-surface
\partial M=(\partial M,\sigma,\partial g)
, where
\partial g\in[\partial M,B\chi]_{\sigma}
is the restriction of
g
to
\partial M
(see Section
8.1
).
Here we use the ‘outward vector first’ convention for the induced orientation of the boundary: at any point of
\partial M
, the given orientation of
M
is determined by the tuple (a tangent vector directed outward, a basis in the tangent space of
\partial M
positive with respect to the induced orientation). A
\chi
-manifold
(M,\sigma,g)
is
closed
if
\partial M=\emptyset
. In this case
\sigma=\emptyset
,
g\in[M,B\chi]_{\emptyset}=[M,B\chi]
, and we denote
(M,\sigma,g)=(M,\emptyset,g)
by
(M,g)
as in Section
6.7
.

Any
\chi
-surface
(\Sigma,\sigma,f)
determines the
cylinder
\chi
-manifold

(\Sigma\times[0,1],\bar{\sigma}=(\sigma\times\{0\})\sqcup(\sigma\times\{1\}),\bar{f})

where
\Sigma\times[0,1]
is endowed with the product orientation and
\bar{f}
is the image of
f
under the map

[\Sigma,B\chi]_{\sigma}\to[\Sigma\times[0,1],B\chi]_{\bar{\sigma}}
induced by pre-composing with the projection
\Sigma\times[0,1]\to\Sigma
.

Disjoint union of
\chi
-surfaces (
\chi
-manifolds) are
\chi
-surfaces (
\chi
-manifolds) in the obvious way. A
\chi
-homeomorphism

(\Sigma,\sigma,f)\to(\Sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},f^{\prime})
between
\chi
-surfaces is an orientation preserving diffeomorphism
\phi\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\prime}
such that
\phi(\sigma)=\sigma^{\prime}
(as oriented graphs) and
f=f^{\prime}\circ\phi
(in
[\Sigma,B\chi]_{\sigma}
). A
\chi
-homeomorphism

(M,\sigma,g)\to(M^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},g^{\prime})
between
\chi
-manifolds is an orientation preserving diffeomorphism
\psi\colon M\to M^{\prime}
such that
\psi(\sigma)=\sigma^{\prime}
(as oriented graphs) and
g=g^{\prime}\circ\psi
(in
[M,B\chi]_{\sigma}
). In particular, observe that any
\chi
-homeomorphism
(M,\sigma,g)\to(M^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},g^{\prime})
between
\chi
-manifolds restricts to a
\chi
-homeomorphism
(\partial M,\sigma,\partial g)\to(\partial M^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\partial g^{\prime})
between the boundary
\chi
-surfaces.

For brevity, we shall sometimes omit the dual graphs
\sigma
and the maps to
B\chi
from the notation for
\chi
-surfaces and
\chi
-manifolds.

8.4.
The category of
\chi
-cobordisms

A
\chi
-cobordism
from a
\chi
-surface
\Sigma_{0}
to a
\chi
-surface
\Sigma_{1}
is a pair
(M,h)
where
M
is a
\chi
-manifold and
h\colon(-\Sigma_{0})\sqcup\Sigma_{1}\to\partial M
is a
\chi
-homeomorphism. Here,
-\Sigma_{0}
is the opposite of
\Sigma_{0}
(see section
8.3
). Two
\chi
-cobordisms
(M,h)
and
(M^{\prime},h^{\prime})
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
are
equivalent
if there is a
\chi
-
homeomorphism
G\colon M\to M^{\prime}
such that
h^{\prime}=G\circ h
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
the category of
3
-dimensional
\chi
-cobordisms. Objects of
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
are
\chi
-surfaces. A morphism
\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}
in
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
is an equivalence class of
\chi
-cobordisms
from
\Sigma_{0}
to
\Sigma_{1}
.
The identity morphism of an
\chi
-surface
\Sigma
is represented by the cylinder
\chi
-manifold
\Sigma\times[0,1]
with
tautological identification of the boundary with
(-\Sigma)\sqcup\Sigma
.
Composition of morphisms in
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
is the gluing of
\chi
-cobordisms. More precisely, the
composition of two morphisms represented by the
\chi
-cobordisms
(M_{0}=(M_{0},\sigma_{0},g_{0}),h_{0})\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}

and
(M_{1}=(M_{1},\sigma_{1},g_{1}),h_{1})\colon\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
is represented by
the
\chi
-cobordism
(M=(M,\sigma,g),h)
defined as follows. The 3-dimensional manifold
M
is obtained
by gluing
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
along
h_{1}h_{0}^{-1}\colon h_{0}(\Sigma_{1})\to h_{1}(\Sigma_{1})
. The graph
\sigma
in
\partial M=h_{0}(\Sigma_{0})\cup h_{1}(\Sigma_{2})
is

\sigma=\bigl(\sigma_{0}\cap h_{0}(\Sigma_{0})\bigr)\cup\bigl(\sigma_{1}\cap h_{1}(\Sigma_{2})\bigr).

Pick representatives
(M_{0})_{\sigma_{0}}\to(B\chi)_{*}
of
g_{0}
and
(M_{1})_{\sigma_{1}}\to(B\chi)_{*}
of
g_{1}
which agree on
h_{0}(\Sigma_{1})\simeq h_{1}(\Sigma_{1})
. They define thus a filtered map
M_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{*}
. The fact that
B\chi
is a 2-type ensures that the
\sigma
-homotopy class
g\in[M,B\chi]_{\sigma}
of this filtered map is well defined.
Finally,

h=h_{0}|_{\Sigma_{0}}\sqcup h_{1}|_{\Sigma_{2}}\colon(-\Sigma_{0})\sqcup\Sigma_{2}\simeq\partial M.

The disjoint union operation on
\chi
-surfaces and
\chi
-manifolds turns the category
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
into a symmetric monoidal category whose unit object is the empty
\chi
-surface.

8.5.
Homotopy quantum field theories

Let
\mathrm{Mod}_{\Bbbk}
be the category of
\Bbbk
-modules and

\Bbbk
-linear homomorphisms. It is a symmetric monoidal category with the standard tensor product and the unit object
\Bbbk
.

A 3-dimensional
homotopy quantum field theory (HQFT) with target
B\chi
is a symmetric strong monoidal functor
Z\colon\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\Bbbk}
. In particular,
Z(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime})\simeq Z(\Sigma)\otimes Z(\Sigma^{\prime})
for any
\chi
-surfaces
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}
, and similarly for morphisms. Also,
Z(\emptyset)\simeq\Bbbk
. We refer to
[
ML
]
for a detailed definition of a strong monoidal functor.

8.6.
Representations of mapping class groups of
\chi
-surfaces

The category
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
of
\chi
-cobordisms includes as
a subcategory the category
\mathrm{Homeo}^{\chi}
of
\chi
-surfaces and
their
\chi
-homeomorphisms considered up to isotopy (in the class of
\chi
-homeomorphisms).
Indeed, a
\chi
-homeomorphism of
\chi
-surfaces
\phi\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\prime}

determines a morphism
\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}

represented by
(C,h\colon(-\Sigma)\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}\to\partial C)
, where
C
is the cylinder
\chi
-
manifold associated with
\Sigma^{\prime}
and

h(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\phi(x),0)&\text{if $x\in\Sigma$,}\\
(x,0)&\text{if $x\in\Sigma^{\prime}$.}\end{array}\right.

Isotopic
\chi
-homeomorphisms give rise to the same morphism in
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}
. The category
\mathrm{Homeo}^{\chi}
inherits a structure of a symmetric monoidal category from that of
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
. Restricting a 3-dimensional HQFT
Z\colon\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\Bbbk}
to
\mathrm{Homeo}^{\chi}
, we obtain a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{Homeo}^{\chi}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\Bbbk}
.
In particular,
Z
induces a
\Bbbk
-linear representation of the
mapping class group of a
\chi
-surface
\Sigma
defined as the group of isotopy classes of
\chi
-homeomorphisms
\Sigma\to\Sigma
.

8.7.
Remark

Consider two
\chi
-surfaces
(\Sigma,\sigma_{0},f_{0})
and
(\Sigma,\sigma_{1},f_{1})
having the same underlying surface
\Sigma
. The oriented graph
\bar{\sigma}=(\sigma_{0}\times\{0\})\sqcup(\sigma_{1}\times\{1\})
in
\partial(\Sigma\times[0,1])
is dual to a skeleton of
\partial(\Sigma\times[0,1])
. Then any
g\in[\Sigma\times[0,1],B\chi]_{\bar{\sigma}}
restricting to
f_{i}
on
\Sigma\times\{i\}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
determines a
\chi
-manifold
(\Sigma\times[0,1],\bar{\sigma},g)

which, endowed with tautological identification of its boundary with
(-\Sigma)\sqcup\Sigma
, represents a
\chi
-
cobordism
C_{g}\colon(\Sigma,\sigma_{0},f_{0})\to(\Sigma,\sigma_{1},f_{1})
. This
\chi
-cobordism is an isomorphism in the category
\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}
. Consequently, the image of
C_{g}
under a 3-dimensional HQFT
Z\colon\mathrm{Cob}^{\chi}_{3}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\Bbbk}
induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
Z(\Sigma,\sigma_{0},f_{0})\simeq Z(\Sigma,\sigma_{1},f_{1})
.

8.8.
The case of aspherical targets

Let
X
be an aspherical pointed connected CW-complex. Recall that
X
is homotopy equivalent to
BG
with
G=\pi_{1}(X)
. The trivial group homomorphism
1\to G
is a crossed module such that

B(1\to G)=BG\quad\text{and}\quad(B(1\to G))_{*}=(\{x\}\subset BG\subset BG).

Let
\Sigma
be a closed oriented surface. Assume that
\Sigma
is pointed (meaning that every connected component of
\Sigma
is endowed with a basepoint). Pick an oriented graph
\sigma
in
\Sigma
which is dual to a skeleton of
\Sigma
and whose set of vertices is the set of basepoints of
\Sigma
. Note that

[\Sigma,B(1\to G)]_{\sigma}=[\Sigma,BG]_{*},

where the right-hand side denotes the set of pointed homotopy classes of pointed maps
\Sigma\to BG
.
Then any homotopy equivalence
X\approx BG
induces a bijection

[\Sigma,B(1\to G)]_{\sigma}\simeq[\Sigma,X]_{*}.

This implies that the category
\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{G}
defined in
[
TVi1
]
(whose objects are pointed closed oriented surfaces with a pointed homotopy class of pointed maps to
X
) is equivalent to the category
\mathrm{Cob}^{(1\to G)}_{3}
defined in Section
8.5
. Consequently, HQFTs with target
X
in the sense of
[
TVi1
]
correspond to HQFTs with target
B(1\to G)
in the sense of Section
8.5
.

9.
The state-sum HQFT with target
B\chi

We fix throughout this section a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
, a spherical
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
(over
\Bbbk
) such that
\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})\in\Bbbk
is invertible, and a
\chi
-representative set
I=\amalg_{h\in H}\,I_{h}
for
\mathcal{C}
. We extend the numerical state-sum invariant
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
of closed
\chi
-manifolds (defined in Section
7
) to a
3
-dimensional HQFT with target
B\chi
(in the sense of Section
8
).

9.1.
Colored
\chi
-surfaces

Let
\Sigma=(\Sigma,\sigma,f)
be a
\chi
-surface (see Section
8.3
). The
H
-label
of an edge
s
of
\sigma
is the element of
H=\pi_{1}(BH,x)
represented by the loop
u(s)
in
BH
, where
u\colon\Sigma_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{*}
is any representative of
f\in[\Sigma,B\chi]_{\sigma}
.

Any skeleton
A
of
\Sigma
dual to
\sigma
(see Section
8.2
) becomes a
\chi
-graph in
\Sigma
as follows. The
H
-label of an edge
a
of
A
is the
H
-label of the edge of
\sigma
dual to
a
. Let
\ell
be a half-edge of
A
. The vertex of
A
adjacent to
\ell
belongs to a connected component of
\Sigma\setminus\sigma
which is the interior of a 2-cell
D
attached to
\sigma
along a map
\rho\colon\partial D\to\sigma
. We orient
D
so that the induced orientation of its interior is opposite to that of
\Sigma
, and we orient
\partial D
using the ‘outward vector first’ convention. Let
S=\rho^{-1}(\sigma_{0})\subset\partial D
, where
\sigma_{0}
is the set of vertices of
\sigma
.
Denote by
\iota\colon D\to\Sigma
the canonical map obtained by attaching
D
on
\sigma
along
\rho
. By traversing (from its adjacent vertex) the inverse image of
\ell
under
\iota
, one reaches a point in
\partial D\setminus S
whose predecessor in
S\subset\partial D
(with respect to the orientation of
\partial D
) is denoted
p_{\ell}
. (For an example see Figure
3
where
x,y\in H
are
H
-labels of edges of
\sigma
and blue dots are elements of
S
.)

.

Figure 3.

The
E
-label of
\ell
is then the element of
E=\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)
represented by the map
u\circ\iota\colon(D,\partial D,p_{\ell})\to(B\chi,BH,x)
, where
u\colon\Sigma_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{*}
is any representative of
f\in[\Sigma,B\chi]_{\sigma}
. With these labels,
A
becomes a
\chi
-graph in
\Sigma
. This follows from the fact that the
boundary crossed module
\partial\colon\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)\to\pi_{1}(BH,x)
is
\chi\colon E\to H
. In particular, (
16
) follows from the definition of the boundary map
\partial
and (
17
) from the definition of the action of
\pi_{1}(BH,x)
on
\pi_{2}(B\chi,BH;x)
. For instance, in the above example, the
E
-label
e
of
\ell
does satisfy the expected relation
\chi(e)=xyx^{-1}
since both sides of this relation are equal to
\partial([f\circ i])
.

A
\mathcal{C}
-coloring
of a
\chi
-surface
\Sigma=(\Sigma,\sigma,f)
is a map
d\colon\{\text{edges of $\sigma$}\}\to\mathrm{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
such that for each edge
s
of
\sigma
, the object
d(s)
is homogeneous of degree the
H
-label of
s
.
A
\mathcal{C}
-coloring
d
of
\Sigma
turns the
\chi
-graph in
\Sigma
associated with a skeleton
A
of
\Sigma
dual to
\sigma
(as above) into a
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph denoted
A_{d}
by assigning to each edge
a
of
A
the object
d(a^{*})
, where
a^{*}
is the edge of
\sigma
dual to
a
. Section
5.5
yields then the
\Bbbk
-module
H(A,d)
. If
A
and
B
are two skeletons of
\Sigma
dual to
\sigma
, then the canonical bijection between their half-edges (coming from the fact they are both dual to
\sigma
) induces a
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
\rho_{A,B}\colon H(A_{d})\to H(B_{d})
. The family
(\{H(A_{d})\}_{A},\{\rho_{A,B}\}_{A,B})
, where
A,B
run over all skeletons of
\Sigma
dual to
\sigma
, is a projective system of
\Bbbk
-modules and
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphisms. Consider the projective limit of this system:

H(\Sigma,d)=\underleftarrow{\lim}\,H(A_{d})\,.

The module
H(\Sigma,d)
depends only on
(\Sigma,d)=(\Sigma,\sigma,f,d)
and comes equipped with a family
\{H(\Sigma,d)\simeq H(A_{d})\}_{A}
of
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphisms, called the
cone isomorphisms
, which commute with the
\rho_{A,B}
.

Let
-\Sigma=(-\Sigma,\sigma,f)
be the opposite of
\Sigma
(see Section
8.3
). Any
\mathcal{C}
-coloring
d
of
\Sigma
is a
\mathcal{C}
-coloring of
-\Sigma
in the obvious way. For any skeleton
A
of
\Sigma
dual to
\sigma
, the oriented graph
A^{\mathrm{op}}
(which is
A
with the orientation of all edges reversed) is a skeleton of
-\Sigma
, the
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
A^{\mathrm{op}}_{d}=(A^{\mathrm{op}})_{d}\subset-\Sigma
is opposite to
A_{d}\subset\Sigma
, and the canonical isomorphism
H(A_{d}^{\mathrm{op}})\simeq H(A_{d})^{\star}
(see Section
5.5
) yields a canonical isomorphism

H(-\Sigma,d)\simeq H(\Sigma,d)^{\star}.

9.2.
Skeletons of 3-manifolds

By a
skeleton
of an oriented compact 3-dimensional manifold
M
, we mean an oriented stratified 2-polyhedron
P\subset M
such that
P\cap\partial M=\partial P
and

(i)

the oriented graph
\partial P
is a skeleton of
\partial M
;

(ii)

for every vertex
v
of
\partial P
, there is a unique edge

e_{v}
of
P
such that
v
is an endpoint of
e_{v}
and
e_{v}\nsubseteq\partial M
; the edge
e_{v}
is not a loop and

e_{v}\cap\partial M=\{v\}
;

(iii)

for every edge
a
of
\partial P
, the only region
r_{a}
of
P
adjacent to
a
is a closed 2-disk
meeting
\partial M
precisely along
a
;

(iv)

all connected components of
M\setminus P
are
open or half-open 3-balls.

Conditions (i)–(iii) imply that the
intersection of
P
with a tubular neighborhood of
\partial M
in

M
is homeomorphic to
\partial P\times[0,1]
. Also, an edge of
P
with both endpoints in
\partial M
is an edge of
\partial P
.

Components of
M\setminus P
are called
P
-balls
.
Note that the boundary disks of the half-open

P
-balls are precisely the components of
\partial M\setminus\partial P
. A
P
-ball branch
at a vertex or an edge of
P
is a germ (at the vertex or edge) of an adjacent
P
-ball.

An edge or vertex of
P
is
interior
if it is not included in
\partial P
.
The
link graph
of an interior vertex of
P
is defined as in Section
6.2
.

9.3.
\chi
-labelings

We extend the notion of a
\chi
-labeling of a skeleton of an oriented closed 3-dimensional manifold given in Section
6.3
to the case of oriented compact 3-dimensional manifolds.

Let
P
be a skeleton of an oriented compact 3-dimensional manifold
M
.
As in Section
6.3
, for each oriented interior edge
e
of
P
, the set
P_{e}
of branches of
P
at
e
inherits a cyclic order from the orientations of
e
and
M
, and any
P
-ball branch
B
at
e
turns the cyclic order on
P_{e}
into a linear order (so that the first element is the first branch
b_{B}
of
P
at
e
encountered while traversing a small loop in
M
positively encircling
e
starting from
B
). We define the map
\varepsilon_{e}\colon P_{e}\to\{+,-\}
as in Section
6.3
. Note that when the orientation of
e
is reversed, the cyclic order on
P_{e}
is reversed and
\varepsilon_{e}
is multiplied by
-
.

Recall that
\mathrm{Reg}(P)
denotes the (finite) set of regions of
P
(see Section
6.1
).
Let
\mathrm{EB}(P)
be the set of pairs
(e,B)
where
e
is an interior oriented edge of
P
and
B
is a
P
-ball branch at
e
.
A
pre-
\chi
-labeling
of
P
is a pair

\bigl(\alpha\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to H,\beta\colon\mathrm{EB}(P)\to E\bigr)

of maps verifying the conditions (
20
), (
21
), (
22
) of Section
6.3
.

A pre-
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
turns the set
P_{e}
of branches of
P
at an oriented interior edge
e
of
P
into a
\chi
-cyclic set
P_{e}=(P_{e},\alpha_{e},\beta_{e},\varepsilon_{e})
as follows: for any branch
b\in P_{e}
, set
\alpha_{e}(b)=\alpha(b)\in H
and
\beta_{e}(b)=\beta(e,B)\in E
, where
B
is the
P
-ball branch at
e
such that
b=b_{B}
(in the above notation).

Also, a pre-
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
turns the oriented graph
\partial P
into a
\chi
-graph in
\partial M
as follows. The
H
-label of an edge
a
of
\partial P
is
\alpha(r_{a})\in H
, where
r_{a}
is the region of
P
adjacent to
a
.
Let
\ell
be a half-edge of
\partial P
. It is adjacent to a vertex
v
of
\partial P
. The vertex
v
is adjacent to a (unique) interior edge
e
of
P
. Orient
e
so that it originates at
v
. Let
B
be the
P
-ball branch at
e
so that
\ell
is the
the first half-edge of
\partial P
encountered while traversing a small loop negatively encircling
v
(with respect to the orientation of
\partial M
) from any point of
B\cap\partial M
. Then the
E
-label of
\ell
is
\beta(e,B)\in E
.

As in Section
6.3
, a pre-
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
of
P
turns the link graph
\Gamma_{v}
of an interior vertex
v
of
P
into a
\chi
-graph in
\partial B_{v}\cong S^{2}
, where
B_{v}
is a
P
-cone neighborhood of
v
(see Section
6.2
). A
\chi
-labeling
of
P
is a pre-
\chi
-labeling of
P
such that, for any interior vertex
v
of
P
, the
\chi
-graph
\Gamma_{v}
in
B_{v}\cong S^{2}
is 1-spherical (see Section
5.8
).

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
M
be an oriented compact 3-dimensional manifold such that its boundary is endowed with a structure of a
\chi
-surface
(\partial M,\sigma,f)
. Let
P
be a skeleton of
M
such that
\partial P
is dual to
\sigma
(see Section
8.2
). Let
(\alpha,\beta)
be a
\chi
-
labeling of
P
compatible with
f
in the sense that the two
\chi
-graph structures on
\partial P
induced by
(\alpha,\beta)
(as above) and by
f
(as in Section
9.1
) coincide. Then this data determines a class
g_{\alpha,\beta,f}\in[M,B\chi]_{\sigma}
such that
\partial g_{\alpha,\beta,f}=f
.

Proof.

Pick a center in every
P
-ball so that the centers of half-open
P
-balls are vertices of
\sigma
.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of all regions of
P
contained in
\mathrm{Int}(M)
.
For each
r\in\mathcal{R}
, pick an oriented arc
\gamma_{r}
as in Section
6.3
and let
S_{r}
be as in Section
6.5
.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be the set of all (unoriented) interior edges of
P
with both endpoints in
\mathrm{Int}(M)
.
For each
e\in\mathcal{E}
, pick a
P
-ball branch
B_{e}
at
e
, an orientation for
e
, and a disk
\delta_{e}
embedded in
M
as in Section
6.5
.
Set
S=(\cup_{r\in\mathcal{R}}S_{r})\cup\sigma
.
Then the pair
(M,S)
has a relative CW-decomposition with only 2-cells and 3-cells. The 2-cells are the disks
\{\delta_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
together with the 2-cells of the cellular decomposition of
\partial M
induced by
\sigma
(i.e., the cellular decomposition dual to that induced by the skeleton
\partial P
of
\partial M
, see Section
8.2
). The 3-cells are ball neighborhoods in
M
of the interior vertices of
P
. Define the map
\cup_{r\in\mathcal{R}}S_{r}\to B\chi
as in the proof of Claim
6.2
and extend it to
S\cup\partial M
using a representative of
f\in[\partial M,B\chi]_{\sigma}
. As in the proof of Claim
6.2
, we further extend this map to the relative 2-skeleton
S\cup\partial M\cup(\cup_{e\in\cup\mathcal{E}}\delta_{e})
by using Condition (
20
), and then to all 3-cells by using the
1
-sphericality of the
\chi
-graphs associated with the interior vertices of
P
and the compatibility of
(\alpha,\beta)
and
f
on
\partial P
.
The conditions (
21
) and (
22
) ensure that the obtained filtered map
M_{\sigma}\to(B\chi)_{*}
does not depend on the choices of the adjacent
P
-ball branches and orientations for the edges
e\in\mathcal{E}
. Its
\sigma
-homotopy class
g_{\alpha,\beta,\partial g}
satisfies
\partial g_{\alpha,\beta,f}=f
and depends only on
P
,
(\alpha,\beta)
, and
f
since any two systems of arcs
\{\gamma_{r}\}_{r\in\mathcal{R}}
, disks
\{\delta_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}}
, and suspensions
\{S_{r}\}_{r\in\mathcal{R}}
as above are isotopic in
M
.
∎

9.4.
\chi
-skeletons of
\chi
-manifolds

A
\chi
-skeleton
of a
\chi
-manifold
(M,\sigma,g)
is a skeleton
P
of
M
together with a
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
such that
\partial P
is dual to
\sigma
, the
\chi
-labeling
(\alpha,\beta)
is compatible with
\partial g
(as in Lemma
9.1
), and
g
is equal to the class
g_{\alpha,\beta,\partial g}\in[M,B\chi]_{\sigma}
defined in Lemma
9.1
.

The primary
\chi
-moves
T_{0}^{\pm 1}-T_{3}^{\pm 1}
defined in Section
6.8
for
\chi
-skeletons of closed
\chi
-manifolds extend to
\chi
-skeletons of
\chi
-manifolds in the obvious way. All these moves proceed inside 3-balls in the interior
\mathrm{Int}(M)
of
M
and do not modify the boundary of the skeletons. In particular, all vertices/edges/regions created or destroyed by the moves lie in
\mathrm{Int}(M)
.
The action of the moves on the
\chi
-labelings is determined by the
requirement that the labels of the big regions are preserved under
the moves. Under
T_{0}^{\pm 1},T_{1}^{\pm 1},T_{2}^{\pm 1},T_{3}^{\pm 1}
, the labels of the regions and edges lying in
\mathrm{Int}(M)
are transformed as in Section
6.8
and the labels of the regions and edges meeting
\partial M
remain unchanged.
These moves as well as label-preserving ambient isotopies of
\chi
-skeletons of
(M,\sigma,g)
are called
primary
\chi
-moves
. The following lemma extends Lemma
6.4
.

Lemma 9.2
.

Any primary
\chi
-move transforms a
\chi
-skeleton of
\chi
-manifold
(M,\sigma,g)
into a
\chi
-skeleton of
(M,\sigma,g)
.
Moreover, any two
\chi
-skeletons of
(M,\sigma,g)
can be related by a finite sequence of primary
\chi
-moves.

Proof.

The proof is parallel to the proof of Lemma
6.4
with the following changes. Given a skeleton
P
of an oriented compact 3-dimensional manifold
M
, denote by
\mathrm{Ball}(P)
the set of
P
-balls and by
\mathrm{Reg}(P)
the set of regions of
P
.
The gauge group
\mathcal{G}_{P}
associated with
P
is the set of pairs
(\lambda,\mu)\in\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{Ball}(P),H)\times\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{Reg}(P),E)
such that
\lambda(B)=1_{H}
whenever
B
is adjacent to
\partial M
(i.e.,
B
is a half-open 3-ball) and
\mu(r)=1_{E}
whenever
r
is adjacent to
\partial M
(i.e.,
r\cap\partial M\neq\emptyset
). The product in
\mathcal{G}_{P}
and the action of
\mathcal{G}_{P}
on
\chi
-labelings of
P
are defined as in Section
6.3
. Also, we use that any two skeletons of
M
whose boundaries are isotopic in
\partial M
can be related by a finite sequence of primary moves (see
[
TVi4
, Theorem 11.5]
).
∎

9.5.
An invariant of
\chi
-manifolds

By an
I
-coloring
of a
\chi
-surface
\Sigma
, we mean a
\mathcal{C}
-coloring of
\Sigma
(see Section
9.1
) taking values in
I
.

Let
(M,\sigma,g)
be a
\chi
-manifold and
d
be an
I
-coloring of the
\chi
-surface
\partial M=(\partial M,\sigma,\partial g)
.
We define a vector

|M,\sigma,g,d|_{\mathcal{C}}\in H(\partial M,d)

where
H(\partial M,d)
is the
\Bbbk
-module introduced in Section
9.1
.

Pick a
\chi
-skeleton
P=(P,(\alpha,\beta))
of
(M,\sigma,g)
, see Section
9.4
.
An
I
-coloring
of
P
is a map
c\colon\mathrm{Reg}(P)\to I
such that
c(r)\in I_{\alpha(r)}
for all region
r
of
P
. Such an
I
-coloring
c
is said to
extend
d
if
c(r_{s})=d(s)
for all edges
s
of
\sigma
, where
r_{s}
is the region of
P
adjacent to the edge of
\partial P
dual to
s
.

Given an
I
-coloring
c
of
P
extending
d
, we define
\dim(c)\in\Bbbk
by (
26
). We also associate with
c
a vector
|c|\in H(\partial M,d)
as follows. Let
\mathcal{E}
be the set of all oriented interior edges of
P
. For each
e\in\mathcal{E}
, the coloring
c
of
P
turns the
\chi
-cyclic set
P_{e}
of branches of
P
at
e
(see Section
9.3
) into a
\chi
-cyclic
\mathcal{C}
-set by assigning to each branch
b\in P_{e}
the
c
-color of the region of
P
containing
b
. Let
H_{c}(e)=H(P_{e})
be its multiplicity module (see Section
5.1
). Each oriented edge
e
of
P
originates at a vertex of
P
called the
tail
of
e
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}\subset\mathcal{E}
be the set of all oriented edges of
P
with tail in the interior of
M
and
\mathcal{E}_{\partial}=\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{0}
.
Set

H_{c}=\bigotimes_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}\,H_{c}(e)\quad\text{and}\quad H^{\partial}_{c}=\bigotimes_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\partial}}\,H_{c}(e),

where
\otimes
is the unordered tensor product of
\Bbbk
-modules.
Observe that the tail
v(e)
of any
e\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\partial}
is a vertex of
\partial P
and there is a canonical
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism
H_{c}(e)\simeq H_{v(e)}((\partial P)_{d})
. Here
(\partial P)_{d}
is the
\chi
-graph in
\partial M
induced by the
\chi
-labeling of
P
(see Section
9.3
) and is
\mathcal{C}
-colored using
d
(see Section
9.1
). The formula
e\mapsto v(e)
establishes a bijective correspondence between the edges
e\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\partial}
and the vertices
v
of
\partial P
. This together with the cone isomorphism (see Section
9.1
) gives a canonical
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism

H^{\partial}_{c}\simeq\bigotimes_{v}\,H_{v}((\partial P)_{d})=H((\partial P)_{d})\simeq H(\partial M,d).

As in Section
7.1
, an unoriented interior edge
e
of
P
gives rise to two opposite oriented edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
and a vector
\ast_{e}\in H_{c}(e_{1})\otimes H_{c}(e_{2})
independent of the numeration of
e_{1},e_{2}
. The unordered tensor product of these vectors over all unoriented interior edges is a vector

\otimes_{e}\,\ast_{e}\in\bigotimes_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\,H_{c}(e).

We let
\ast_{c}\in H_{c}\otimes H(\partial M,d)
be the image of
\otimes_{e}\,\ast_{e}
under the isomorphism

\bigotimes_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\,H_{c}(e)\simeq H_{c}\otimes H_{c}^{\partial}\simeq H_{c}\otimes H(\partial M,d).

As in Section
7.1
, an interior vertex
v
of
P
determines a
1
-spherical
\mathcal{C}
-colored
\chi
-graph
\Gamma_{v}^{c}
and a vector

\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Gamma_{v}^{c})\in H(\Gamma_{v}^{c})^{\star}\simeq\otimes_{e_{v}}\,H_{c}(e_{v})^{\star},

where
e_{v}
runs over all edges of
P
incident to
v
and oriented away from
v
. Let
V_{c}\in H_{c}^{\star}
be the image of the unordered tensor product
\otimes_{v}\,\mathbb{F}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Gamma^{c}_{v})
over all such
v
under the canonical
\Bbbk
-linear isomorphism

\bigotimes_{v}H(\Gamma_{v}^{c})^{\star}\simeq\bigotimes_{v}\bigotimes_{e_{v}}H_{c}(e_{v})^{\star}\simeq H_{c}^{\star}.

Finally set

|c|=(V_{c}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H(\partial M,d)})(\ast_{c})\in\Bbbk\otimes H(\partial M,d)\simeq H(\partial M,d).

We now define a vector in
H(\partial M,d)
by

|M,\sigma,g,d|_{\mathcal{C}}=\dim(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})^{-|M\setminus P|}\sum_{c}\,\,\dim(c)\,|c|,

where
|M\setminus P|
is the number of connected components of
M\setminus P
and
c
runs over all
I
-colorings of
P
extending
d
.

Theorem 9.3
.

The vector
|M,\sigma,g,d|_{\mathcal{C}}\in H(\partial M,d)
is a topological invariant of
(M,\sigma,g,d)
independent of the choice of
P
.

Proof.

The topological invariance here means that if
\psi\colon(M,\sigma,g)\to(M^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},g^{\prime})
is a

\chi
-homeomorphism between
\chi
-manifolds, then

|M^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},g^{\prime},d^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{C}}=H(\psi)(|M,\sigma,g,d|_{\mathcal{C}})
, where
d^{\prime}
is the composition of
d
with the bijection
\{\text{edges of $\sigma^{\prime}$}\}\to\{\text{edges of $\sigma$}\}
induced by
\psi
and
H(\psi)\colon H(\partial M,d)\to H(\partial M^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is the isomorphism induced by
\psi
.
This invariance follows from the independence of
P
, and the latter is verified exactly as in the proof of Theorem
7.1
using Lemma
9.2
.
∎

The invariant
|M,\sigma,g,d|_{\mathcal{C}}
is multiplicative: for any
\chi
-manifolds
(M_{1},\sigma_{1},g_{1})
,
(M_{2},\sigma_{2},g_{2})

and any
I
-colorings
d_{1}
of
\partial M_{1}
and
d_{2}
of
\partial M_{2}
, we have:

|(M_{1},\sigma_{1},g_{1})\sqcup(M_{2},\sigma_{2},g_{2}),d_{1}\sqcup d_{2}|_{\mathcal{C}}=|M_{1},\sigma_{1},g_{1},d_{1}|_{\mathcal{C}}\otimes|M_{2},\sigma_{2},g_{2},d_{2}|_{\mathcal{C}}

up to the canonical isomorphism

H(\partial(M_{1}\sqcup M_{2}),d_{1}\sqcup d_{2})\simeq H(\partial M_{1},d_{1})\otimes H(\partial M_{2},d_{2}).

9.6.
Functoriality

Let
\Sigma_{0}=(\Sigma_{0},\sigma_{0},f_{0})
and
\Sigma_{1}=(\Sigma_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1})
be
\chi
-surfaces and let
\xi\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\chi}
represented by a
\chi
-cobordism
(M,h)
where
M=(M,\sigma,g)
is a
\chi
-manifold
M
and
h\colon(-\Sigma_{0})\sqcup\Sigma_{1}\to\partial M
is a
\chi
-homeomorphism (see Section
8.4
).
Any
I
-colorings
d_{0}
of
\Sigma_{0}
and
d_{1}
of
\Sigma_{1}
yields an
I
-coloring
d_{0}\cup d_{1}
of
(-\Sigma_{0})\sqcup\Sigma_{1}
in the obvious way and so an
I
-coloring
d
of the
\chi
-surface
\partial M=h((-\Sigma_{0})\sqcup\Sigma_{1})
. Theorem
9.3
gives a vector
|M,\sigma,g,d|_{\mathcal{C}}\in H(\partial M,d)
. Now let
\Upsilon
be the composition of the isomorphisms

\displaystyle H(\partial M,d)\simeq H((-\Sigma_{0})\sqcup\Sigma_{1},d_{0}\cup d_{1})\simeq H(-\Sigma_{0},d_{0})\otimes H(\Sigma_{1},d_{1})

\displaystyle\simeq H(\Sigma_{0},d_{0})^{\star}\otimes H(\Sigma_{1},d_{1})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\Bbbk}\bigl(H(\Sigma_{0},d_{0}),H(\Sigma_{1},d_{1})\bigr),

where the first isomorphism is induced by
h^{-1}
, the second and fourth are obvious, and the third is induced by the canonical isomorphism
H(-\Sigma_{0},d_{0})\simeq H(\Sigma_{0},d_{0})^{\star}
discussed in Section
9.1
.
Set

(38)

|\xi,d_{0},d_{1}|=\frac{\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})^{v(\Sigma_{1})}}{\dim(d_{1})}\,\,\Upsilon(|M,\sigma,g,d|_{\mathcal{C}})\colon H(\Sigma_{0},d_{0})\to H(\Sigma_{1},d_{1}),

where
v(\Sigma_{1})
is the number of vertices of
\sigma_{1}
and
\dim(d_{1})

is the product over all edges of
\sigma_{1}
of the dimensions of their colors. Theorem
9.3
implies that the homomorphism
|f,d_{0},d_{1}|
does not depend on the choice of the pair
(M,h)
representing
f
.
The normalization factor in the definition of
|f,d_{0},d_{1}|
is justified by the next lemma.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\xi_{0}\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma
and
\xi_{1}\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma_{1}
be morphisms in
\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\chi}
.
For any
I
-colorings
d_{0}
of
\Sigma_{0}
and
d_{1}
of
\Sigma_{1}
,

|\xi_{1}\xi_{0},d_{0},d_{1}|=\sum_{d}\,|\xi_{1},d,d_{0}|\circ|\xi_{0},d_{0},d|\colon H(\Sigma_{0},d_{0})\to H(\Sigma_{1},d_{1}),

where
d
runs over all
I
-colorings of
\Sigma
.

Proof.

Represent
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
, respectively, by pairs
(M_{0},h_{0})
and
(M_{1},h_{1})
as above. The morphism
\xi_{1}\xi_{0}\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}
is represented by the pair
(M,{h})
, where
M
is obtained by gluing
M_{0}
to
M_{1}
along
h_{1}h_{0}^{-1}\colon h_{0}(\Sigma)\to h_{1}({\Sigma})
and
h=h_{0}|_{\Sigma_{0}}\sqcup h_{1}|_{\Sigma_{2}}
.
To simplify notation, we identify
{h}_{0}(\Sigma)={h}_{1}({\Sigma})\subset\mathrm{Int}(M)

with
\Sigma
via
h_{0}
. Pick
\chi
-skeletons
P_{0}
of
M_{0}
and
P_{1}
of
M_{1}
such that
\partial P_{0}\cap\Sigma=\partial P_{1}\cap\Sigma
. Then
P=P_{0}\cup P_{1}\subset M
is a
\chi
-skeleton of
M
. Since
v(\Sigma)
is equal to the number of connected components of
\Sigma\setminus A
where
A=\partial P_{0}\cap\Sigma
(see Section
8.2
), we have:

|M\setminus P|=|M_{0}\setminus P_{0}|+|M_{1}\setminus P_{1}|-v(\Sigma).

The term
-v(\Sigma)
explains the need for the factor
\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})^{v(\Sigma)}
in the definition of
|\xi_{1},d,d_{0}|
. Similarly, given a region
r_{0}
of
P_{0}
and a region
r_{1}
of
P_{1}
adjacent to the same edge
e
of
A
, the union
r=r_{0}\cup r_{1}\cup e
is a region of
P
with Euler characteristic
\mathrm{Eul}(r)=\mathrm{Eul}(r_{0})+\mathrm{Eul}(r_{1})-1
. The term
-1
explains the need for the factor
\dim(d)^{-1}
in the definition of
|\xi_{1},d,d_{0}|
.
∎

9.7.
The HQFT
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}

For a
\chi
-surface
\Sigma
, denote by
\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma)
the set of all
I
-colorings of
\Sigma

and consider the
\Bbbk
-module

|\Sigma|^{\circ}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma)}\,H(\Sigma,d).

For a morphism
\xi\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}
in
\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\chi}
, consider the
\Bbbk
-linear homomorphism

|\xi|^{\circ}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{0}\in\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma_{0})\\
d_{1}\in\mathrm{Col}(\Sigma_{1})\end{subarray}}|\xi,d_{0},d_{1}|\colon|\Sigma_{0}|^{\circ}\to|\Sigma_{1}|^{\circ}.

Lemma
9.4
implies that for any
\xi_{0},\xi_{1}
as in this lemma,

(39)

|\xi_{1}\xi_{0}|^{\circ}=|\xi_{1}|^{\circ}\circ|\xi_{0}|^{\circ}.

In particular, this implies that

p_{\Sigma}=|\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma}|^{\circ}\colon|\Sigma|^{\circ}\to|\Sigma|^{\circ}

is a projector onto a direct summand
|\Sigma|=\mathrm{Im}(p_{\Sigma})
of
|\Sigma|^{\circ}
.
We next associate with each morphism
\xi\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}
in
\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\chi}

a homomorphism
|\xi|\colon|\Sigma_{0}|\to|\Sigma_{1}|
. Formulas (
39
) and
\mathrm{id}_{\Sigma_{1}}\circ\xi=\xi
imply that
|\xi|^{\circ}=p_{\Sigma_{1}}\,|\xi|^{\circ}
,
and so the image of
|\xi|^{\circ}
is contained in
|\Sigma_{1}|
. Denote by
|\xi|\colon|\Sigma_{0}|\to|\Sigma_{1}|
the restriction of
|\xi|^{\circ}\colon|\Sigma_{0}|^{\circ}\to|\Sigma_{1}|^{\circ}
to
|\Sigma_{0}|
and
|\Sigma_{1}|
.
It is clear that the rule
\Sigma\mapsto|\Sigma|
,
\xi\mapsto|\xi|
defines a functor
|\cdot|\colon\mathrm{Cob}_{3}^{\chi}\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\Bbbk}
. We endow this functor with monoidal constraints as follows. By definition,
|\emptyset|=|\emptyset|^{\circ}=\Bbbk
for the empty
\chi
-surface
\emptyset
.
Given two
\chi
-surfaces
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
, the canonical isomorphisms
H(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime},d\sqcup d^{\prime})\simeq H(\Sigma,d)\otimes H(\Sigma^{\prime},d^{\prime})
, where
d
runs over
I
-colorings of
\Sigma
and
d^{\prime}
runs over
I
-colorings of
\Sigma^{\prime}
, yield an isomorphism
|\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}|^{\circ}\simeq|\Sigma|^{\circ}\otimes|\Sigma^{\prime}|^{\circ}
. The latter factorizes into an isomorphism
|\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}|\simeq|\Sigma|\otimes|\Sigma^{\prime}|
which, together with the canonical isomorphism
|\Sigma|\otimes_{\Bbbk}|\Sigma^{\prime}|\simeq|\Sigma|\otimes|\Sigma^{\prime}|
, defines the monoidal constraint
|\Sigma|\otimes_{\Bbbk}|\Sigma^{\prime}|\simeq|\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}|
. These monoidal constraints turn
|\cdot|
into a symmetric strong monoidal functor. We denote this functor by
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C},I}
or, shorter, by
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
. Combining the considerations above, we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 9.5
.

The functor
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
is a 3-dimensional HQFT with target
B\chi
.

The HQFT
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
is called the
state sum HQFT
derived from
\mathcal{C}
. Considered up to isomorphism, the HQFT
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
does not depend on the choice of the
\chi
-representative
set
I
for
\mathcal{C}
. The scalar invariant of closed
\chi
-manifolds produced by this HQFT is
precisely the invariant of Section
7
.

9.8.
The case of push-forwards

Let
\phi=(\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime},\varphi\colon H\to H^{\prime})
be a morphism from a crossed module
\chi\colon E\to H
to a crossed module
\chi^{\prime}\colon E^{\prime}\to H^{\prime}
such that
\psi
and
\varphi
are surjective,
\mathrm{Ker}(\psi)\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)=1
, and both
\mathrm{Ker}(\varphi)
and
\mathrm{Ker}(\chi^{\prime})
are finite with invertible cardinal in
\Bbbk
. Consider a spherical
\chi
-fusion category
\mathcal{C}
(over
\Bbbk
) such that
\dim(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{1})
is invertible. Then the push-forward
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
is a
\chi^{\prime}
-
fusion category such that
\dim\bigr(\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{1}_{1}\bigl)
is invertible (see Section
7.9
).
By Theorem
9.5
, the category
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})
defines the 3-dimensional HQFT
|\cdot|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}
with target
B\chi^{\prime}
and the category
\mathcal{C}
defines the 3-dimensional HQFT
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{C}}
with target
B\chi
. Recall that Theorem
7.2
computes the values of the former from the latter for connected closed
\chi^{\prime}
-manifolds. There is a similar computation for
\chi^{\prime}
-surfaces. More precisely, let
B\phi\colon(B\chi)_{*}\to(B\chi^{\prime})_{*}
be the filtered map induced by
\phi
. Then for any
\chi^{\prime}
-surface
(\Sigma,\sigma,f^{\prime})
,

|\Sigma,\sigma,f^{\prime}|_{\phi_{*}(\mathcal{C})}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in[\Sigma,B\chi]_{\sigma}\\
B\phi\circ f=f^{\prime}\end{subarray}}|\Sigma,\sigma,f|_{\mathcal{C}}.

The proof of this equality follows that of Theorem
7.2
.
